Ruleborn: game design document v3.1

Ruleborn

Game design document, version 3.1

Collect the laws of nature, build a universe that runs on them, and pull off heists in universes that run on someone else's.

This document defines the whole game from moment zero: its scope, every system, and the logic that connects them. Names and numbers are starting values, written to be tuned.

Draft for review, September 2026. Native iPhone game built in Godot 4.5, rated 13+, for players aged 16 to 34 at its core; iPhone and Android together at global launch.

[SVG]

31How this document is kept true

What changed in version 3.1

An external audit of version 3.0 found 100 items. Version 3.1 closes the eight that were about the document rather than the game, and — more usefully — makes that whole class of failure impossible to repeat: every fact now has one home, and the build fails if a second copy disagrees with it (31).

The document checks itself (31.5). Fifteen deterministic checks run over the built file: canonical constants, retired names, content counts recounted from their own tables, arithmetic re-derived, superseded models banned by phrasing, derived attributes, numbering, citations both ways, cross-references, generated-block digests, and open audit items. A second tool breaks the document ten different ways and asserts every break is caught, because a suite that has never failed is not evidence (31.7).

Six contradictions found and closed. The simulation ran at two different tick rates; the trait and building counts disagreed with the tables that list them; the formulas appendix still encoded a detection model deleted two revisions ago and the page costs of a rarity scheme that no longer exists; the game was listed as its own fallback name; and three laws read attributes no table carried (31.9).

The Library is real. The one page source that needs no raiding had three of its ten levels specified. All ten are now given, with the curve they sit on, and it is in the page economy — which changes what a non-raider's progression actually looks like (10.4, 14.4).

The agent that writes is not the agent that checks (31.6). A verification pass that had not seen this revision being written found five further defects in it, including two inside the section claiming the fixes had landed. Frontier models reward-hack on a third of runs on some benchmarks, and telling them not to makes it worse, not better — the strongest anti-hacking prompt tested raised the rate from 80% to 95%. So every check is deterministic and none of them asks for a judgement.

Reconciliation is a gate, not a milestone (31.8). The blocking half of the register is cleared, the shared numbers move into data files, the checks are turned on and deliberately broken — and only then does the specification lock and the first line of simulation code get written.

Carried over from version 3.0

Validation without players (19.27, 25.4). The paper heist and the twenty-person build test are gone. A simulation harness answers what is structural — decision density, whether skill separates outcomes, hold rates, pacing — the developer is the first player, and everything only a person can answer waits for soft launch. The loss is named rather than hidden: simulation can show the game is not broken, which is a smaller claim than showing it is fun.

Nobody has to build (13.3). The riskiest assumption in the design is removed instead of measured: every player has a generated defence from the first minute, curation is the default creative act, and authoring is the deep end for those who want it.

Six systems specified twice now have one answer — detection, payout, rarity, defence budgets, the roster and production. The superseded halves are deleted, not left standing (30.18).

The inflation dial was keyed to a level that does not exist. It is the target's Vault level, not realm level, which stops at five (9.6).

The Vault decision was two seconds long. Every role now loads, at a fifth of the old rate, and what you carry is what you bank (12.19).

Movement, fights, the Library and matchmaking are specified (12.18, 12.20, 10.4, 12.22), including the rule that the crew never makes a decision the player did not make.

The trade-off the design rests on now binds: a crew on a heist does not work, so raiding genuinely costs production (5.8).

1Vision, pillars and audience

Ruleborn is a strategy game about floating islands that each follow a magic rule. On one island stones float, on another rivers crawl, on a third anyone knocked down comes back as an echo. You collect these rules, build your islands around them, and send a small crew of odd little creatures to rob other players' islands by outsmarting theirs.

1.1 The pitch

| Where it's used
| Line

| Store and trailer
| Your islands. Your rules. Their treasure.

| One sentence for a friend
| "I build floating islands with their own magic rules, and I rob other people's islands by outsmarting theirs."

| For the team
| Pikmin-style crews pulling heists through Monument Valley-style islands that players design and share, with Balatro-style combinations of rules and a Pokémon-style collection of creatures shaped by those rules (see 2).

1.2 The seven pillars

Every feature has to serve at least one of these. When two ideas compete, the one that serves more pillars wins.

| Pillar
| What it means in practice

| Every rule changes the game
| A new law changes how the game plays, not just its numbers, and you can see it in how everything moves. Adding one law creates hundreds of new situations.

| Clever beats strong
| Heists are won by reading an island's rules and finding a way through, not by bigger stats.

| Your islands are yours, and anyone can play them
| Which islands you open, how you shape them, which rules you write into them and which creatures you raise make every player's sky different, and the Island Workshop lets anyone publish an island for everyone to play (18.6).

| Better with friends
| Friends raid together, gift to each other, trade creatures and see each other's best moments. The strongest predictor of staying with a game is having people to play with (19.9).

| Nothing is wasted
| Characters are never spent, failure returns them tired rather than lost, losses are capped, and defending well pays.

| Short sessions, deep planning
| A session is 5 to 9 minutes. The depth lives in planning, which can happen away from the screen.

| Worth your time
| Players come back because they want to, not because they have to (see 19).

1.3 Audience

The core audience is players aged 16 to 34, with 25 to 34 at its heart: people who enjoy clever puzzles, strategy and collecting, and who are tired of pay-to-win and endless timers. Older players are welcome. The game is rated 13+, is not made for or marketed to children, and gives players aged 13 to 17 extra protections (see 20).

1.4 How we talk about it

The fiction is a storybook, not a science lesson. Words players see are plain and warm.

| We say
| We don't say

| Island, rule, law, page, crew, vault, heist, skiff, Keeper
| Physics, code, algorithm, simulation, dimension, hacking

| "This island's rule: stones float"
| "Gravity coefficient reduced by 50%"

| "Your crew got away with 3,200 Starlight"
| "Extraction successful; yield 3,200"

1.5 The ambition

The goal is a game that could become one of the most successful ever made. No honest analysis can promise or estimate that before launch: in 2025 only nine mobile games passed $1 billion in player spending, and strong launches still fail (2.7). So the ambition becomes three commitments:

Design toward what the record holders share (2.6), without copying their weaknesses.

Set gates at the level of the top 1% of games at every stage (25.1). Only a game that clears them has earned a mega-hit forecast.

Change course when the evidence says no, rather than scaling a game that isn't working.

2Lessons from the most successful games

Version 0.1 leaned on Clash of Clans and Clash Royale. This section widens the lens to the biggest earners, the most downloaded, and the most loved games of 2024 to 2026, and turns each into something Ruleborn takes or avoids.

2.1 What the charts say

In 2025 the top-grossing mobile games were live-service titles driven by events and collaborations: MONOPOLY GO!, Royal Match, Whiteout Survival, Last War and Honor of Kings traded the top spots month by month [26].

The most downloaded were the lowest-friction: Block Blast! held the top spot for much of 2025, alongside Roblox, Free Fire and Subway Surfers [26].

The biggest new hit was a collection game: Pokémon TCG Pocket made an estimated $1.3 billion in its first year and passed 150 million downloads, built on two free packs a day, showcases and trading [27].

Premium and indie games proved depth sells on phones too: Balatro passed 5 million copies [28], and Stardew Valley passed 41 million [29].

And a warning: Supercell ended development of Squad Busters in October 2025. It was the studio's first new global game since Brawl Stars, and after several reworks it became the first global game Supercell has ever shut down [30].

2.2 Sixteen games, and what we do with each

| Game
| Why it works
| What Ruleborn takes
| What Ruleborn avoids

| MONOPOLY GO!
| A constant rhythm of short events; sticker albums completed with friends
| Codex albums per school that friends help fill with page gifts; a steady but calm event calendar
| Dice energy, paid randomness, event pressure

| Royal Match
| Tiny levels with one clear goal; a charming meta of decorating
| Every heist states one goal; decorating islands with cosmetics
| Paid continues and boosters

| Whiteout Survival, Last War
| Alliance goals and shared events keep groups together
| Constellation anomalies and shared goals
| Pay-to-win, long timers, a few big spenders deciding everything

| Honor of Kings
| Revenue from skins and collaborations, not power
| Cosmetic-only earnings; skins for islands, skiffs and crews
| Toxic high-stakes competition

| Pokémon TCG Pocket
| A small daily ritual, the joy of collecting, showcasing and trading
| A showcase of your islands and Codex; page trading inside constellations
| Packs bought with money

| Pokémon GO
| Big shared events and co-op raids
| Anomalies as community events
| Location play; paid event tickets

| Block Blast!
| Fun within seconds of opening, no learning curve
| A first heist inside the first minute
| Heavy advertising

| Roblox, Minecraft
| Players make things other players play
| Defence challenges: the best player-built islands become everyone's ruins (18.5)
| Open user content that teens could misuse

| Balatro
| A few simple rules combine into endless builds
| Laws that combine through notes, harmonies and chords (8)
| A gambling look

| Stardew Valley
| A cosy routine and characters with personality
| Ruleborn with names, quirks and home routines; seasons
| Long grinds

| Pikmin
| Small creatures with elemental immunities follow a leader
| The crew model: ruleborn follow a route, and their school decides what they shrug off
| Constant micro-management

| Monument Valley
| Worlds that follow their own impossible rules, as puzzles
| Islands as visual puzzles; wonder first
| Linear, finite levels

| Into the Breach
| Enemies show what they're about to do, so cleverness wins
| Guard wind-ups and visible vision cones
| Turn-based slowness

| Hitman GO, Lara Croft GO
| Readable stealth on a phone
| Clear stealth rules: cones, shade, tall grass
| Rigid boards

| Genshin Impact
| Elements react to each other in memorable ways
| Harmonies and dissonances between laws
| Character gacha

| Squad Busters
| Strong launch, weak long-term pull
| The lesson: prove the core loop with real players before scaling (26.2)
| Relying on hype or crossovers

2.4 What changed because of this

A diorama camera you can rotate, instead of the fixed three-quarter view every base builder uses (21.1).

Combinations are the core of discovery through notes, harmonies and chords (8).

Pikmin-like crews with telegraphed enemies make heists about reading and timing (12.6 to 12.8).

Collection and showing off: Codex albums, island showcases and page trading.

Player-made islands become content through weekly defence challenges.

No base destruction and no wars: heists, co-op and friendly leagues.

| The same, with collaborations after launch

| Mastery that lasts years
| Honor of Kings, Minecraft
| Harmony, chords, leagues
| The same, plus creator mastery in the Workshop

| Spending that feels fair to most players
| Cosmetic-led games
| Cosmetics and a season pass
| The same, plus gifting cosmetics to friends (22.4)

2.7 The honest odds

In 2025 only 9 mobile games passed $1 billion in player spending and 828 passed $10 million, while the top 1% of publishers took 92.5% of in-app revenue [36].

Even a strong launch from the best studio can fail, as Squad Busters did [30], and soft-launch numbers can fade.

Scale in plain arithmetic: earning $2 billion a year means about $5.5 million a day. That is 18 million daily players spending 30 cents each, or 37 million spending 15 cents. Cosmetic-only games are likely nearer the lower figure, an assumption to test. With a third of monthly players playing on a given day, that means 60 to 120 million monthly players.

So the forecast is earned, not assumed: the gates in 25.1 say what would have to be true at each stage.

3Setting and tone

Long ago, an old keeper called the Archivist looked after the Book of Laws, a huge book whose pages told the world how to behave: stones fall, rivers flow, echoes fade. One stormy night the Book burst open. Its pages scattered across the sky, and wherever a page came to rest, a small island lifted into the air and began to follow what was written on it.

Now the sky is full of islands with strange little rules. Keepers are the people who look after a cluster of them. They gather torn pages of the Book, restore them, and write the laws into their own islands, which makes them richer and more wonderful. Other Keepers' islands hold pages you've never seen, and treasure worth borrowing.

| In the story
| In the game

| A page of the Book of Laws
| A law. Collecting enough torn pages of a law restores it.

| A floating island
| A realm. Each follows the laws written into it.

| A Keeper's cluster of islands
| The player's universe: the Heart and up to six outer realms.

| The little creatures born on the islands
| Ruleborn, shaped by the laws of the island where they hatch.

| The Archivist and other wise old keepers
| Sages, the heroes who can bend a rule for a moment.

| The skiff
| The small flying boat a crew uses to land on and leave another Keeper's islands.

Tone: wonder, cleverness and warmth; a heist caper in a picture-book sky. Nothing is killed; knocked-out characters wake up at home.

Look: floating island dioramas under a violet sky; each school of law has its own colour and shape; every law visible in how the world and its creatures move (see 21).

Humour: gentle and physical. Creatures slip on frictionless ground, sleepwalking guards bump into trees, and puffballs bleat at the worst moment.

The Archivist's laws are also called the rules of the world, and the creatures born where a page landed are the ruleborn. Both words mean the same thing; Keepers use whichever the moment calls for.

4Core loops

Ruleborn has four loops nested inside each other. The inner loops happen every session; the outer loops give reasons to come back for months.

Heists

Loot, pages, renown, strays

New laws, buildings, realms, discoveries

Stronger characters, cleverer defence

Then back to better heists. Each turn around the loop takes one or two sessions early on and a few days later.

| Loop
| Length
| What the player does
| What it feeds

| Heist
| 3 minutes
| Scout, plan a route, cross realms, open the vault, escape
| Loot, pages, renown

| Session
| 5 to 9 minutes
| Collect production, reassign workers, run one or two heists, weave or upgrade, queue a Lab experiment
| Steady growth and a reason to return in an hour

| Discovery
| 2 to 5 days
| Unlock a law from pages, open a realm, find a resonance, redesign the map
| New ways to play and defend

| Season
| 8 weeks
| Climb renown leagues, fight anomalies with the constellation, adapt to weekly eras, optionally begin a new epoch
| Long-term goals and fresh starts

Logic
The two scarce things are stability (how much law your universe can hold) and characters' time (each one can work, guard or raid, never two at once). Almost every decision in the game is a choice about one of these two.

5Your universe

A Keeper's universe is a small sky of floating islands: the Heart and up to six outer realms, joined by bridges. This section sets out where each kind of rule applies, how realms and links work, how raiders arrive, and how much law a universe can hold.

5.1 Where rules apply

Rules nest like boxes. Each box sits inside a bigger one, and when rules meet, the smaller, inner box has the final say.

[SVG]
The six scopes, from the widest to the narrowest. Rules are applied from the outside in, so inner rules override outer ones.

| Scope
| Set by
| Applies to
| Lasts
| Example

| Era
| The game
| Every universe
| One week
| High Tide: Slow rivers costs no stability

| Grand Law
| A constellation's vote
| Every member's universe, as a free extra crown law
| One week
| Bloom in every member's realms

| Crown law
| The Keeper, at the Spire
| Every realm of that universe, at full strength
| While woven
| Stillness everywhere: raiders can hide in any realm, but so can nothing move unseen

| Realm law
| The Keeper, at the Spire
| One realm only
| While woven
| Slow rivers in Tidehold

| Terrain
| The Keeper
| The tiles it covers
| Until changed
| A pond, tall grass, a cliff

| Zone
| Wards, raider casts, Sage powers
| A circle of tiles
| Seconds
| A cast of Haste for 10 seconds

| Character
| Birth, traits, level
| One character, everywhere
| Always
| A Swimmer ignores Slow rivers

Logic: crown and realm laws

A crown law works in every realm of its universe, including the Heart, and costs double stability. The Spire opens 1 crown slot at Spire level 2 and a second at Spire level 5.

A realm law works only in its own realm. The Heart has 2 realm slots; outer realms have 1, 2 or 3 by realm level.

The same law can't be both a crown law and a realm law in one universe; the stronger tier would simply apply.

A crown law adds 5% to its school's resource in every realm; a realm law adds 10% in its own realm.

5.2 Realms

Every universe has one Heart and up to six outer realms. Outer realms open at Keeper levels 3, 6, 10, 15, 21 and 28, and the player picks each one's type from those not yet owned, so a finished universe has one realm of each type.

| Type
| School
| Shape
| Boosted resource
| Signature terrain

| Heart
| Neutral
| Circle
| None
| Gardens and courtyards around the Spire

| Skyfield
| Motion
| Chevron
| Starlight
| Plateaus, updrafts, stepping stones

| Crystalline

| Tidehold

In its own realm, a school's laws cost 20% less stability, buildings making that school's resource produce 10% more, and the Nursery favours that school's ruleborn.

5.3 Links

| Link
| Crossing
| Special rule
| Limit
| Cost

| Stone bridge
| Normal speed
| Can hold one ward
| None
| Free

| Rope bridge
| 30% faster
| Can carry a Snap that drops the bridge; anyone on it falls back
| None
| 300

| Chute
| Fast, one way
| The owner sets the direction
| 2
| 1,500

| Rift
| Instant
| Closes for 30 seconds when an alarm sounds in either realm; 20 Aether an hour
| 1
| 5,000

The Heart has four link sockets and outer realms three. Every realm must connect to the Heart.

A universe needs one link per outer realm and may have two extra, for loops and shortcuts.

At least one walkable route must reach the Heart from every outer realm's edge. The game refuses layouts that seal the Vault.

5.4 Landing and leaving

Raiders arrive by skiff, a small flying boat, and the whole edge of the universe is open to them. Defenders can't choose where raiders come from; they can only make every edge a puzzle.

| Rule
| Detail

| Landing
| The crew lands on any edge tile of any outer realm the raider chooses. The Heart is ringed by strong winds, so skiffs can't land there.

| Being seen landing
| A landing skiff can be heard: guards within 6 tiles of the landing spot start at 250 of 1,000 suspicion (12.17), unless the spot is in fog, darkness or tall grass.

| Leaving
| At any time the crew can call the skiff to any edge tile of any realm. It takes 5 seconds to reach an outer realm and 20 seconds to fight the winds around the Heart. The crew must hold the spot until it arrives.

| Faults
| When a universe is overloaded (5.5), faults open between realms: cracks a crew can jump through once per heist, ignoring the links.

| Heist clock
| 150 seconds plus 15 seconds for each link on the shortest route from the landing realm to the Heart, up to 240 seconds.

Why open landing
Choosing where to land is one of the most fun decisions a raider makes, and it shouldn't belong to the defender. Open edges also make defence about the whole island: cliffs, tall grass, lantern light and laws along every edge, not one guarded gate.

5.5 Stability

Stability is how much law a universe can hold. It now comes from buildings, so growing it is part of the economy, and its effects come in seven bands instead of three.

Capacity = Keeper's Spire (30 + 8 × Spire level) + every Anchor Stone (8 + 5 × its level), raised 8% for each epoch after the first

Cost of a law = base cost × tier (1.0, 1.3 or 1.6) × 2 if crowned × 0.8 in a realm of its school × 0.85 for each harmony × 0.8 for a signature resonance × 0.9 in a chord × 1.25 for a dissonance

Strain = total cost of woven laws ÷ capacity

Harmonies, resonances, chords and dissonances come from the notes laws carry (see 8). At the maximum, a Spire at level 10 and seven Anchor Stones at level 10 give 516 stability, while a full set of 22 woven laws at tier III costs around 500 to 560, so a finished universe always sits near its limit.

| Band
| Strain
| Effect
| What the islands look like

| Quiet
| Under 40%
| None
| Still air; laws barely visible

| Steady
| 40 to 70%
| +5% production everywhere
| Pylons glow softly

| Humming
| 70 to 90%
| +10% production
| A low hum; motes drift up from the pylons

| Brimming
| 90 to 100%
| +15% production; pylons have 10% more health
| Islands shimmer; the Spire's rings spin faster

| Strained
| 100 to 110%
| +15% production; one fault opens between two realms
| A glowing crack runs across one island

| Cracking
| 110 to 125%
| Two or three faults; during heists each law has a 5% chance every 10 seconds of blinking off for 2 seconds
| Cracks spread; laws flicker

| Breaking
| 125% and over
| No new weaving; four faults; laws flicker twice as often
| Islands tremble; stones drift loose

Logic
Production bonuses reward using the capacity you've built; faults and flickering are the price of pushing past it. Overloading is a legitimate choice for a player who wants more laws and accepts a weaker defence. Faults close as soon as strain drops back under 100%.

5.6 Weaving laws

Laws are woven into slots at the Keeper's Spire: 10 minutes at tier I, 40 minutes at tier II and 2 hours at tier III. Weaving costs 40 Aether per point of the law's stability cost.

While a slot is being woven it is empty. Removing a law is instant, but the slot then rests for an hour before it can be woven again.

Upkeep is 0.5 Aether an hour per point of cost. It pauses once the player has been away for 24 hours, and if Aether runs out, laws run at half strength rather than switching off.

5.7 The realm bible

Each realm type has a palette, a soundtrack, wildlife, a level-design pattern that shapes how heists play there, and one signature moment it exists to produce. The pattern is the important part: it is what makes a Tidehold heist feel different from an Emberreach heist even before any law is woven.

The HeartNeutral

| Palette and ground
| Cream flagstones, gold trim, clipped hedges

| What is built here
| The Spire, the Vault, the Lab, the Observatory, the Beacon, the Dock

| Music
| Soft bells over a low drone

| Wildlife
| Lantern moths

| Level-design pattern
| A ring of open plaza around the Vault, so the last 20 m are always exposed

| How defenders use it
| Wards on the plaza; guards on the stairs, not at the edge

| Its signature moment
| The Spire's light going out when a pylon breaks

SkyfieldMotion

| Palette and ground
| Pale gold grass, white stone, floating pebbles

| What is built here
| Star Wells cluster here; ramps and stepping stones

| Music
| Wind chimes and a high flute

| Wildlife
| Sky-drifting jellyfish

| Level-design pattern
| Plateaus at three heights with one-way drops, so routes commit early

| How defenders use it
| Let them climb, then remove the way down

| Its signature moment
| A crew leaping a gap they only just reach

CrystallineMatter

| Palette and ground
| Lilac stone, violet crystal, ice sheets

| What is built here
| Star Wells and Anchor Stones; glass walls

| Music
| Glass marimba and a struck bell

| Wildlife
| Prism beetles

| Level-design pattern
| See-through walls: the route is visible but long

| How defenders use it
| Watch what they can already see; hide only one thing

| Its signature moment

TideholdTime

| Palette and ground

| Wildlife

| Level-design pattern
| Water as a moat, crossings as chokepoints

| How defenders use it
| Make them wade; wading is loud and slow

| Its signature moment
| A Tidewalker strolling through water that stops everyone else

EmberreachEnergy

| Palette and ground
| Rust rock, black basalt, lava cracks, ember vents

| What is built here
| Aether Springs; Guardian Posts

| Music
| Low drums and crackling

| Wildlife
| Ash moths

| Level-design pattern
| Narrow ledges over drops, with timed vents

| How defenders use it
| Time the vents to the patrol, not to the raider

| Its signature moment
| The vent bursting a second after a crew slips past

VerdantLife

| Palette and ground
| Rich meadow, hedges, climbing vines, a great tree at level 5

| What is built here
| Gardens; Nurseries

| Music
| Birdsong and kalimba

| Wildlife
| Puffball flocks, bees

| Level-design pattern
| Hedge mazes that regrow, so the map changes mid-heist

| How defenders use it
| Cut your own paths and let Bloom close them behind

| Its signature moment
| A hedge closing behind the crew, sealing the retreat

Lantern ValeSpirit

| Palette and ground
| Deep blue twilight, fog banks, glowing mushrooms, willows

| What is built here
| Gardens; the most lanterns

| Music
| Choir pads and a distant bell

| Wildlife
| Fireflies, moths

| Level-design pattern
| Light and fog: safety is a pool of light in a dark realm

| How defenders use it
| Light the routes you can watch and leave the rest black

| Its signature moment
| Stepping out of the fog into a lantern's circle, seen

5.8 One producer of each kind per realm

Stated here once, because three tables assumed different answers and the arithmetic only works for one of them. A realm holds one Star Well, one Aether Spring and one Garden, however high its level rises.

Why not two. Permitting a second was a pure gain, so everyone would build it, and it doubled income against every pacing date in 14. Re-running the economy model, only one producer per realm reproduces the published curve — within 10% of the day-301 figure and three days on the Keeper track. Every other reading was out by two to four times.

Staffing is partial and worthwhile. A producer runs at the share of its worker slots that are filled, so a half-staffed Well earns half. The roster can never fill everything — 38 ruleborn against far more positions — and that is the scarcity the design is built on (4), now connected to something real.

Raiding costs production. A crew on a heist is assigned to it, like a guard at a post, and does not work while assigned. Five of thirty-eight is a real choice; the older version withheld them for about 2% of a month and the trade-off the design claims did not exist.

6Terrain

Without walls, the ground shapes every route. Terrain is the Keeper's main building material for defence: 39 features in seven families, each changing how creatures move, what guards can see, and how laws behave.

6.1 Rules

Budget: an outer realm has 30 terrain points plus 15 per realm level (45 to 105). The Heart has 40 plus 10 per Spire level. Reshaping is free and instant; it's the budget that limits it.

Snapshots: a heist is played against the terrain as it was when the heist started.

The route rule: terrain can lengthen routes but never close the last route from the edges to the Heart.

Height: four heights (0 to 3). Dropping down is free and one-way, so height creates one-way paths without chutes.

Noise: stairs, gravel, scree, bells and bounce mushrooms make noise; noise raises the awareness of guards nearby.

Why so much terrain
Terrain is cheap to build in code, readable at a glance, and multiplies with laws: 39 features against 48 laws give well over a thousand distinct interactions, most of them visible in how creatures move. It also makes defence creative, which is what feeds the weekly defence challenges (18.5).

7Laws

Laws are the torn pages of the Book: rules an island follows. They are what players collect, the source of each island's economy and defence, and the puzzle in every heist. There are 48 at launch in six schools, three paradoxes found only in the Lab, and a system of notes that decides how laws combine (see 8).

7.1 What a law is

| Property
| Rule

| School
| One of six families, each with a colour and a shape so it never depends on colour alone.

| Rank
| Common, uncommon, rare or deep, with paradoxes outside the scale. Rank sets how long a law takes to acquire — not how strong it is (7.9).

| Stability cost
| From 10 to 26 points (40 to 50 for paradoxes), before the modifiers in 5.5.

| Notes
| One or two of eight notes (Flow, Weight, Glow, Hush, Bloom, Echo, Spark, Veil) that decide harmonies and dissonances (8).

| Tiers
| I, II and III. Higher tiers are stronger and cost more stability (× 1.3 and × 1.6).

| Where it applies
| As a realm law, in one realm; as a crown law, in every realm of the universe (5.1).

| Symmetry
| A law applies to everyone where it rules: the owner's guards and wards as much as a raiding crew.

| Movement signature
| Every law changes how characters move, so a player can read the rule by watching (7.4, 21.7).

| Economy
| A realm law adds 10% to its school's resource in its realm; a crown law adds 5% everywhere.

7.2 Schools

| School
| Shape
| Theme
| Boosts
| Most common notes

| Motion
| Chevron
| Jumping, sliding, wind, slopes
| Starlight
| Flow

| Matter
| Cube
| Walls, weight, glass, crystal
| Starlight
| Weight, Glow

| Time
| Hourglass
| Speed, pauses, cycles, rewinds
| Aether
| Echo, Veil, Hush

| Energy
| Bolt
| Light, heat, sparks, storms
| Aether
| Spark, Glow

| Life

| Growth, healing, flocks, pollen
| Essence
| Bloom

| Spirit
| Eye
| Echoes, illusions, dreams, masks
| Essence
| Echo, Veil, Glow

7.3 How laws combine

Each realm has a rule sheet of parameters that every character, guard and ward reads. Laws change parameters; they never contain special cases for particular characters.

| Parameter
| Default
| Changed by, for example

| Speed and direction
| 1.0 ×, free
| Haste, Current, Tilt, Inward pull, Wild paths, Honeycomb

| Jumping, climbing, leaping
| 1 tile, no climbing
| Half gravity, Heavy ground, Leapfrog, Honeycomb

| Friction
| On
| Frictionless, ice

| Sight, light and hearing
| 6 tiles, light, normal hearing
| Glowing dark, Aurora, Day and night, Glass, Sleepwalk

| Trails and noise
| None
| Spark trails, Haunting, Afterimage, Leapfrog, Flock

| Damage and toughness
| 1.0 ×
| Brittle stone, Heat bloom, Density

| Healing and regrowth
| None
| Regrowth, Kinship, Bloom

| Flow of time
| Constant
| Haste, Delay, Rewind, Held breath

| Loot weight and type
| 1.0 ×, fixed
| Heavy ground, Transmute, Lodestones

| Who is who
| Clear
| Mirror images, Borrowed Face

| Revival
| None
| Echoes, Soul swap, Swarm birth

Logic: order of application

Start from the defaults.

Apply the Era, then the Grand Law, then crown laws, then the realm's laws, in the fixed school order Motion, Matter, Time, Energy, Life, Spirit.

Apply terrain, then zones (wards and raider casts).

Apply Sage powers, which can suspend or flip any law.

Apply each character's own traits and immunities last.

Multipliers multiply; on/off rules take the last value applied. The simulation runs at a fixed 30 steps a second so heists replay identically on the server (the rate is defined once, in 24.10, and every other mention is checked against it).

7.4 The 48 laws

Each school has three common, two uncommon, one rare and two deep laws — eight laws a school, six schools, 48 in all. The table gives each law's rank and base stability cost, its notes, what it does at tier I and higher tiers, and how it changes the way characters move.

Motion
Shape: chevron. Boosts Starlight.

| Law
| Rank, stability cost
| Notes
| Effect at tier I
| Tiers II and III
| How characters move

| Bounce

| Common, 12
| Flow

| Common, 11
| Flow

| ±40%, ±50%

| Uncommon, 15

7.5 Paradoxes

Paradoxes are wild laws found only in the Lab, by combining two tier III laws with opposite notes. Each such experiment has a 10% chance of revealing one, and the Lab warns when a pairing is "unstable". A universe can hold one paradox at a time, and paradoxes give no economy bonus.

| Paradox
| Cost
| Effect
| How characters move

| Reversal
| 45
| The Heart swaps places with the outermost realm: the Vault moves to the frontier and every route is reversed.
| Everyone walks with a slight upside-down sway; hair points at the sky.

| Twin suns
| 50
| Every other law in the realm applies twice.
| Every character casts two shadows and moves with doubled flourishes.

| Unmaking
| 40
| Every 20 seconds one law in the realm, raider casts included, switches off at random for 10 seconds.
| Characters stutter as rules blink in and out.

7.6 Getting and improving laws

Pages from heists, which is the point of raiding. After each heist the player picks one law the crew crossed to study and receives 2 of its pages, 3 if the heist was clean. The first time a player meets a law it joins the Codex with 1 page. Breaking a pylon gives a page of its law; a foiled heist gives one.

Raiding is the efficient path, not the only one. A Library at the Heart produces about one page per realm every few hours — enough to keep restoring something slowly, nowhere near enough to raise a school. A single heist is worth roughly two to three sessions of that trickle. Making raiding the sole source would punish the many players who prefer building (13.1), and gating a resource behind one activity is where players stop feeling directed and start feeling forced [210].

Pages lean toward what you raided. Raiding a Tidehold yields mostly Time pages — around two in three — with the rest spread across the schools you met. That is enough to make "I want Time laws, so I raid Tideholds" a real plan, without the resentment that comes from an exclusive lock [211].

Nothing is gated by the clock. No day-of-the-week rotations, no timed schools, no windows. The most-complained-about version of directional farming is the one that decides when you may pursue what you want [210]; ours decides only where.

A floor for bad luck. Every eighth heist guarantees a page of a law you are actually working on, so an unlucky run of targets never costs a week.

The Codex opens in chapters: 12 laws are findable from the start, 24 from Keeper level 6, 36 from level 12, 42 from level 18, and all 48 from level 24. Matchmaking only shows universes running laws from open chapters.

Restoring a law: 15 pages for a common law, 30 for a rare one, 60 for a deep one.

Tiers: tier II costs 20 more pages and 2,000 Aether (Spire level 4); tier III costs 40 more pages and 8,000 Aether (Spire level 7).

Other sources: Lab experiments that find nothing give 3 pages of a law from those schools; the Collector swaps pages; constellation members can gift pages; daily goals give a few.

Starting laws: Half gravity, Slow rivers and Bloom, all at tier I.

Why pages come from crossing
Players learn new laws by experiencing them on someone else's island. Every heist is also a lesson, which gives a reason to raid islands with unfamiliar laws rather than always the easiest target.

7.7 Every law in full

Each law's full sheet: what it does at every tier, how it changes movement, how the realm looks under it, how a raider turns it to their advantage, how a defender uses it, where it is weak, and what it sounds like. A law that cannot be used by both sides is not finished, and a law without a weakness is not shipped.

BounceCommon · Motion · cost 14

Flow Echo

| Tier I
| Walls and wards bounce back 50% of what hits them, including characters.

| Tiers II and III
| 65%, 80%

| How characters move
| Squash-and-stretch rebounds off walls with a springy boing.

| How the realm looks
| Walls wobble like jelly when touched.

| A raider uses it to
| Ricochet past a guard you cannot out-fight.

| A defender uses it to
| Line wards along walls so a bounce lands the crew in them.

| Its weakness
| Wardens are heavy enough to absorb a rebound.

| It sounds like
| a rising whistle and a soft thump on landing

Half gravityCommon · Motion · cost 12

Flow

| Tier I
| Everyone jumps twice as high and can leap one obstacle; thrown attacks fly 30% farther.

| Tiers II and III
| Leap 2 obstacles; range +40%, +50%

| How characters move
| Floaty strides and long, slow hops; cloaks and loot sacks drift upward.

| How the realm looks
| Pebbles hover a hand above the ground; dust falls slowly.

| A raider uses it to
| Leap the gaps the defender cannot wall off; reach roofs and ledges.

| A defender uses it to
| Put loot where only a long leap reaches it, then watch that landing.

| Its weakness
| Heavy carriers barely rise; wind and slopes push a leaping crew off course.

| It sounds like
| a rising whistle and a soft thump on landing

Flow

| Tier I

| Tiers II and III
| ±40%, ±50%

| A defender uses it to

| Its weakness

| It sounds like
| a rising whistle and a soft thump on landing

7.8 How much there is to understand

Depth comes from combining laws, which is also the biggest risk: too many unfamiliar rules at once and new players leave. Every heist has a complexity budget.

| Rule
| Keeper levels 1 to 9
| 10 to 19
| 20 and up

| Laws on the route the player has never met
| 1 at most
| 2 at most
| 3 at most

| Rules that interact at any one moment
| Never more than 4, at any level. Working memory holds about four chunks of new, interacting information [75] [76], and difficulty rises with how many pieces must be held at once [77] [78]. A realm may hold more laws than that only when the extra ones do not interact with the crew's route.

| Realms on a typical route
| 2 to 3
| 3 to 4
| 3 to 5

| Harmony shown on the route
| Harmonies only
| Plus signature resonances
| Plus chords and paradoxes

Laws are always visible: every law has a movement signature, a world signature, a card with a looping preview, and a two-second banner when the crew enters its realm.

Colour is never the only signal: every school has a shape, and every note has a small symbol.

Each law is taught alone first: the Drifting ruins introduce each new law on its own before it appears in combinations (15).

7.9 Rarity buys specificity, not strength

Version 2.0 splits three ranks into four, plus paradoxes. The rule behind the split matters more than the split: rarity governs how long a law takes to get, while stability cost and conditions govern how strong it is. These are two dials, and the earlier version tied them together.

| Rank
| Count
| Character
| Pages to restore

| Common
| 18
| Always useful, small effect, no drawback. Three per school
| 10

| Uncommon
| 14
| Conditional, with a mild cost
| 20

| Rare
| 10
| Strong inside a condition you have to build for
| 35

| Deep
| 6
| Changes how a realm works. Heavy stability cost and a real trade-off. One per school
| 60

| Paradox
| 3
| Universe-wide, one at a time, found only in the Lab
| 120

This follows the published design philosophy of the game that invented collectable rarity: rarity is set by complexity, size of effect and excitement, and a higher rarity does not mean a card must be more powerful [207]. The honest counter-evidence is that in every large shipped game rarer cards are stronger in practice, and no competitive game has fully achieved the separation [208]. So something else has to do the balancing work.

Power is paid for with legibility

A deep law is more dramatic, not more efficient — and every rare and deep law appears in the scout report (12.5). Raiders can see it coming and plan around it. This visibility is charged against the law's strength the way range is charged against a character's kit, and it is the primary reason a rare law can be exciting without being unfair. If playtests show rare and deep laws still producing materially higher defence rates than common ones, the answer is more stability cost or a tighter condition, not a smaller number.

Conditions, not punishments. A rare law is powerful when a condition you control is met. Laws that simply hurt their owner are avoided: the evidence on collectable design is that conditional build-around power is enjoyed while flat drawbacks are resented [207].

Collecting is grouped by school, eight laws each. A set of 48 is far past the point where a collection reads as completable, while seven to twelve is where people push to finish [209]; six sets of eight gives that feeling six times over.

8Harmony: how laws combine

Version 0.2 had a list of 32 special pairs. Harmony replaces that with a system players can learn and predict: every law carries one or two notes, and notes decide what happens when laws share an island. Players meet it like music: some laws sound well together, some clash, and a few special combinations sing.

8.1 The eight notes

| Note
| What laws with this note do
| Opposite
| Symbol

| Flow
| Change how things move: speed, direction, jumping, sliding
| Weight
| A wave

| Weight
| Slow, pull, hold and weigh down
| Flow
| An anchor

| Glow
| Light, reveal and show
| Hush
| A sun

| Hush
| Hide, silence and still
| Glow
| A crescent

| Bloom
| Grow, heal and multiply
| Spark
| A sprout

| Spark
| Burst, stun, break and surge
| Bloom
| A star

| Echo
| Return, repeat and rewind
| Veil
| A ring

| Veil
| Disguise, deceive and transform
| Echo
| A mask

[SVG]The eight notes. Laws that share a note harmonise; laws with opposite notes (joined by dashed lines) clash. The number shows how many of the 48 laws carry each note.

8.2 Harmonies

When two laws in the same realm share a note, they harmonise. Crown laws count in every realm, so a crown law can harmonise with every realm's laws. A harmony lowers the stability cost of both laws by 15% and strengthens the shared kind of effect. Harmonies are shown automatically when weaving, so players learn them by doing.

| Shared note
| Harmony effect on both laws
| Example

| Flow
| Movement effects 25% stronger
| Current + Tilt: wind and slope together

| Weight
| Slowing and pulling 25% stronger
| Slow rivers + Heavy ground

| Glow
| Light and reveal ranges 2 tiles longer
| Soul lanterns + Spark trails

| Hush
| Hiding works 1 tile closer to guards
| Stillness + Glowing dark

| Bloom
| Regrowth and healing 25% stronger
| Bloom + Kinship

| Spark
| Damage and stuns 25% stronger
| Static + Chain lightning

| Echo
| Returns, rewinds and repeats 25% sooner or stronger
| Rewind + Echoes

| Veil
| Illusions and disguises last 25% longer
| Mirror images + Borrowed Face

8.3 Dissonances

When a realm holds laws with opposite notes, they clash. A dissonance raises both laws' stability cost by 25% and adds a visible, unpredictable effect. Some players will choose dissonance on purpose, to make their islands chaotic for raiders.

| Clash
| Effect in the realm
| What it looks like

| Flow against Weight
| Gusts and lulls: everyone's speed changes by ±20% at random every 10 seconds
| Characters lurch and stall

| Glow against Hush
| Flicker: every 15 seconds the realm goes fully bright or fully dark for 3 seconds
| Light strobes across the island

| Bloom against Spark
| Wildfire: hedges and grass hit by any spark effect burn for 5 seconds, hurting anyone inside
| Small flames race through greenery

| Echo against Veil
| Déjà vu: once per heist, an illusion of the crew appears somewhere else and draws guards
| A ghostly copy of the crew

8.4 Signature resonances

A signature resonance is a named, hand-designed bonus between two specific laws. It lowers both laws' cost by 20% and adds a unique effect. There are 44 at launch: the 32 from version 0.2 and 12 for the new laws. Unlike harmonies, they are hidden until discovered in the Lab, or hinted at after crossing them in someone else's universe.

8.5 Chords

A chord is three different notes, none of them opposites, carried by laws in the same realm (crown laws count). Chords are the late-game discovery: they need three distinct laws in play and a Lab of level 3 to identify. A chord lowers the cost of all three laws by 10% and adds a strong effect.

| Chord
| Notes
| Effect

| Tempest
| Flow Spark Glow
| Everyone moves 20% faster, and every 20 seconds lightning lights the whole realm for a second.

| Deep sleep
| Weight Hush Bloom
| Every 20 seconds each guard dozes for 3 seconds; flowers muffle footsteps.

| Ghost tide
| Flow Echo Hush
| Knocked-out characters drift back to where they entered the realm instead of being caught.

| Fair lights
| Glow Bloom Veil
| Floating lights form illusions of crew members that draw guards away.

| Iron bell
| Weight Echo Glow
| Every step echoes: guards hear 2 tiles further but see 2 tiles less.

| Mirage
| Flow Veil Glow
| Anything moving appears one tile from where it really is.

| Clockwork
| Weight Spark Echo
| Every 15 seconds all wards in the realm trigger together.

| Wild hunt
| Flow Spark Hush
| Anyone running makes no noise at all.

| Hidden garden
| Bloom Hush Veil
| Tall grass spreads one tile a minute across the realm.

| Lantern festival
| Glow Bloom Echo
| Lanterns heal anyone in their light and revive each character once.

| Quicksand
| Weight Veil Hush
| A few tiles become hidden pits that drop anyone a level.

| Rebound
| Flow Echo Spark
| Every stun bounces on to the nearest enemy.

8.6 How players discover combinations

| Kind
| How it's found
| Shown when
| Count

| Harmony
| Automatic whenever two laws share a note
| While weaving and scouting, from the first day
| 8 kinds

| Dissonance
| Automatic whenever opposite notes meet
| While weaving, with a warning
| 4 kinds

| Signature resonance
| The Lab, or crossing it in a heist (gives a hint)
| Once discovered; the Codex shows how many remain in each school
| 44

| Chord
| The Lab from level 3, or crossing one (gives a hint)
| Once discovered
| 12

| Paradox
| The Lab, from two tier III laws with opposite notes
| Once discovered
| 3

Why notes
A list of pairs has to be memorised; notes can be reasoned about. A player who knows that Hush laws hide better together can predict hundreds of combinations without looking anything up, which is how Genshin Impact's elemental reactions and Balatro's jokers stay learnable while feeling endless. The hidden signatures and chords keep the thrill of discovery on top of that.

8.7 What happens when everything applies at once

A law, a resonance and a chord can all change the same thing. The result must be identical on the phone, on the server and a year later, so the order is fixed rather than arbitrary. It is adapted from the rules engine that has been solving this problem in public for thirty years [217].

| Step
| Rule

| 1
| Kind before number. Changes to what something is — terrain becoming shadow, a guard gaining an ability — resolve before changes to how much of something there is. Within numbers, "becomes X" comes before "and then add Y"

| 2
| Older first. A law woven while designing the island applies before one triggered mid-heist

| 3
| Dependencies jump the queue. If one effect changes what another does or whom it touches, the second waits for the first. Circular cases fall back to age order

| 4
| Ties break by name. Lower rank, then alphabetical order, then the older object. Because names are unique and fixed, a tie breaks the same way everywhere, forever

Paradoxes always win. They are written as "cannot" rather than "less", and resolve last inside their layer, so no ordinary law can overturn one.

8.8 A chord needs three laws, not three notes

Because 23 of the 48 laws carry two notes, every chord was reachable with two laws, several with two commons — Slow rivers and Whispers together gave Iron bell for 26 stability, inside a second-day player's budget. A chord is meant to be the late discovery, so it now requires three distinct laws contributing the three notes. One line, and 8.5 becomes true.

The same count also rebalanced the notes themselves: Glow was carried by twelve laws and its opposite Hush by six, so the revealing note was abundant and the hiding note scarce — for a defender who has every reason to reveal. The table now runs eight or nine laws per note, and since the note count is frozen at eight and laws are the expansion axis (24), that balance is a permanent constraint on every future season rather than a one-time tidy.

9.3 Production

Output an hour = base × 1.35level − 1 × staffing (0.6, 0.85 or 1.0) × (1 + 0.10 per realm law of a matching school + 0.05 per matching crown law) × 1.1 in a realm of that school × (1 + worker bonuses) × stability band bonus × (1 − 0.10 per fault in the realm)

Base: Star Well 80 Starlight, Aether Spring 60 Aether, Garden 40 Essence. Each building holds 12 hours of output until collected.

9.4 Storage, exposure and theft

One-way theft (changed in version 0.8)
By default a raider's haul is created by the game from a snapshot of the target, and the defender loses nothing at all. Defenders still earn rewards for foiling raids. Players who want real stakes opt into the Open Vault league, where the rules below apply in full and rewards are 40% higher on both sides. The reasoning is in 27.5.

The Vault holds most Starlight and Essence. Its sealed share (40% at Vault level 1, rising to 71.5% at Vault level 10) can never be stolen.

Storehouses in outer realms add capacity and are fully exposed, so where you place them decides what raiders pass on the way in.

What a heist pays is computed once, in 9.6, and the exposed amount here only nudges it inside a narrow band. The percentages this paragraph used to carry are gone: they described a world where the loot left the defender, and it does not.

Balance target: a successful heist against an even match brings about a fifth of a day's production, so heists supply roughly a third of an active player's resources.

Entry fee: 2% of the raider's Starlight, from 100 to 25,000, returned in full after a clean heist.

Defence rewards: a foiled heist gives the defender the entry fee plus 5% of what the crew was carrying.

| What

| Refreshing targets

| Raider casts

| A new ruleborn

| Character levels

| Waking a tired character
| 2 Essence per character level per minute of rest left

| Sage levels

| Heist entry fee

| Cosmetics
| Starglass only

9.6 What a raid pays

The number at the centre of the economy, and until now undefined. Because theft is one-way, a haul is minted rather than taken — which makes it a pure faucet, and makes its size the single dial that sets how fast the whole game inflates.

The decision that shapes everything else
The obvious approach is the one base builders use: pay the raider a percentage of what the defender is holding [223]. That cannot work here. In those games the loot leaves the defender, so the money supply is unchanged; here it is created, so tying the payout to how much people hoard would tie the inflation rate to behaviour we neither see clearly nor control. The haul is therefore driven by the raider's own level, and the target's wealth only nudges it within a narrow band.

Haul = B(V) × grade × difficulty × richness × fatigue × fill

| Term
| Value
| What it does

| B(V), the base
| 100 × 1.5(V−1)
| V is the target's Vault level, one to ten — not the realm level, which stops at five. 100 at Vault level 1, about 500 at 5, about 3,800 at 10. The master inflation lever. Changing the growth rate from 1.5 to 1.65 nearly triples the faucet by level 10, so it is locked first and moved rarely

| Grade
| Clean 1.0, loud 0.7, partial 0.4, foiled 0
| Supersedes the older outcome multipliers in 12.9

| Difficulty
| Level or above 1.0 to 1.2, one below 0.8, two below 0.5, three or more below 0.25
| Punching up pays a little more; farming the weak pays almost nothing. The bonus is capped so it cannot be gamed

| Richness
| Exposed wealth against the level median, clamped between 0.7 and 1.3
| A richer island is worth more, but a hoarder can never spike the faucet. A clean raid on a fully sealed island still pays 0.7 — nobody ever leaves with nothing [224]

| Fill
| The share of the crew's bags actually loaded
| What you carry is what you bank (12.19). This is the mechanism behind the Vault phase's central decision, and it does not raise the ceiling: a full crew is fill 1.0

| Fatigue
| 0.85 on each successive raid that day
| Six raids pay about 4.15 times one, so playing more is worth it and playing endlessly is not

What is exposed: everything in storehouses, plus the unsealed part of the Vault, where the sealed share runs 40% at realm level 1 and rises by 3.5 points a level to 71.5% at level 10 (9.4).

What it works out to

| Vault level
| Base
| A clean raid
| Average per attempt
| A full day, six raids

| 2
| 150
| 150
| 68
| about 284

| 6
| 759
| 759
| 346
| about 1,437

| 10
| 3,844
| 3,844
| 1,753
| about 7,278

The average assumes defences hold 37.5% of the time — the middle of the target band in 13.2 — and that successful raids split roughly 35% clean, 40% loud, 25% partial. An attempt is worth about 46% of a clean same-level haul, which is the number the progression curve is built on: an upgrade costing 25 clean raids takes about 55 attempts, or nine days at six a day, at every level.

The caps that hold it

Per raid: never more than 1.3 times the base, which is what the richness clamp already implies.

Per day: the fatigue curve, plus a hard ceiling half again above it for players punching up.

Per week: five days' worth, not seven — so missing two days a week costs nothing, and grinding every day gains little.

There is no hard-currency sink, because nothing is sold for power. The caps are the inflation control, and the health signal is the weekly growth in what players are holding [225].

What has to be watched
As players get better, more raids come out clean, which raises the faucet without anything changing. If the clean rate climbs from 35% to 60%, payouts rise about 14% on their own. The grade split is monitored and the caps re-tuned rather than the formula rewritten.

10Buildings

Sixteen kinds of building. There are no build timers: building and upgrading are instant, and the pace comes from resources and from the Spire's level, which caps every other building. Every level of every building is listed below with its cost, what it gives, and when an engaged player typically builds it.

10.1 Placement rules

Buildings sit on the realm grid and block movement and sight. Most are 2 by 2 tiles; the Spire and Vault are 3 by 3.

No building may be taller than the Spire's level allows: a building's level can't exceed the Spire's (for five-level buildings, half the Spire's level, rounded up).

Producing buildings need workers: 1 slot at levels 1 to 3, 2 at levels 4 to 6, 3 at levels 7 to 10.

Moving a building is free and instant, like terrain.

10.2 Every level of every building

"Engaged player builds it" is the day an engaged player's first building of that kind reaches the level, from the simulation in 14. "Income hours to afford" is the cost divided by that player's hourly Starlight production at that moment, a measure of how big the purchase feels.

10.3 Wards

| Ward
| School
| Effect at level 1
| Resets after

| Gravity Well
| Motion
| Pulls anyone within 3 tiles to its centre and holds them 2 seconds.
| 10 s

| Crystal Wall
| Matter
| A breakable wall (800 health) that blocks a path. It can never close the last route to the Heart.
| Stays broken

| Time Snare
| Time
| Freezes the first character to step on it for 2.5 seconds.
| 12 s

| Static Coil
| Energy
| Shocks anyone within 2 tiles every 3 seconds.
| Always on

| Thorn Hedge
| Life
| Blocks a path and hurts anyone pushing through (5% health a second).
| Regrows only with Bloom

| Lantern Glare
| Spirit
| Shows the crew to every guard in the realm for 5 seconds and blinds them for 2.
| 20 s

A rope bridge can carry a Snap instead of a ward: when triggered, the bridge drops and anyone on it falls back to the realm they came from. Wards of a school are 25% stronger in a realm running a law of the same school.

10.4 The Library

A load-bearing faucet that four sections referenced and no table contained: the seventeenth building type, at the Heart, one per universe. It is the only page source that does not require raiding, which makes it the whole of a non-raider's progression and the reason 13.3 can claim nobody has to build. Three of its ten levels were specified; a curve cannot be priced from three points, so all ten are given here.

| Level
| Cost
| Pages a day
| Notes

| 1
| 900 Starlight
| 3
| Unlocked at Keeper 4, with the first outer realm

| 2
| 1,790
| 5
| Keeper 7

| 3
| 3,550
| 6
| Keeper 10

| 4
| 7,050
| 8
| Keeper 13

| 5
| 14,000
| 9
| Keeper 16 — roughly a page every three hours across the universe

| 6
| 22,500
| 11
| Keeper 20

| 7
| 36,200
| 13
| Keeper 24

| 8
| 58,100
| 14
| Keeper 28

| 9
| 93,400
| 16
| Keeper 34

| 10
| 150,000
| 18
| Keeper 40 — never enough to raise a school alone; a heist is still worth two to three days of it (7.6)

Cost: 900 Starlight at level 1, × 1.99 a level to level 5, × 1.61 a level thereafter, rounded to three figures

Pages a day: 3 at level 1, + 1.5 a level to level 5, + 1.8 a level thereafter

Both curves are fitted to the three levels the document already stated (1, 5 and 10), which are unchanged

Pages from the Library are drawn from schools the player already has laws in, so it deepens what someone is building rather than handing them new directions. Those come from raiding.

What this settles, and what it does not. It settles the count — 17 building types, not 16 (26.1) — and gives the economy a curve to price. It does not settle the pacing that falls out of it: adding the Library to the page table in 14.4 roughly doubles what an engaged player earns, and whether that is right depends on the rarity decision still open in the register. The page economy is re-derived there, not here.

11Characters

Characters are the second scarce thing after stability. Every character is a ruleborn with a school, a role, an ability and two traits, and at any moment is either working, guarding, resting, on a heist or idle.

11.3 Traits

Every ruleborn is born with two traits. They are common (60% of rolls), uncommon (30%) or rare (10%). Traits tied to a law are three times as likely when a character is born in a realm running that law.

| Kind
| Traits

| Body
| Swift: +10% speed. Sturdy: +15% health. Brave: +10% power when outnumbered. Hoarder: +30% carry. Greedy: +50% carry but drops 20% of it when hit. Clumsy: +15% speed, 10% chance to trigger any ward it passes.

| Heist
| Quiet: guards notice it 25% later. Tinker: disarms wards 30% faster. Lucky: 10% chance of a bonus page. Loyal: rests 25% less after a success. Shadow: invisible after standing still 3 seconds.

| Law-linked
| Floaty: an extra leap under Half gravity. Swimmer: ignores Slow rivers. Grounded: unmoved by Current and Inward pull. Sure-footed: doesn't slide on Frictionless. Night-eyed: full sight in the dark. Insulated: immune to Static and Chain lightning. Sun-hardened: immune to Sunburst. Thornproof: immune to Thorns. Clear-headed: immune to Spores and Delay. Echo-touched: returns once at 50% even without Echoes. Lantern-born: safe outside lantern light.

| Home
| Homebody: +20% work, -10% power in heists. Sleepy: +30% work, rests 50% longer. Green thumb: +25% in Gardens. Well-digger: +25% in Star Wells. Spring-finder: +25% in Aether Springs. Watchful: +3 sight as a guard. Stubborn: +25% health as a guard. Teacher: births in its realm roll one extra trait to choose from. Steady: its building keeps producing for one hour after it leaves.

11.4 Levels

Essence to reach level n = 150 × 1.55n−2 (150 for level 2, about 5,000 for level 10)

Each level: +8% health and power, +5% carry. Abilities improve at levels 4, 7 and 10 (15% shorter cooldown and 20% stronger effect each time)

Highest level = 2 × the level of the Hearth the character lives in

11.5 Where characters come from

Births at a Nursery. The kind is drawn from the schools of the laws woven in that realm (weight: the law's tier + 1) and the realm's own school (weight 2). Within a school, roles the player has fewest of are twice as likely. A birth costs 100 Essence plus 40 per character already owned.

Strays found on heists. About one heist in eight finds a stray in the target universe; carrying it out adds it to the roster with three traits, one of them rare.

Visitors' favours, constellation events and the season track.

Every player starts with five ruleborn: three wake in the first minute, and two strays follow the crew home after the first heist, where a free Hearth waits for them. The roster holds 3 characters for the Spire plus the places in every Hearth, around 30 at the most at launch. There is no training and no army camp: the crew for a heist is chosen from characters who are idle or can be pulled off work.

11.6 States and tiredness

| State
| Rules

| Working
| Produces in one building. Production starts 5 minutes after assignment, so constant swapping doesn't pay.

| Guarding
| Stationed at a Guardian Post. Guards defend only while on duty at the moment a heist starts.

| On a heist
| Away for the heist's 3 minutes.

| Resting
| After a heist: 45 minutes after a clean escape, 60 after a loud one, 180 if caught. Hearths shorten rest 5% per level. Waking early costs 2 Essence per level per minute left.

| Idle
| Available for anything.

Why this is the core trade-off
A character on a heist isn't working or guarding. Raiding more means earning less at home and defending worse, so a larger roster is the real measure of strength, and every session starts with a small decision about who does what.

11.7 Sages, the heroes

Sages are rare, named characters who bend rules instead of fighting. A player brings one Sage on each heist, and can station another at the Heart as its guardian. Sages are earned through play and never sold. (They were called Keepers in version 0.1; the name now belongs to the player alone.)

| Sage
| Unlocks
| Heist powers
| At home

| The Archivist
| Start
| Suspend: switch off one law in the current realm for 10 s (1 use). Reveal: show every ward in the realm (2 uses). Recall: bring a knocked-out character back at 40% (1 use).
| Lab experiments 20% faster. On guard: when an alarm sounds, the crew is shown to every guard for 5 s.

| The Tinkerer
| Keeper level 7
| Invert: turn a law into its opposite for 10 s, such as Half gravity into heavy gravity (1 use). Jam: disable a ward for 12 s (2 uses). Plank: lay a 4-tile shortcut over a gap for 15 s (2 uses).
| Buildings cost 10% less. On guard: the first ward the crew disables rearms after 5 s.

| The Gardener
| Keeper level 13
| Vine bridge: join two points up to 6 tiles apart for 20 s (1 use). Renew: heal the crew 30% over 5 s (1 use). Decoy: plant a sapling guards chase for 8 s (2 uses).
| Gardens make 15% more. On guard: hedges and wards in the Heart heal 50% faster.

| The Wanderer
| Keeper level 20
| Copy: take one enemy law and carry it around the crew for the rest of the heist (1 use). Rift: open a two-way rift between two realms already visited for 20 s (1 use). Tailwind: crew moves 40% faster for 6 s (2 uses).
| The Observatory offers one more target. On guard: raiders can't call their skiff to one chosen outer realm for the first 60 s.

Sages level from 1 to 40 with Insight. Every fifth level improves one power (an extra use, a longer effect or a shorter cooldown). The Chronicler (Time) and the Lamplighter (Energy) follow after launch.

11.8 Special characters

Visitors

Visitors arrive at the Visitor's Dock and stay 12 hours.

| Visitor
| What they offer

| The Cartographer
| Sells a full scouting map of one target, with ward positions, for Starlight instead of Aether.

| The Collector
| Swaps pages: three of one law for one of another (five for two at Dock level 3).

| The Stray
| Asks a favour, such as crossing a Slow rivers realm twice or discovering a resonance, and joins the roster with three traits when it's done.

Paradox-born

Legendary ruleborn. While a paradox is woven, the Nursery in its realm can birth its paradox-born once. A player can own one of each.

| Paradox-born
| From
| Gift

| The Reverser
| Reversal
| Swaps places with any character or guard in sight (15 s).

| The Twinned
| Twin suns
| Acts twice: every ability fires two times.

| The Unmade
| Unmaking
| Ignores every law, helpful or harmful.

Anomalies

Cosmic bosses fought by a whole constellation (see 17.3): the Devourer, a drifting black hole; the Hourglass Storm, which scrambles time; and the Wildfire Bloom, where fire and growth run wild.

11.9 How laws shape ruleborn

What hatches: each Nursery weighs the schools around it: 3 for each law woven in its realm, 2 for the realm's own school and 1 for each crown law. A Tidehold Nursery with Slow rivers and Bloom woven hatches Time ruleborn five times in eight and Life ruleborn three times in eight.

Law-linked traits: 30% of births roll a trait tied to one of the realm's laws, such as Swimmer from Slow rivers or Floaty from Half gravity; a tier III law raises that to 40%.

Resistances: many ruleborn resist a law of their own school (a Tidewalker ignores Slow rivers, a Stormhound is immune to Static), which is why crew choice matters against a target's laws.

Movement: laws change how every character moves (7.4), and each body exaggerates it differently: a Featherkin floats furthest under Half gravity, while a Mossbear barely leaves the ground (21.6).

Strays: the surprise strays from heists (14.6) are born under one of the laws the crew crossed.

11.10 Law-shaped forms

Every ruleborn hatches in a form shaped by the laws around its Nursery, so the islands a player designs decide the creatures they collect. Eighteen kinds and eight notes make 144 forms.

| Note of the form
| How it looks
| Its small quirk

| Flow
| Streaming fins, ribbons and swept feathers
| +5% speed

| Weight
| Stone plates and a stocky stance
| +10% carrying

| Glow
| A warm inner light and bright markings
| Lights the tile around it

| Hush
| Midnight colours and soft, silent edges
| Noticed 10% later

| Bloom
| Leaves, petals and moss
| Heals 1% a second while still

| Spark
| Crackling tips and ember flecks
| +5% power

| Echo
| Faint ghost outlines that trail a step behind
| Returns once at 20% after being knocked out

| Veil
| Shimmering, shifting patterns
| A one-second disguise after landing

How forms are decided: the note carried by the most laws in the Nursery's realm, crown laws included, sets the form. When notes tie, the odds are shown before hatching.

Collecting by design: to hatch Veil forms, weave Veil laws next to a Nursery. Forms are never sold, and there are no paid hatches.

The Forms album: completing all eight forms of one kind unlocks an outfit; completing one note across every kind unlocks a skiff sail.

Trading: constellation members can swap one ruleborn a week when both agree. No currency is involved.

Balance: quirks are small sidegrades, so a player's favourite form is never wrong.

11.11 The character bible

Players form real attachments to game characters, and those attachments go with enjoyment and time played; a survey of 213 players found seven distinct kinds, from admiring a character's competence to worrying about its wellbeing [124] [125]. Attachment is built from small, specific things — a look, a habit, a way of moving — so every ruleborn is specified rather than left to the artist or the balance sheet. Proportions follow the cuteness rules in 19.19; abilities follow the roles in 11.1.
