Rules that hold across all eighteen

Each has one habit you can watch at home. Idle behaviour is where attachment is built, not in combat.

Each resists exactly one law family, so crew choice against a scouted island is a real decision (12.4).

No ruleborn is strictly better than another. Levels raise numbers; they never turn a Scout into a Warden.

Each has a sound so a player can tell who acted without looking (21.8).

Each takes eight forms (11.10), and the form changes the look and one small quirk, never the role.

12Heists

A heist is a run of two and a half to four minutes into another player's universe: get in, open the Vault, get out before the way home closes. Nothing is destroyed for good, and the challenge is reading the laws, not out-muscling them.

12.1 Why raid

| Reward
| Why it matters

| Starlight and Essence
| About 40% of mid-game growth

| Pages of the laws you crossed
| The reason to raid. Pages restore and upgrade laws, a heist is worth two to three sessions of the Library's trickle, and what you get leans toward the school you raided (7.6)

| Insight
| Levels your Sages

| Renown
| Sets your league, your season rewards and your targets

| Strays
| Rare recruits with three traits

| Constellation progress
| Counts toward shared goals

12.2 The heist in six steps

1Find

2Scout

3Plan

4Cross

5Vault

6Escape

Steps 1 to 3: no time limitSteps 4 to 6: on the clock, 2.5 to 4 minutes

12.3 Finding a target

The Observatory offers 3 universes (5 at level 5) biased so the median heist is winnable (12.15): within 150 Renown of yours, within 3 Keeper levels, and within 20% of your universe's strength (stability capacity plus the levels of your five best characters).

Refreshing the list costs Aether: 50, doubling with each refresh in the same hour.

Echo universes: when fewer than three real universes fit, the list is topped up with echoes: snapshots of real universes from the last 14 days. Robbing an echo takes nothing from its owner; its loot is created by the game. This keeps matchmaking full while the player base is small.

Complexity budget: suggested targets respect the limits on unfamiliar laws in 7.8.

New players can't be raided by other players for their first 72 hours, or until they raid a player themselves.

Trace: within 24 hours of being robbed, a player can target the raider once, with a free scout and no entry fee.

12.4 Scouting

Free information: name, Keeper level, number of realms, Renown and a rough loot estimate. A paid scout (20 Aether per realm, less with Observatory levels) shows each realm's laws, the number and roles of its guards, and how many wards of each type it has, but not where they are. Ward positions come from a Lantern Wisp during the heist, the Archivist's Reveal, or the Cartographer's maps. A scout stays valid for 15 minutes.

12.5 Planning

| Choice
| Rules

| Entry
| Any edge tile of any outer realm (see 5.4), or a fault if the target is overloaded.

| Route
| A path of realms to the Heart and out again. The game proposes the shortest; the player can drag it through other realms.

| Crew
| A crew of 3 to 5: 3 characters, 4 from Keeper level 8, 5 from level 16. Resting characters can't go.

| Sage
| One, from Keeper level 2.

| Casts
| 1 slot, 2 from Keeper level 12. A cast brings one of your own unlocked laws into their universe: it covers a circle of 4 tiles for 10 seconds and costs 30 Aether per point of the law's base cost.

Heist clock = 150 seconds + 15 seconds × links on the shortest route from the landing realm to the Heart, up to 240 seconds. Haste makes the clock run 25% faster while the crew is in its realm.

12.6 Moment to moment

A heist should feel like a string of small, tense decisions: roughly one every 8 to 12 seconds, or 15 to 25 in a run. Each is about timing, routes or greed, never about tapping faster.

| Phase
| Typical time
| Decisions
| Tension comes from

| Entry
| 0 to 20 s
| Read the realm's law banner; get past the first guard
| The first vision cones

| Crossing
| 20 to 120 s
| Take or skip forks; break a pylon or not; when to use abilities and casts; whether to split
| Guard awareness meters and the realm's alarm

| Vault
| About 30 s
| Who cracks, who loads, when to stop loading
| Cracking is loud: every 5 seconds adds 100 suspicion to every guard in the Heart (12.17)

| Escape
| Last 30 to 60 s
| Where to call the skiff; whether to drop loot to run faster
| The clock turns red for its last 30 seconds

Camera: a fixed top-down view follows the crew. When the crew splits, it follows the group last touched while the other group's portraits show its health and danger. Crossing a link plays a one-second swoop to the next realm. Pinching zooms out to the whole realm, never the whole universe.

Always on screen: the clock, the realm's alarm meter, the vision cones and awareness of guards the crew can see, carried loot, and the Vault's progress.

Pylons: breaking one switches its law off for the rest of the heist and gives a page of it. It's often worth a detour.

The Vault: 1,500 health per Vault level; Breakers deal double damage to it. Once open, each Carrier loads 150 loot a second until full or until the exposed share is gone. Storehouses on the route open the same way.

Escape: call the skiff to any edge tile. It takes 5 seconds to reach an outer realm and 20 seconds to reach the Heart (5.4). Anyone still inside when the clock ends is caught and drops what it carries. Loot already carried out is always kept, and every law met joins the Codex whatever the outcome.

12.7 Controls

Never more than eight things to tap at once, each at least 9 mm across, which is roughly 48 points, and none in a screen corner (21.8).

| Control
| Tap
| Hold or drag

| Crew portraits (3 to 5)
| Fire the character's ability at the best target
| Drag onto the world to aim; drag onto a path at a fork to split the crew

| Sage (1)
| Open a small wheel with its three powers
| Drag a power onto the world to aim it

| Cast (1)
| Open a wheel with the player's one or two casts
| Drag to place the circle

| Get out (1)
| Call the skiff to the nearest edge and send everyone to it
| Hold to choose where the skiff comes

While the crew is split, a regroup button takes the place of "Get out". Controls can be mirrored for left-handed play.

12.8 Guards, stealth and fights

Fights must reward cleverness, not only bigger numbers. Guards are the defender's own ruleborn on Guardian Posts, and their role decides how they behave.

| Guard's role
| Behaviour
| Beaten by

| Scout
| Sees 8 tiles and raises the alarm 50% faster, but is weak in a fight
| Tall grass, Stillness, or a quick ambush

| Warden
| Tough and slow; holds chokepoints
| Going around, leaping, or freezing it

| Breaker
| Hits hard but sees only a short way
| Staying out of reach while a Warden holds it

| Carrier
| Fast; chases and follows loot trails
| Splitting, decoys, or dropping a rope bridge behind you

| Mender
| Heals other guards
| Knocking it out first

Logic: rules that let clever beat strong

Sight and awareness: specified once, in 12.17. Three vision zones inside a cone, a suspicion meter that fills by zone, six states. The older single-cone and percent-per-second rules are gone; where any other section still describes perception, 12.17 wins.

Ambush: the first hit on a guard that has not noticed the crew stuns it for 2 seconds and deals triple Power, or half the guard's full health, whichever is greater. The floor exists because triple Power alone delivers the rule below for only three of twenty-five role pairings — a Mender would need 68 hits. Two ambush hits now knock out a guard of equal level in every pairing, which is what the rule was always for.

Wind-ups: every guard attack shows a 0.8-second wind-up. A stun or freeze during it cancels the attack and dazes the guard for a second.

Level cap in fights: a level advantage counts for at most 3 levels, so a well-played crew can rob a stronger universe and a careless strong crew can still be caught.

Knockouts: a crew member at zero health is caught unless revived. A knocked-out guard stays down unless a law brings it back.

12.9 Outcomes

What each outcome multiplies. The haul itself is computed in 9.6; this table no longer carries a second, competing account of it.

| Outcome
| Condition
| Haul
| Renown
| Rest
| Entry fee

| Clean
| No alarm; everyone out with loot
| ×1.0
| ×1.0
| 45 min
| Returned

| Loud
| Alarm raised; everyone out
| ×0.7
| ×0.8
| 60 min
| Returned

| Partial
| Some caught; some loot out
| ×0.4
| ×0.5
| 60 min, 180 for caught
| Half returned

| Foiled
| No loot out
| ×0
| −8, flat
| 180 min
| Paid to defender

Renown for a clean heist = 20 × (1 + (defender's Renown − raider's Renown) ÷ 1,000), kept between 8 and 40, then multiplied by the outcome above. The defender gains or loses the mirror amount. A foiled heist costs the raider a flat 8 rather than a share of a gain that was never made.

12.10 Heists against the game

Drifting ruins: three universes built by the game each day, for pages and Essence but no Renown. They carry the tutorial and give new players safe practice with unfamiliar laws.

Echo trials: weekly puzzle heists with a fixed crew and set laws, scored on style (clean, fast, untouched). They reward Insight and cosmetics.

12.11 Heists with friends

Friendly heists (at launch): rob a friend's island for fun. The friend loses nothing, both see the replay, and the friend earns a page for every raider their guards catch.

Co-op heists (version 1.1), asynchronous first: the first version is turn-based, reusing the deterministic simulation at almost no cost; a real-time version is built only if playtests show the asynchronous one is not enough (26.5). In either form, two or three friends raid one universe together, sharing one clock and splitting the loot evenly. Crews are interdependent: one player's ruleborn can break pylons but can't carry loot, another can carry but not fight, a third can cast but not climb. Cooperation and interdependence each raise enjoyment and trust on their own, and together they add up [67]. Players aged 13 to 15 use preset pings only.

Why: players in tight-knit groups stay the longest [34], and social ties matter most for long-term retention [20].

12.12 Choice, novelty and suspense

Version 0.4 planned ruins that quietly adjusted their difficulty. Three experiments across more than 20,000 play sessions found that difficulty itself did little for motivation, while choice of difficulty, novelty and suspense each raised it [43]. Difficulty is therefore no longer something the game does to the player behind their back.

| Lever
| How every heist uses it

| Choice
| Every ruin offers three openly labelled difficulties with their rewards shown, and a recommendation the player can ignore. Targets are chosen by the player from a list that spans easier and harder universes.

| Novelty
| Every run contains at least one thing the player hasn't seen: an unfamiliar law, a new terrain feature, a harmony, a visiting Sage, a weather mood. The game tracks what each player has met and draws from what they haven't.

| Suspense
| Outcomes stay uncertain until late: the Vault's contents are only revealed when it opens, guards' patrols shift slightly each run, and the last 30 seconds put the clock and the escape in doubt.

What adaptation is still allowed: a safety net, never a dial. After two failures in a row the game offers an easier version, visibly and by name. It never silently makes a chosen difficulty easier or harder.

Heists against players are never adjusted for engagement at all (19.12).

12.13 Why it went that way

How players explain an outcome shapes what they feel and whether they try again [68], so every heist result names the moment that decided it and what could have been done instead.

The decisive moment: "Your Carrier was spotted crossing the open ledge at 0:48" with a three-second replay of it.

One thing to try: a single suggestion tied to that moment, never a list.

Never blame luck, and never take credit for it: if a fault or a flicker decided the run, the result says so plainly.

Never blame the player's spending: no result ever suggests a purchase would have changed the outcome.

In co-op, results are shown for the crew as a whole. No player is ever singled out as the cause of a failure.

12.14 Temporary failure, never perpetual

Failure is part of the pleasure of succeeding, but only while it stays temporary; repeated failure with no way through is what makes players leave [81]. Disengagement follows when a game repeatedly frustrates a player's sense of control or competence and they revise their expectations of it [82].

The three-strike rule: nobody fails the same content three times without the game visibly changing something — an easier version by name, a new route revealed, or a different target suggested.

Say it out loud: any assistance is disclosed. Telling players they are being helped did not harm their experience in a controlled study; equalised performance mattered more than knowing about the help [83].

Never take control away: stun-like effects that remove control frustrate autonomy [84], so no law freezes a crew for more than 2 seconds, and the player can always still call the skiff.

Struggle is designed, defeat is not: a foiled heist still returns the crew, the pages and the Codex entries. The loss is the haul, never the progress.

12.15 Why these targets are offered

Version 1.4 aimed for even matches. Six million matches from 262,000 players say that is the wrong target: churn rose when players met stronger opponents, and being matched with weaker ones reduced churn more than fair matches did [146]. A short-term win rate near 50% is what satisfies [147] — players want to win slightly more than they lose.

The median offered target is winnable at the player's chosen difficulty. Harder targets are always in the list, clearly marked, never forced.

Nothing is hidden. Targets are drawn by Renown band and the player's own choice, and the reason each was offered is printed on the card (19.12).

Ratings are internal. Skill and island difficulty use a Bayesian rating with an explicit uncertainty term; the raw numbers are never shown, because visible ratings invite target-shopping.

A new island starts wide. Its difficulty rating begins uncertain and narrows as it is attacked, so early raiders are not punished for being first.

12.16 Guards say what they are thinking

In two controlled studies of top-down stealth, contextual guard dialogue let a simple AI match a complex one for player enjoyment [148]. Telegraphing buys as much perceived intelligence as expensive behaviour, at a fraction of the cost.

Every guard state change is spoken: "was that the wind?", "someone's been here", "back to the post".

Every attack keeps its 0.8-second wind-up (12.8), so the player always has a window to act.

Near misses are never tuned. They raise arousal and the urge to continue but are experienced negatively, through a wanting-without-liking mechanism linked to problem gambling [149]. They will happen naturally; we do not engineer their frequency (19.12).

12.17 How guards perceive

The most important system in the game, and until now the least specified. Every number is an integer, because the simulation contains no decimals (24.7), and nothing here is random: a player who scouts an island can predict exactly what will happen. That is required anyway, since the server re-simulates the result.

What each guard can see

Vision is not one cone but three zones inside a cone, plus weak peripheral awareness — the model shipped stealth games converge on, because a single cone gives absurd long-range vision or blind close range [215].

| Guard
| Cone
| Near
| Mid
| Far
| Corner of the eye
| Turn

| Scout
| 45°
| 0–2 m
| 2–4 m
| 4–6 m
| 110°
| 6°/tick

| Warden
| 40°
| 0–1.5 m
| 1.5–3 m
| 3–4.5 m
| 90°
| 3°/tick

| Breaker
| 35°
| 0–1.5 m
| 1.5–2.5 m
| 2.5–3.5 m
| 70°
| 8°/tick

| Carrier
| 30°
| 0–1 m
| 1–2 m
| 2–3 m
| 60°
| 4°/tick

| Mender
| 40°
| 0–1.5 m
| 1.5–2.5 m
| none
| 100°
| 4°/tick

A guard standing a level higher sees a metre further; one standing below loses its far zone entirely. Being crouched halves how fast you are noticed, running raises it by three quarters, an unlit tile halves it again, and standing still in tall grass makes you invisible until a guard walks into the tile.

Being noticed takes time

Each guard carries a suspicion value from 0 to 1000. Inside the near zone it fills in about half a second, the middle zone in about a second, the far zone in just under three. It empties more slowly than it fills — about 1.7 seconds — which is what creates the tail after a close call. A state cannot fall back for half a second once reached, so nothing flickers at the edge of a zone.

| State
| At
| What the guard does

| Unaware
| under 200
| Patrols or holds its post

| Suspicious
| 200 to 599
| Stops, turns toward it, stays on route

| Investigating
| 600, or on hearing something
| Walks to the spot and searches nearby for ten seconds

| Alerted
| 1000
| Calls out, converges, raises the alarm. Never lethal — the crew is knocked out and sent home

| Searching
| After losing sight
| Twenty seconds of wider, faster searching

| Returning
| After giving up
| Walks home, still easily re-alerted

Alarm spreads by distance, not everywhere at once: guards within four metres start investigating half a second later. The realm alarm itself climbs from 0 to 6 — once every thirty seconds and once per knocked-out guard discovered — and at 5 another Warden arrives at an entrance. Rising pressure with no dice in it.

Noise travels the way a crew walks

Sound follows the walkable path rather than passing through walls, so a wall genuinely muffles direction: a guard turns toward the way around, not toward you [216].

| Action
| Heard within
| Action
| Heard within

| Crawling
| silent
| Landing from one level
| 2 m

| Crouched walk
| 1 m
| Landing from three
| 4 m

| Walking
| 2.5 m
| Breaking something
| 5 m

| Running
| 4.5 m
| Gravel and water are louder, moss is quieter (6.1)

Guards far off screen hear at half strength, so nothing is lost to something the player could not have seen [215].

12.18 How a crew moves, and what it decides

The document specified what guards see long before it specified how a crew walks. This settles it, because everything about whether delegated control feels like playing rests here.

| Question
| Answer

| Tiles or free movement?
| Both, deliberately. Terrain, laws and building are per tile; position and facing are continuous, in millitiles. That is the only reading under which one-metre tiles and sub-metre vision zones are both true

| How is a move ordered?
| Tap a crew member to select, tap the ground to send. Tap two to move them together. A held tap on a wall, ledge or vent offers the action that fits

| Pathfinding
| Breadth-first on the tile graph with a fixed neighbour order, ties broken by tile index. Deterministic by construction (24.10)

| What is recorded
| The command, never the path. A destination and a tick, so the same route is recomputed identically on the server

| Crew avoiding each other
| They pass through one another. Collision between friends is a source of non-determinism and of the exact frustration this rule exists to prevent

The crew never makes a decision the player did not make

No auto-attack, no auto-flee, no automatic re-routing around a guard the player walked them into, no picking a different door. If a crew member is spotted it keeps doing what it was told until told otherwise. A creature cannot look stupid for a decision it never made — and the failure this protects against, delegated control reading as watching rather than playing, is a named risk (30.7).

12.19 The Vault, and when to stop loading

12.6 called the loading phase the heist's central decision, and its own numbers gave that decision two seconds and one option. Both are fixed here.

Every role can load, at 30 units a second rather than only Carriers at 150. A full crew fills in about ten seconds, which is one decision at the eight-to-twelve second cadence 12.6 sets for itself. The rate scales with Carry as characters level, so ten seconds holds all game.

What you carry is what you bank. The haul in 9.6 is multiplied by the share of the crew's bags actually filled, so loading longer earns more and the alarm clock is the price. Dropping loot to run faster costs exactly the fraction dropped.

The break-even sits at about 70% full — at that point the extra load is worth exactly what a loud exit costs. That single number is the dial for the game's emotional peak.

Cracking is 10 to 25 seconds of pressure with no decision in it, which is correct: it is the wind-up, not the choice.

12.20 What happens when a guard catches you

| Rule
| Detail

| Attack cadence
| One hit every 0.8 seconds for damage equal to Power, on both sides. This is the only cadence the document states, and it is the reading under which Vault health, role Power and the phase lengths in 12.6 agree

| May a crew fight?
| Yes, at any moment — but hitting a guard that has not been ambushed sends its suspicion straight to 1000. An open fight is an alarm with a price, not a forbidden move

| A crew member at zero health
| Drops its loot on the tile and has ten seconds before capture. An ally standing adjacent revives it in 3 seconds, a Mender in 1.5

| Captured
| Sent home, rested longer, and its share of the haul lost. Never killed, never lost permanently (12.14)

12.21 Attributes laws are allowed to read

Three laws read properties no creature had — Density changes what "big" and "small" creatures do, and Heavy ground grounds "flyers". A law that reads a parameter which does not exist is the same failure as a law full of special cases, seen from the other side. Both attributes are now carried by the roster table itself rather than asserted in prose, and both are checked by the build.

Size is a band on every ruleborn — small, standard or large — and it is derived, not assigned: the head count already given on each character card in 11.11 decides it. Under 2.0 heads is small, 2.0 to 2.5 standard, above 2.5 large. That yields 4 small, 10 standard and 4 large, and every large one is a Warden, which is why Density reads as a Warden rule in play. The band is now a column in the roster table in 11.2, so a law can read it, and the build re-runs the derivation: change a character's proportions and its size changes with them, or the check fails (31.4).

Flight is a column in the same table, stated for all 18 rather than implied by one creature's passive line. Three fly: Featherkin, Lantern Wisp and Soulmender. Heavy ground grounds exactly those three, and nothing else in the document has to be read to know it.

Split guards created by Swarm birth inherit the role of the guard they came from, at 30% of its health and power, so perception has something to resolve against.

The rule: every tier effect of every law must be expressible as a change to a named parameter. Anything that cannot be is a design smell to resolve while authoring, not at implementation.

12.22 How a target is chosen

| Step
| Rule

| 1. The band
| Renown within 150, Keeper level within 3, and an island difficulty rating within one band of the raider's recent success rate

| 2. The rating
| Skill and island difficulty are Bayesian, with an uncertainty term. A new island starts wide and narrows as it is attacked. Renown and Keeper level are filters; the rating orders what survives them

| 3. The guarantee
| The list is built so the median offered target is winnable at the player's chosen difficulty: candidates are sorted by predicted success and the middle of the list is drawn from the 55 to 70% band (13.2)

| 4. When nothing matches
| The bands widen in order — rating, then Keeper level, then Renown — and if still nothing, generated islands and ruins fill the list (13.3). The list is never empty and never silently unfair

| 5. The lowest tier
| The 0.25 payout for targets three or more levels below is unreachable through normal matchmaking by design. It exists for challenge links, friendly heists and Trace targets

13Defence

Defence is designed, not built. There are no walls to spam and no towers to level: the defender chooses laws, a map, a few wards and who stands guard.

Five layers of roughly comparable weight. No layer decides an outcome on its own, and the share beside each is the design target, not a measurement.

| Layer
| Share
| How it defends

| Geometry
| 25%
| Where the Vault sits, which routes exist, what can be seen from where, chutes that force a choice, bridges that drop on alarm. Free, unlimited, and the layer a thoughtful designer beats a richer player with

| Guards
| 20%
| Your own ruleborn on posts. Anyone guarding is someone not raiding — the single decision that links the two halves of the game. Guards who have been home a while are rested and fight better, so the choice is a trade rather than a loss [212]

| Wards
| 20%
| Three to seven per realm, placed where the laws make them strongest and where the geometry says a crew must pass

| Laws
| 20%
| The flavour and the puzzle: a Heart running Heavy ground and Haunting makes every loaded carrier slow and easy to follow. One layer among five, not the defence itself

| Response
| 15%
| Bells, alarms, the Spire's reach, and the guardian Sage's single power. What turns being seen into being caught

Roughly 60% of a defence's strength should come from how it was designed and 40% from what has been built, so a clever player at a lower level can still turn back a richer raider. The published design of the base builders this borrows from makes the same point: no single element stops an attack — walls funnel, traps punish the route, and the defenders held in reserve are the hidden win condition [213].

Logic: snapshots
A heist is played against a snapshot of the defender's universe taken when it starts: laws, map, wards, and whoever is on guard. Changes the defender makes during those minutes don't affect it. The defender is notified afterwards and can watch the replay.

Defending pays

A foiled heist gives the defender the raider's entry fee and the mirror of the Renown the raider loses.

If the raider cast a law during a foiled heist, the defender gets one page of it.

Knocked-out raiders leave behind whatever they were carrying.

Rehearsals

Defence is designed, so defenders need a way to test their design. A rehearsal sends one of three simulated crews (cautious, greedy or fast) against the universe as it stands and shows the route it took, where it was seen and what it carried out. Rehearsals are free and unlimited, give no rewards, and run on the same simulation as real heists.

Watching live (after launch)

In version 1.1, a player who is in the game when raided can watch the heist live and fire their guardian Sage's power once.

Why fairness rules exist
A defence that can't be beaten makes raiding pointless, and a heist that can't be stopped makes building pointless. The route rule (there's always a way in), the scouting rules (laws are always visible before you commit) and the loss caps keep both sides worth playing.

13.1 Making sure islands get built at all

The riskiest assumption in the whole design
Ruleborn assumes players will design islands as a defence. Every comparable game says they will not. In asymmetric games the population piles into one role — one game had to offer a 400% reward bonus to fill the less popular side [150] — and in base builders most players copy a layout rather than design one, which is what the enormous copy-a-base ecosystem exists to serve. Creative communities run at roughly 1% creating and 9% editing [73]. If this holds here, the Workshop starves, the human presence that makes heists matter disappears, and the content ecosystem collapses.

| Mitigation
| Detail

| Everyone builds once, briefly
| Before the first heist against a player, a 60-second guided build: place three terrain features, one guard and one law. It is short, it is not optional, and it is the moment most players discover they enjoy it.

| Seeded islands
| The target pool and the ruins are stocked with designed islands from the start, so raiders never run dry while the player base is thin.

| Defenders are paid best
| The largest non-cosmetic rewards in the game go to islands other people choose to play: defence rewards, creator marks, and the fund (18.9).

| Design is a two-minute act, not an afternoon
| Terrain and buildings are instant and free to move (6.1, 10.1), so redesigning is cheap. One-tap layouts exist for players who do not want to design; they are deliberately weaker than a designed island.

| It is measured weekly
| Share of active players who changed their defence in the last 7 days, and who published an island in their first week. Below 15 to 20% publishing, the loop is under-supplied and the mitigations escalate (25.3).

13.2 What a fair defence looks like

Because theft is one-way and a defender loses nothing, this game can afford a far higher rate of successful defence than a base builder where losing hurts — and it should, because that is what makes designing an island worth doing.

| Target
| Value
| Why

| Raids that succeed
| 55 to 70%
| Most raids should pay, or raiding dies. A third to a half of defences holding is what gives defenders something to be proud of

| Defences that hold
| 30 to 45%
| Below a quarter, defenders feel helpless and stop designing. Above half, raiding feels futile

| From design rather than level
| About 60%
| A well-built island beats a richer one often enough to matter

| Any single layer
| No more than 25%
| If one layer decides outcomes, the other four are decoration

The health signal is the shape of the outcomes, not the average. A game where raids either sweep or fail completely, with nothing in between, is a polarised one even if the mean looks right; the developers of the largest base builder treat exactly that compression as the warning sign [213]. We watch the spread of clean runs, partial hauls and foiled raids, and treat a populated middle as the goal. Comparable games are far more attacker-sided than this — one runs at around 98% successful attacks [214] — but they are punishing games, and this one is not.

13.3 Nobody has to build

The riskiest assumption in this design was that players would author islands. It was to be tested on twenty people. Since that test cannot be run, the assumption is removed instead of measured.

| Rule
| What it means

| Every player has a defence from the first minute
| Generated from their realm, their laws and their ruleborn — legal, coherent, and never empty. Nobody is ever a blank island in someone else's target list

| Curation is the default creative act
| Swap a law, move a post, pick a different generated layout, name it. Editing is a far commoner behaviour than authoring, and it is where most players will live [73]. Asking people whether they would build was never going to settle it: across thousands of participants the gap between intending and doing runs near 46% [218], and shifting an intention moves behaviour only half as far [219] [73]

| Authoring is the deep end, not the entry
| The full builder is there for anyone who wants it, and the creator ladder in 18.7 rewards what other people choose to play. It is a wing of the game, not its foundation — and the ladder rests on recognition, the lever with a measured effect on whether anyone contributes [220]

| The Workshop is stocked either way
| Generated islands, seeded designs and published ones share one pool. A raider never runs dry, whatever share of players build

Why this is not a retreat. Every other system survives untouched: laws still define an island, defences still hold or fall, the ladder still exists, the ruins still fill. What changes is where the first draft comes from — and a game that works when 1% author is a better game than one that needs 30% to.

If soft launch shows people authoring more than expected, nothing has to change: the builder is already there, and the ladder already rewards it. The design is now robust to the answer rather than dependent on it.

13.4 Budgets, and how a layer's share is measured

13.2 sets targets for how much each layer should contribute. Targets without a mechanism are decoration, so here is both: what a defender may spend, and how the shares are actually measured.

What an island may hold

A realm has a stability pool that grows with its level, and each layer has a slot limit as well as a price. The limits matter more than the pool: they are what makes it impossible to pour everything into one layer, which is how the "no layer above a quarter" rule is enforced rather than merely hoped for [226].

One set of limits, drawn against the universe's stability pool (5.5). The older per-realm pool is gone, and these numbers now match 5.2 and 10.2 rather than competing with them.

| Realm level
| Guardian Posts
| Wards
| Law slots
| Prices, in stability

| 1
| 1
| 4
| 1
| Laws 10 to 26 by rank (7.4); wards and posts are built with Starlight (10.2) and hold stability only while staffed

| 3
| 2
| 6
| 3

| 5
| 3
| 8
| 5

One ruleborn stands at one Guardian Post. That was never stated anywhere, and it is the missing conversion between counting posts and counting guards — without it the two tables could not be reconciled and defence could have consumed more characters than production. Wards follow the rule already in 5.2: three plus the realm level.

Geometry costs nothing and is unlimited, which is deliberate: it is the layer a thoughtful designer beats a richer one with. Response is earned rather than bought.

What "a layer's share" actually means

It means this, exactly: run the same thousand raids against the island with that layer switched off, and see how much the hold rate falls. Do it for every combination of the five layers — there are only thirty-two — and divide the credit the way cooperative game theory does, which is the standard method for attributing an outcome to the parts that produced it and is already used to attribute win rates in live games [227].

| Question
| How it is answered

| How much does each layer contribute?
| All 32 combinations, 400 simulated raids each — about 12,800 runs, which a deterministic simulation does in seconds. That gives each share to within about five points [228]

| Is design beating progression?
| Split each layer's levers into those any player can use — placement, choice, pattern — and those unlocked by level, then attribute across the two groups. The target is 60% from design (13.2)

| Has a change helped?
| About 1,600 raids per version to detect a five-point shift in hold rate with confidence. Anything smaller is noise

| What is held constant?
| A frozen set of attacking crews, seeds and target levels, versioned like code, so results stay comparable between builds

The targets in 13.2 therefore stop being aspirations and become a test that either passes or fails on every build.

14Progression and pacing

How long everything takes, for three kinds of player. Every timing in this section comes from the economy model (24), which simulates each kind of player day by day using the costs and production rates in 9 and 10.

14.1 Three kinds of player

| Player
| Sessions a day
| Heists a day
| Minutes a day
| Lab experiments a day

| Engaged
| 5
| 4
| About 40
| 3

| Regular
| 3
| 2
| About 20
| 2

| Casual
| 1
| 1
| About 8
| 1

Logic: what the model assumes

Producing buildings hold 12 hours of output, so one session a day collects half of what they make.

Heist success follows the arc in 14.5 (about 80% at first, 60% after a month), and a successful heist brings about a fifth of a day's production.

Players save for the next Spire level first, then buy the cheapest available upgrade; producers come before everything else.

Pages and Insight arrive as described in 7.6 and 11.7; births and levels are bought whenever Essence allows.

14.2 Keeper levels

Keeper experience comes from successful heists (30 + 6 × Keeper level), foiled heists (10 + 2 × level), spending (1 for every 40 Starlight) and restoring laws (60 + 10 × level). The experience table is set so an engaged player follows a target curve: day ≈ 0.13 × level1.96, which means level 8 in a week, 16 in a month, 28 in three months and 40 in six.

Engaged players reach Keeper level 16 on day 30, 28 on day 90 and 40 on day 181, and have every building at its highest level around day 377, about a year in.

Regular players reach 16 on day 64, 28 on day 160 and 40 on day 274, and finish around day 505, about 17 months in.

Casual players reach 16 on day 135, 28 on day 333 and 40 on day 556, and finish around day 1,019, almost three years in. Whether that is too long is an open decision (27.3).

The pacing gate: for engaged players each Spire level arrives within a few days of the Keeper level that allows it. From Spire level 8, Starlight becomes the limit, so saving becomes part of the late game.

Laws: an engaged player owns about 10 laws after a week, 21 after a month, 36 by around day 82 and all 48 by about day 234.

14.4 The page economy

| Source
| Pages

| Studying a law after a heist
| 2, or 3 if clean

| Meeting a law for the first time
| 1

| Breaking a pylon
| 1

| A foiled heist
| 1

| A Lab experiment that finds nothing
| 3

| Daily goals
| About 2

| Constellation gifts
| Up to 5 a day

| The Library (10.4)
| 3 a day at level 1, 9 at level 5, 18 at level 10

An engaged player earns about 11 pages a day from play, plus the Library: about 16 a day over the first 60 days and about 23 a day after

A player who never raids earns the Library alone, which is the floor the whole design rests on (13.3)

The full Codex at tier I: 18 common × 10 + 12 uncommon × 20 + 6 rare × 35 + 12 deep × 60 = 1,350 pages in total

Restoring it, less the three commons the player starts with: 1,350 − 30 = 1,320 pages

Engaged: 60 days × 16 = 960, then 360 ÷ 23 = 16 more — about 76 days

Never raids: the Library alone, averaging about 6 a day while it is levelled out of production income — about 220 days

Tiers II and III for the 22 laws a finished universe can weave at once: 22 × 60 = 1,320 pages more, about 133 days in all

The Codex therefore fills well before the economy does: laws are the fastest axis of progression, and the late game is about weaving them, not acquiring them

14.5 The first 60 days

Habits typically take around two months to form (see 19), so the first 60 days are designed as one arc rather than as a tutorial followed by a grind.

| Period
| What the player gets
| Heist success

| Days 1 to 3
| Ruins that teach one law at a time; the first outer realm; the first stray; a guided first harmony
| About 80%

| Days 4 to 14
| First heists against players; the first defence and rehearsal; a suggested constellation; the Tinkerer
| About 75%

| Days 15 to 30
| Second and third realms; tier II laws; the first anomaly; the Gardener
| About 70%

| Days 31 to 60
| League placement; mastery tracks; the first paradox hint; the Wanderer
| 55 to 65%

Success is tuned through matchmaking: newer players are offered targets slightly below their strength, and harder targets are always available but never forced.

14.6 Daily rhythm

Daily goals: the player picks 3 of 6 offered goals, such as "cross a Time realm" or "birth a ruleborn", with one free swap. Rewards are small and shown as progress toward something the player chose.

No reward for logging in. Surprises replace it: any heist has about a one-in-eight chance of a stray or a bonus page, with the odds shown in the Codex.

Weekly streak: playing on 4 days out of 7 keeps it going. One automatic saver a week covers a missed week, and a broken streak is repaired by playing two days in a row. Streak rewards are cosmetic.

Notifications: only for things the player can act on (storage full, a character rested, an experiment finished, a heist against you). Players choose a daily reminder time, or none, and nothing is sent at night (see 20.2).

Nothing decays while you're away. Returning players get extra pages on their next five heists.

Design targets for the Renown formula in 12.9.

| League

14.8 Progress that feels close

People work harder as a goal gets nearer, and a goal that starts partly filled is reached faster than an identical one that starts empty: café customers given a 12-stamp card with two stamps already on it finished their ten purchases faster than customers given a plain 10-stamp card [44], and a car-wash field experiment found the same for artificial early progress [45]. Ruleborn uses this to make goals feel reachable, never to push spending.

| Rule
| In practice

| Start partly filled
| The first law album opens with 3 of its 12 laws already found; the season track's first tier is complete on day one; a new Forms album starts with the form the player already owns.

| Always show what's left
| Every album, law, level and track shows "2 pages to go", not "83% complete".

| Nearest goal first
| The home screen surfaces whichever goal is closest to done, whatever kind it is.

| Never for money
| Progress bars never appear in the shop, and no purchase ever fills one. This is a pacing tool, not a sales tool.

| Re-engage after a win
| In the same research, effort dipped after each reward, so a finished album immediately opens the next one with a piece of it already found.

14.9 What the player is ultimately playing for

Everything in 14.1 to 14.8 describes how fast things arrive. This section answers the harder question: when all of it has arrived, what is the player still here for? A progression system without an answer to that is a countdown to quitting.

Why accumulation cannot be the answer

| Finding
| Evidence
| Consequence

| Rewards given for playing reduce later interest in the activity; positive feedback about competence raises it.
| Meta-analysis of 128 experiments [3]
| A game whose endgame is "collect the last numbers" is training its own players to stop

| Points, levels and leaderboards raised output but left intrinsic motivation and competence unchanged.
| Controlled experiment [65]
| Keeper level 40 is information, not a destination

| Time played does not predict wellbeing; the perceived value of the time does.
| 38,935 players [5]; 703 players over 140,000 hours [6]
| The endgame is judged by whether a session felt worth it, not by how much is left to unlock

| Achievement predicts retention early; social ties predict it at the top level.
| Logs of 51,104 players [20]
| The late game has to become social or it ends

| Entertainment is sought for pleasure and for meaning, and the meaningful kind — feeling moved, reflective, that something mattered — is a distinct experience, not just stronger fun.
| Motivation studies and a scoping review of the concept in games [126] [127]
| Ruleborn's endgame is designed for appreciation, not only enjoyment

| Specific, difficult goals produce more effort and persistence than vague or easy ones, across roughly a thousand studies; commitment and feedback are the conditions that make them work.
| 35-year synthesis [128]
| Endgame goals must be named, hard and measurable — not "keep playing"

The three sources of fulfilment

Ruleborn's answer is that the player is building three things that outlast any number: a place, a reputation, and a body of work.

| Source
| What the player actually has
| Why it holds
| Where it lives

| 1. A place that is unmistakably theirs
| A sky of seven islands, shaped tile by tile, running a set of laws nobody else chose, populated by creatures they named and raised
| People value what they built themselves, but only when they finish it [49]; identification with characters raises autonomy, immersion and enjoyment [113]
| The universe, the terrain editor, the roster, forms (5, 6, 11)

| 2. A reputation among people who know what it took
| A name in a constellation, on islands other people failed to beat, on a defence others study. Self-referenced mastery markers come first; global ranking is secondary and optional
| Social ties are what predicts retention at the top [20] [34]; competition inside cooperation is the combination the evidence supports [13]
| Constellations, leagues, defence challenges, friends (17, 18)

| 3. A body of work other people play
| Published islands with their name on them, completion counts, a place in the Drifting ruins that new players meet without knowing whose it is
| Creation is the deepest form of ownership [49]; the best-selling game ever is a creation game [38]
| The Island Workshop and the creator ladder (18.6, 18.7)

The honest status of this thesis
The place/reputation/body-of-work answer is the best-supported one available, but one link in it is not proven: no study shows that a meaningful or appreciated experience predicts continued play. The authors of the main survey of eudaimonic game motives say outright that they could not test whether these motives predict actual game use, and validated scales for them are almost absent [129] [127]. A large two-wave panel found eudaimonic preferences predicted later meaning in life only negligibly [130]. So 14.9 is written as a hypothesis with a test attached (14.10), not as a mechanism we are relying on.

The one-sentence test
A year in, a player should be able to answer "what have you got out of it?" with something other than a number. If the honest answer is "I'm level 40", the endgame has failed. The intended answers are: "I built a sky that works", "I'm someone in my constellation", and "people play my islands".

What the economy does after month six

The curves in 14.2 and 14.3 are deliberately front-loaded, so accumulation finishes while the other three things are still growing. The shape of the change:

| Phase
| What the player is doing
| What the economy is for
| What fulfils them

| Days 1 to 30
| Opening realms, meeting laws, raising the first crew
| Growth: almost every resource turns into a new thing
| Competence — visible progress every session

| Months 2 to 4
| Combining laws, shaping terrain, defending, joining a constellation
| Choice: resources fund alternatives, not just more
| Autonomy — the sky starts to look like a decision

| Months 5 to 12
| Mastery tracks, chords and paradoxes, leagues, publishing islands
| Expression: resources fund experiments and rebuilds, not new tiers
| Reputation and craft

| Year 2 and after
| Epochs, seasonal themes, teaching newer players, building for the Workshop
| Renewal: an epoch resets the numbers and keeps the collection
| A body of work and a place in a community

The late economy is not a drip. Once everything is maxed, surplus Starlight converts into Workshop materials and rebuild credits, so a veteran can tear down and rebuild an island in an afternoon without losing progress. Wealth becomes freedom to experiment rather than a slower bar.

No numbers are inflated to fake depth. There is no level 41, no tier IV, no second currency introduced at month six. When accumulation is over, it is over, and the game says so.

Heist gains change meaning, not size. Early they fund building; later they fund experiments, Workshop publishing and gifts. The formula in 9.4 does not change; what a haul is for does.

Epochs are the renewal valve (18.4), and they are optional. A player who never takes one loses nothing but the fresh start.

Endgame goals, written as goals

Specific and difficult beats vague and easy, with commitment and feedback as the conditions [128]. Every endgame pursuit is therefore a named, hard, measurable thing with a visible track — not an open-ended grind.

| Goal
| What it demands
| Typical time
| What it leaves behind

| Master a school
| Weave and cross every law of one school, at tier III, and win with each. Measured against your own past runs, not against other players
| 6 to 10 weeks each
| A title, and that school's laws get a visual style nobody else has

| Find every chord
| All 12, which needs three-note realms and a level 3 Lab
| 4 to 8 months
| The complete Codex and a Lab ornament

| Hold a Nova season
| Finish a season in the top 10% and defend it the next
| Two seasons
| A permanent banner on your islands

| Be published
| An island featured in the weekly spotlight
| Weeks to never
| Architect, and your island in the ruins with your name on it

| Raise a full set of forms
| All 8 forms of one ruleborn, which means building islands to hatch them
| 3 to 6 months
| An outfit, and proof your sky was designed on purpose

| Take an epoch
| Rebuild from nothing keeping your laws and Sages
| About 80 days of entropy first
| +8% stability capacity, and a third crown slot at the second epoch

What we will not do to extend the endgame

No level cap raises, no tier IV, no new currency at month six.

No power sold. The shop stays cosmetic whatever the retention numbers say (19.12).

No obligation: missing a season, an anomaly or a week costs nothing.

No engagement-tuned matchmaking to keep veterans winning [50].

If a veteran's honest answer is that they are done, the game should let them leave well. A player who stops at month eight having built something they are proud of is a success, not churn.

14.10 How the endgame is measured, and when we kill a mechanic

Every claim in 14.9 is testable with validated instruments. None of it is judged on retention alone, because the strongest finding in this whole area is that hours played is not a measure of fulfilment and can indicate its opposite: players whose needs were poorly met played more hours, with lower enjoyment and more tension afterwards [2] [5] [6].

| What we measure
| Instrument
| Why

| Needs met and needs frustrated
| BANGS, the games-specific needs scale, which measures both directions [24]
| Frustration is a separate construct from low satisfaction and is what actually drives people out [82]

| Experience quality across 11 constructs
| PXI; the 11-item miniPXI for live use [56] [131]
| Meaning, mastery, autonomy and curiosity are tracked separately, not as one score

| Appreciation, separately from fun
| The 3-item appreciation scale: moved, thought-provoking, meaningful [129]
| Tests the 14.9 hypothesis directly rather than assuming it

| Harmonious versus obsessive passion
| The Passion Scale [132] [133]
| The two forms differ in harm at the same number of hours; one study of 597 long-term players found obsessive players keep playing while exhausted [134]

| Belonging and commitment in a constellation
| Investment Model Scale (satisfaction, investment, alternatives) [135]; the single-item closeness pictogram [136]
| Commitment predicts staying; investment alone predicts being trapped

Kill criteria: when a mechanic is removed regardless of its engagement numbers

Playtime up, enjoyment and energy down, tension up. That is the obsessive-passion signature, not success [2] [134].

Need frustration rises in a cohort on BANGS, even if satisfaction is unchanged [24] [82].

A season or event raises frustration without raising meaning or appreciation. The mechanic goes.

Players report returning out of obligation. Measured by one question: "did you come back today because you wanted to, or because you would have lost something?"

Falling activity is a welfare signal, not a re-hook trigger

Veterans do not leave cleanly; they fade over weeks, and one operator reported predicting departures two to three weeks ahead with high accuracy in a live game [137], though the method is not published. Ruleborn uses that signal in the opposite direction from the industry norm:

No win-back pressure. No escalating notifications, no "your islands miss you", no expiring comeback offers.

A legacy card instead. A fading player is shown what they built: islands published, laws restored, creatures raised, people who played their work.

Returning costs nothing. Nothing decayed, no streak lost, and a short "what changed while you were away" on return.

Leaving well is a success state. A player who stops at month eight proud of what they built is counted as a good outcome in our own reporting, not as churn to be recovered.

15What the player learns, and when

Ruleborn has more to understand than most mobile games, so learning is designed as carefully as the economy. The goal is that players never read a manual and never meet more than one new idea at a time, yet understand combinations of laws by month 2.

15.1 Principles

| Principle
| Why
| In practice

| Teach in context
| Tutorials helped only in a complex game, and teaching in context beat manuals [9]
| Every idea is taught the first time it matters, by doing it

| One new thing at a time
| Too many complex options overwhelm [11]
| The complexity budget in 7.8; ruins that introduce each law alone

| Succeed early
| Easier versions held players longer [7]
| About four heists in five succeed in the first days

| Curiosity before explanation
| Curiosity improves memory [10]
| Show the "?" and the strange movement first; explain after the player has noticed

| Feedback, not rewards
| Feedback builds interest; expected rewards wear it down [3]
| Replays that show where you were seen; one suggestion after each heist

| Practice without risk
| Players learn by experimenting
| Ruins, rehearsals and the Lab cost nothing to fail

| Fade guidance as skill grows
| Support that helps a novice can hold back someone who already knows [79]
| Every tip, banner and preview has a competence trigger that retires it, and can be switched back on

| Help when stuck, not on a schedule
| A help button reduced play in a simpler game [9]
| Hints appear only after repeated failure, and every hint is tested before release

15.2 The learning map

| What the player learns
| When (engaged player)
| Taught by
| Practised in
| Shown mastered when
| Taught again if

| Weaves a second law
| —

| Work and production

| Stealth: vision cones, tall grass, ambushes

| Caught three times: a tip about cones

| Realms and terrain

| —

| Choosing a target, scouting and landing
| Day 2

| Heists

| A crew blocked by a law twice: a suggestion

| Harmony
| Day 3
| The weaving screen shows a harmony before it's woven
