| Weaves a harmony
| —

| Crown laws and realm laws
| Day 3 (Spire level 2)
| The crown slot opens, with the scope picture in 5.1
| Home
| Weaves a crown law
| —

| Defence and rehearsals
| Days 4 to 7
| The first raid on you, its replay, and a suggested rehearsal
| Rehearsals
| Changes the defence after a replay
| Three heavy losses: a defence check-up

| Splitting the crew, casts and Sage powers
| Days 2 to 14, one per session
| A prompt the first time each would help
| Heists
| Uses each successfully
| —

| Constellations
| Day 4 (Keeper level 6)
| A suggested constellation and its Grand Law vote
| Constellation
| Votes or gifts pages
| —

| Signature resonances and the Lab
| Weeks 1 and 2
| Lab hints; crossing one in a heist
| The Lab
| First discovery
| —

| Dissonance and overloading
| Weeks 2 to 4
| A warning when weaving; the first fault
| Home
| Keeps strain in the band they chose
| Laws flicker in three defences: a tip

| Chords and paradoxes
| Months 1 to 3
| Lab hints and "unstable" warnings
| The Lab
| First chord
| —

15.3 Teaching tools

| Tool
| What it does
| Rules

| Law banner and card
| A two-second banner when entering a realm; a card with a looping preview
| Shown the first three times for each law, then only on request

| The Archivist's tips
| One short line of advice
| At most one per session; never blocks play; can be turned off

| Annotated replays
| Shows where the crew was seen and why
| Available after every heist and every defence

| One thing to try
| A single suggestion on the heist result
| Based on what went wrong, never a lecture

| Weaving preview
| Shows harmonies, clashes and the stability band before committing
| Always on

| Rehearsals and Echo trials
| Safe places to experiment
| Free and unlimited

| Codex notes
| What the player has seen each law do
| Filled in automatically

15.4 Measuring learning

Time to the first pylon break, first ambush, first harmony and first rehearsal.

Share of heists where the crew resists or counters at least one of the target's laws.

Where players stop playing in the first week, matched against the learning map.

A/B tests of every hint and tip before global launch.

15.5 Making it stick

Practice spread over time beats practice crammed into one session: spaced retrieval practice beat massed retrieval with a large effect (g = 0.74) [46], and recalling something beats re-reading it in most reviews, with a medium effect (g ≈ 0.50) [47], though a recent meta-analysis of mathematics learning found the recall advantage there was small and not robust [48]. So Ruleborn spaces its teaching and asks players to recall rather than re-read, while keeping both changes cheap enough to be worth it even if the effect is modest.

| Rule
| In practice

| Every law returns
| After a law is first taught, the game tries to place it in the player's runs again after about 1 day, 3 days, a week and a month, by weighting matchmaking and ruins toward realms that run it.

| Recall before reveal
| In the scout report, a law the player has met is shown by its icon and movement first, with a one-tap "what does this do?" rather than an automatic explanation.

| Use, not quiz
| The recall is always part of play — choosing a crew against a law, predicting a harmony — never a test screen.

| Space the new
| No more than one new law introduced per session in the first two weeks, even when the player is unlocking quickly.

15.6 One new word at a time

Ruleborn asks players to learn laws, notes, schools, chords, resonances, ruleborn and realms, plus six school names, eight note names and seven realm names. This is learnable because the words are repurposed English rather than invented — a player already has a schema for notes and chords — but only if they arrive one at a time. Density, not any single word, is the risk.

One new term per teaching beat, never a glossary.

Names are chosen to sound like what they do. The bouba-and-kiki effect holds across 25 languages and 976 participants [173]: soft, round sounds read as soft things. Hush and Veil are soft; Spark and Echo are sharp. Every new name is checked against its meaning and said aloud in all twelve launch languages (19.20).

Comprehension is a gate. If fewer than 70% of testers can define chord, resonance and ruleborn after the tutorial, the vocabulary is cut or re-staged, not defended (25.3).

16Moment zero: the first session

The first ten minutes must teach the idea that laws change everything, and end with a reason to come back. No text walls: every lesson is something the player does.

The first heists are tuned to succeed about four times in five (see 14.5), and each one teaches a single law.

| Time
| What happens
| What it teaches

| 0:00
| A shattered universe drifts in the dark. The Archivist catches a falling glowing page: Half gravity.
| Laws are things you find

| 0:30
| The player places it in their Heart. Stones lift, and a character leaps over a hedge.
| A law changes the world you see

| 1:15
| Three ruleborn wake: a Featherkin, an Emberling and a Tidewalker. Each shows its ability once.
| Every character has a job it does best

| 2:00
| First heist, into a Drifting ruin that runs Slow rivers. The Tidewalker crosses the water easily, the Emberling breaks the pylon and the river stops slowing everyone, the Vault opens, and the crew escapes with seconds left.
| Read the laws, use the right character, break pylons, get out in time

| 5:00
| The haul unlocks Slow rivers, and two strays follow the crew home to a free Hearth. The player puts them to work at the Star Well and the Garden, weaves Slow rivers into the Heart's second slot, and sees the stability bar for the first time.
| Heists teach new laws and bring new characters; characters at home earn; laws cost stability

| 6:30
| The Archivist suggests putting Half gravity and Slow rivers together in the Lab. The first experiment takes 5 minutes.
| Combining laws reveals secrets

| 7:00
| Two more ruins appear. The player chooses one and runs it alone.
| Choosing targets

| 10:00
| The experiment finishes with insight pages, and three daily goals appear. The session ends with the characters tired and the next realm two levels away.
| There's always a next step

The first week

Day 1: Keeper level 3, the first outer realm chosen, the first birth at its Nursery.

Day 2: the first heist against another player's universe. Because raiding a player ends the newcomer's 72-hour protection early, the game says so before the first one.

Day 3: the first resonance for most players, and a first visitor at the Dock.

Days 4 to 7: joining a constellation, the second outer realm, the Tinkerer, the first rehearsal, and the first real defence decision: who stays home.

17Constellations

Clans are called constellations. They cooperate rather than go to war: they share a law, fight anomalies together and help each other grow. Nothing in a constellation is an obligation.

17.1 Basics

Up to 30 Keepers, joined through the Beacon from Keeper level 6: a founder, up to four elders, and members. Chat, shared replays and shared heist plans. Players aged 13 to 15 use preset chat inside constellations (see 20.2).

17.2 The Grand Law

Each week members vote on three candidate laws, drawn from the laws the members own between them.

The winner is woven into every member's Heart as an extra slot that costs no stability, at tier I, or tier II if the member's Beacon is level 3 or higher.

Raiders see the Grand Law when they scout, so a constellation's choice shapes how its members are raided.

17.3 Anomalies

Every two weeks an anomaly appears and drifts toward one member's universe, arriving in 48 hours.

Members fight it in a shared arena: a three-realm universe built by the game, running the anomaly's two laws plus a third that changes each day. The Devourer runs Inward pull and Heavy ground, the Hourglass Storm runs Rewind and Haste, and the Wildfire Bloom runs Heat bloom and Regrowth.

Each member can make up to three runs a day, but only their best run of the day counts, so nobody needs to play more than once. Runs score for loot carried out, pylons broken and time left on the clock.

If the constellation's total reaches the target before arrival, every member earns Insight and a cosmetic; members who ran at least once also earn Essence, and top contributors have a chance at a stray with the rare Starborn trait.

If the anomaly arrives, the target's production drops 10% for 12 hours. Losing is a nuisance, never a disaster.

17.4 Helping each other

Page requests: ask for one law's pages; each member can give up to five a day.

Borrowed crew: from Beacon level 3, lend one character a day to a member's heist. It comes home tired, and the lender gets Essence.

No penalties: missing a vote, a donation or an anomaly never costs a player anything.

17.5 Competition inside cooperation

Constellations compete as teams in a seasonal league ranked by members' combined Renown and anomaly scores. There are no constellation wars at launch. Team competition built on cooperation is the combination the research supports best (see 19).

17.6 Sharing and invitations

| Feature
| What it does
| Kind of spread

| Friends' feed
| The Beacon shows what friends just did: "Marta's crew slipped through Rui's Borrowed Face." Visible to friends in the game.
| Passive broadcast

| Highlight clips
| After each heist the game cuts a 15-second vertical clip of its best moment, with the laws labelled, ready to share in one tap.
| Passive broadcast

| Challenge links
| Send your island to a friend as a playable challenge. Where the platform allows, it opens instantly in a lightweight version of the game; otherwise it installs straight into that island.
| Active and personal

| Invites with gifts
| A personal invite gives both players a matching cosmetic when the friend plays their first heist.
| Active and personal

In a randomised trial with the 1.4 million friends of 9,687 users, automatic notifications increased peer adoption by 246%, and adding personal invitations added another 98%. Personal invitations worked better per message and were linked to longer use; automatic notifications were used far more often [31]. Ruleborn therefore builds in both. Age-band limits on sharing are in 20.7.

17.7 Crews: why 30 is too many to belong to

Large-scale guild data from a long-running online game found guild sizes follow a power law with a mean around 17 members and a median of 9, and that guilds are fragile: members leave often and groups dissolve [138]. Being in the same large group as 29 people is not the same as belonging. Commitment, not raw membership, is what predicts staying [135].

| Layer
| Size
| What it is for

| Crew
| 4 to 8
| The people you actually play with: co-op heists, shared goals, page gifts, a private thread. Formed inside a constellation, or with friends across constellations.

| Constellation
| Up to 30
| The wider club: the Grand Law vote, the anomaly, the spotlight, the league. A place to be known, not a place to be counted.

| Season league
| Everyone
| Context, optional, with safe floors.

No quotas anywhere. Donation targets, activity minimums and shared quests that punish the group for one absent member are documented dark patterns built on guilt [139], and guilt at letting teammates down is exactly the relatedness frustration that drives people out [82].

A crew survives its founder leaving. Any member can keep it; nothing is lost when someone stops playing.

Recognition is peer-to-peer first. A crewmate naming your defence as the one that beat them counts for more than a global position, because it comes from someone who knows what it took.

17.8 Blocking, and what it does not do

Blocking hides a person; it does not remove their island from the world. They disappear from chat, invitations and anything with their name on it, and you disappear from theirs.

Their island can still appear as a target, and yours as theirs. This looks odd until you remember that a raid takes nothing: there is no harm to be protected from, and raids are anonymous while they happen. Letting blocking remove targets would create a way to shrink the pool, dodge strong defenders, and coordinate who faces whom — real abuse in exchange for protection against a harm that does not exist here.

18Seasons, eras and epochs

18.1 Seasons

A season lasts eight weeks. Renown places players in six leagues: Dust, Ember, Tide, Star, Nova and Paradox, each with three divisions except Paradox. Nobody can drop out of Dust or Ember, so new players only climb. At the end of a season, rewards are given by league and Renown is pulled halfway back toward the floor of the player's league.

18.2 The season track

Fifty tiers, climbed with season points from heists, defences and discoveries. The free track gives pages, Essence and a stray. The premium track, bought with Starglass, adds cosmetics only: realm skins, sky themes, bridge styles, character outfits, Sage robes and new visual styles for laws.

18.3 Eras (at launch)

Each week one rule changes for every universe, which refreshes which laws and crews are strong. Eras are pure data, so they are cheap to ship at launch.

| Era
| Rule for the week

| Heavy Stars
| Motion laws cost 30% less; gravity can't be set lower

| The Long Night
| Vision is 30% shorter everywhere

| High Tide
| Slow rivers costs no stability

| Wildgrowth
| Life laws are 25% stronger

| Echoing Skies
| Everyone returns once at 40% after being knocked out

| Static Storm
| Energy wards are 50% stronger

| Glass Moon
| Walls everywhere are see-through

| Quiet Week
| Heists are 30 seconds longer

18.4 Epochs (after launch)

Every universe gathers entropy: 1.25% a day, full after about 80 days.

When it's full, the player may begin a new epoch: buildings, resources and ruleborn levels reset; laws and their tiers, Sages, resonances, cosmetics and trophies stay.

Each new epoch raises stability capacity by 8%, and the second epoch adds a third law slot in the Heart.

Epochs are always optional. A full universe keeps working normally; there is no penalty for waiting.

18.5 The long game

By month 3 most players own every realm, so the late game is built from mastery, social play and creativity rather than more levels.

| System
| What it gives veterans

| Mastery tracks
| One per school, filled by crossing and weaving its laws. Milestones unlock titles and new visual styles for that school's laws.

| Paradox hunts
| Lab experiments with tier III laws, rumours from visitors, and the three paradox-born characters.

| Defence challenges
| Each week sets a theme (for example "only Time laws"). Players submit a defence; the best become next week's Drifting ruins for everyone, named after their designer.

| Echo trials
| Weekly puzzle heists with fixed crews, scored on style.

| Harder anomalies
| Each season adds a tougher anomaly tier for experienced constellations.

| Top leagues
| Star, Nova and Paradox leagues with season-end cosmetics.

| Epochs
| A fresh start that keeps the collection (version 1.1).

18.6 The Island Workshop

The Workshop turns every player into a possible level designer. It is a free sandbox with every law and terrain feature the player has unlocked and no stability limit, where they build islands and publish them as heist puzzles for anyone to play.

Publishing: an island must be cleared by its creator before it can be published, so every island can be solved.

Discovery: players rate islands for fun, cleverness and fairness. The best join the Drifting ruins and a weekly spotlight, with the creator's name on them.

Rewards: Creator marks unlock the in-game ladder in 18.7, which stays the main reward for everyone. On top of it, a share of revenue is paid to creators whose islands people actually play, under the rules in 18.9, which are written specifically to avoid turning a hobby into piecework.

Structured data only: an island is a set of laws, terrain, guards and a layout — never an uploaded file or image. This is a safety and cost decision as much as a technical one (20.10).

Safety: island names come from word lists, every island can be reported, featured islands are reviewed by a person, and players aged 13 to 15 browse curated lists only (20.7).

Why: the best-selling game ever is a creation game [38], and Steam games that support player-made content keep 75% more concurrent players after two years and 115% more after five. That comparison doesn't prove cause and effect, because such games may differ in other ways [39].

18.7 What creators get

| Level
| Earned by
| What it gives

| Builder
| Publishing an island anyone completes
| A creator badge on your profile; your name shown on the island; 3 extra island slots

| Wright
| 1,000 completions across your islands
| Every law and terrain feature unlocked inside the Workshop, including ones you don't own; a creator's realm skin

| Architect
| 10,000 completions, or one island featured in the weekly spotlight
| The season pass free for that season; early access to each season's new laws, a week before everyone; a unique skiff

| Loremaker
| Three featured islands, or 100,000 completions
| The season pass free for a year; a Sage robe nobody else can get; your island permanently in the Drifting ruins with your name on it; a say in one season's theme

No power: none of these make a creator stronger in heists or defence. Everything is access, cosmetics and recognition.

Completions, not ratings: counting how many people finished an island rewards islands that are fun to play, and is harder to game than a rating.

Anti-gaming: completions by the same player count once; completions from a creator's own constellation count at a quarter; sudden spikes are reviewed by a person.

Why it works: people value what they built themselves, but only when they finish it [49] — so the reward is for finished, playable islands, and the ladder gives a reason to keep building.

18.8 Seasons without pressure

The season pass is the least evidence-backed part of this design, so it is built with its own weaknesses in mind. The one published behavioural study of a battle pass found it had minimal effect on player uptake even as spending rose, and that players' own themes were a grind-versus-spend trade-off, elitism and pressure [140]. Much of what the industry believes about seasons is designer opinion rather than evidence (28.1).

| Rule
| What it means

| Nothing expires forever
| Unclaimed tiers stay claimable in a later season at the same cost. A missed season costs time, never access.

| Catch-up is built in
| Joining in week 6 does not mean losing 5 weeks. The track scales to the time remaining.

| No countdown pressure
| No "last chance" banners, no timers on the home screen, no notification when a season is ending.

| Cosmetic only, on both tracks
| Nothing in a season affects a heist, so nobody is behind for skipping one.

| It has to earn its place
| If the pass raises need frustration without raising meaning or appreciation in the tests in 14.10, it is removed, whatever it earns.

18.9 The Island Fund

Version 1.3 ruled revenue sharing out. Version 1.4 puts it back in, deliberately, with a design built around the reasons it was ruled out. This section states the risk honestly before describing the scheme.

What the evidence says against paying creators

Crowding out is real and hits your best people hardest. High-powered monetary incentives reduce motivation specifically among the already highly motivated, while leaving the unmotivated unchanged [144]. Those are exactly the creators a Workshop depends on.

Expected, contingent rewards undermine interest. Across 128 experiments, rewards expected for doing a task lowered later interest, with effects around 0.36 to 0.40 [3].

The money concentrates. On the largest creator platform, the median creator in the payout programme received about $1,575 across a year while the top ten averaged $33.9 million; most creators earn almost nothing [145].

It brings obligations: tax and contract rules in every country, identity checks, and the problem of creators aged 13 to 17 in a 13+ game.

Why we are doing it anyway, and what that means for the design

Paying people for work others enjoy is fair, and unpaid creator economies age badly once a game earns real money. But the same 128-experiment meta-analysis that found expected rewards undermine interest also found that unexpected rewards did not, and that positive feedback about competence raised it [3]. The whole scheme is therefore built to be a consequence of creating, never a reason to create.

How it works

| Rule
| Detail
| Which risk it answers

| A fixed fund, not a rate
| 10% of net cosmetic and season-pass revenue goes into a Island Fund each quarter. No per-play rate is ever promised, so nobody can calculate an hourly wage for building an island.
| Stops creation becoming piecework

| Paid in arrears, and quietly
| The fund is divided at the end of each quarter by time actually played on each island. There is no live earnings counter, no projected income, and no "you could have earned" messaging.
| Keeps the reward unexpected rather than contingent [3]

| Progressive, with a cap
| The share per hour played falls in bands as a creator's total rises, and no single creator may take more than 2% of a quarter's fund. The remainder spreads down the tail.
| Answers the concentration in [145]

| A floor that matters more than the ceiling
| Any island that passes 500 completions in a quarter earns a minimum payment. Being paid something for work people enjoyed is the point; being paid a lot is not the design goal.
| Makes the long tail visible rather than symbolic

| Recognition stays primary
| The ladder in 18.7, the spotlight, the name on the ruins and the creator badge are unchanged and remain what the interface celebrates. Payments are shown in a settings page, not on the profile.
| Competence feedback, which raises interest [3]

| No creator storefront
| Creators never sell anything to players. There are no paid islands, no tips, no creator currencies. All money flows from the company's own cosmetic revenue.
| Keeps the Workshop a place to play, not a marketplace

| Opt-in, and 18+
| Payment requires opting in, verifying identity and being 18 or over. Creators aged 13 to 17 keep the full in-game ladder and can opt in when they turn 18; nothing they built before is excluded from later payment.
| Answers the legal and child-safety problem directly (20.7)

| Paid for play, never for engagement tricks
| Time played is capped per player per island per quarter, self-play and constellation play are discounted as in 18.7, and any island found gaming the count is removed from the fund permanently.
| Stops the fund rewarding retention bait

The test that decides whether it stays

Before the fund launches, we record output and PXI-meaning for the top 200 creators (14.10).

Six months later we re-measure the same people. If their output or their sense of meaning has fallen, that is crowding out [144], and the fund is withdrawn while the in-game ladder stays.

We also watch what gets built: if islands start converging on whatever maximises played time rather than on what creators wanted to make, the fund is failing even if the numbers look good.

This is a live experiment with a stated failure condition, not a permanent commitment.

What the fund costs the business
10% of net cosmetic and pass revenue is real money in a game with no other income. At the base scenario in 22.2 that is roughly €2,500 a month; at the high scenario about €16,800. It is affordable precisely because the game is cosmetic-only and has no paid power to fund. If soft launch shows the business cannot carry it, the percentage drops before anything else in this document changes.

19What makes a game fun, and engagement design

This section has two halves: what the evidence says makes a game fun and how Ruleborn is measured against it (19.1 to 19.5), and how engagement is designed without manipulation (19.6 onward). The aim is players who want to come back, not players who feel they have to. The rules in this section come from meta-analyses, large experiments and large play-data studies; weaker evidence is marked as such. Full references are in section 28.

19.1 Can fun be researched?

Mostly, yes. "Fun" as a single word can't be measured, but enjoyment has been broken into constructs that can be, and three validated instruments now exist:

The Player Experience Inventory separates what a game does (ease of control, goals and rules, challenge, progress feedback, audiovisual appeal) from what the player feels (mastery, curiosity, immersion, autonomy, meaning), and shows that the first group produces enjoyment through the second [56]. It has since been independently validated on a large sample [57].

GameFlow gathers the design literature into eight elements of enjoyment — concentration, challenge, skills, control, clear goals, feedback, immersion and social interaction — with 38 criteria beneath them [58].

CORGIS shows that challenge is not one thing: players experience performative, cognitive, decision-making and emotional challenge separately [59], and a separate scale does the same for uncertainty [60].

What research cannot do is tell us whether this game is fun. It gives the variables, the instruments and the traps. The answer still comes from players, which is what 19.5 is for.

19.2 The variables of fun

Fourteen variables, each with evidence behind it, what Ruleborn does about it, and how it is measured.

| Variable
| What the evidence says
| In Ruleborn
| Measured by

| 1. Control that feels immediate
| Ease of control is one of the strongest routes to enjoyment, through feeling capable [56] [58]
| At most eight controls, 44-point targets, no reflex requirements, one-finger play (12.7, 24.8)
| PXI ease of control ≥ +1.5

| 2. Clear goals and rules
| Clear goals and rules are a separate driver from challenge [56] [58]
| One stated goal per heist; every law visible as a banner, a card and a way of moving (7.8)
| PXI goals and rules ≥ +1.5

| 3. Challenge, in the right kind
| Challenge splits into performative, cognitive, decision-making and emotional [59]; difficulty alone does less than expected [43] [7]
| Deliberately cognitive and decision-making; performative kept low; emotional kept gentle
| CORGIS: cognitive and decision-making high, performative low

| 4. Uncertainty and suspense
| Suspense from an uncertain outcome predicts enjoyment beyond competence: people enjoyed a close game against a strong opponent more, whatever the result [61]; dopamine responses peak at even odds [14]
| The Vault's contents unknown until opened; patrols that shift; a clock that stays in doubt (12.12)
| The uncertainty scale [60]; heist outcomes near 50/50 at high difficulty

| 5. Curiosity
| Curiosity was the strongest predictor of both enjoyment and time played in an experiment on moment-to-moment feedback [62], and improves memory [10]
| Unknown laws shown as "?", hidden resonances and chords, scouting, the Codex
| PXI curiosity ≥ +1.5; share of players who open the Codex unprompted

| 6. Feedback that means something
| Feedback that depends on how well you did raised curiosity, competence and effectance; feedback simply amplified reduced them, probably by muddying what caused what [62]. Medium and high polish beat none and beat excess [63]
| Every effect scales with the quality of the action: a clean ambush looks different from a scrappy one. Nothing fires twice for the same event. Polish tuned to "medium to high", never maximum
| PXI progress feedback; an A/B test of feedback intensity in the slice

| 7. Choice
| Choice raises intrinsic motivation, effort and perceived competence, most strongly with 2 to 4 successive choices, and less when a reward follows the choice [64]
| Three difficulties, target selection, route forks, crew picks, law weaving: 2 to 4 real choices per session, none of them rewarded for choosing
| PXI autonomy ≥ +1.5

| 8. Competence and mastery
| A sense of growing skill is the psychological consequence most tied to enjoyment [56]
| Grades on heists, replays, mastery tracks, a level cap in fights so skill still decides
| PXI mastery ≥ +1.5

| 9. Concentration without overload
| Concentration is an element of enjoyment; attention must be on things that matter [58]
| The complexity budget (7.8); at most one new law per session early on (15.5)
| Drop-off in the first three sessions; PXI challenge not above +2

| 10. Novelty
| Novelty raised motivation in the same experiments where difficulty did little [43]; moderate novelty beats both the familiar and the strange [41]
| Something unseen in every run; familiar anchors (heists, creatures, islands) around unfamiliar rules
| Share of runs containing something new, by design ≥ 90%

| 11. Progress you can see
| Effort rises as a goal nears, and partly filled goals are finished faster [44] [45]; but points, levels and leaderboards did not raise intrinsic motivation in a controlled experiment — they raised output [65]
| Albums start partly filled and show what's left (14.8). Levels and leagues are treated as information, never as the source of fun
| PXI progress feedback; completion rates per album

| 12. Other people
| Social interaction is one of the eight elements [58]; tight-knit groups stay longest [34]; social features predict late retention [20]
| Friendly heists, co-op, constellations, gifting, trading, the Workshop
| Share of players who play with a friend in week 1 ≥ 40% (25.1)

| 13. Meaning and fantasy
| Meaning — a sense that the game connects to something you care about — is one of the five psychosocial constructs [56]
| Your islands, your rules, your creatures, your name on islands other people play
| PXI meaning ≥ +1.0

| 14. Immersion
| Immersion is an element of enjoyment, though it measures less reliably than the rest [56] [57]
| The diorama, the sound of each school, a session that fits in a coffee break
| PXI immersion, read with caution

19.3 The traps

| Trap
| Evidence
| Rule

| Mistaking points for fun
| Points, levels and leaderboards left intrinsic motivation and competence unchanged while raising output [65]
| No system is justified by "it adds progression". It has to serve a variable in 19.2.

| Over-polishing
| Excessive feedback reduced motivation; too much juice can obscure what the player actually did [62] [63]
| Feedback must be legible and proportional. When in doubt, less.

| Chasing difficulty
| Moderate difficulty did not maximise engagement [7] [43]
| Tune uncertainty, novelty and choice first; difficulty last.

| Claiming the game adapts to you
| Simply telling players a game adapts changes their experience, even when it doesn't, and mismatched claims can disappoint [66]
| Never claim adaptation we don't do. Difficulty is labelled and chosen (12.12).

| Measuring one "fun" number
| Enjoyment runs through several separable constructs [56] [59]
| Always measure the constructs, not a single score.

| Assuming everyone wants the same fun
| Motivations vary widely across players [52]; challenge comes in four kinds [59]
| Report results by motivation profile, not only as averages.

19.4 Where Ruleborn is weak

| Variable
| Honest assessment
| What we do about it

| Performative challenge
| Almost absent by design. Players who want reflexes and spectacle will not stay.
| Accepted. The escape and the alarm carry what tension a slower game can.

| Emotional challenge
| Low. There is no story that asks anything difficult of the player.
| Accepted at launch; Sages and seasons could carry more later.

| Immersion
| Short sessions work against deep absorption, and immersion is the least reliable construct anyway.
| We aim for charm and presence rather than absorption, and read this measure with caution.

| Concentration
| The biggest risk in the whole design: the game asks more of a player's attention than its competitors.
| The complexity budget, the learning map and one-new-law-per-session (7.8, 15).

19.5 The fun test

Every phase ends with the same measurement, so the question "is it fun?" is answered the same way each time.

| Instrument
| What it tells us
| When

| Player Experience Inventory, 11 constructs [56]
| Which parts of the experience work and which don't
| Every playtest from the vertical slice onward

| CORGIS, four kinds of challenge [59]
| Whether the challenge is the kind we intended
| Vertical slice, alpha, beta

| The uncertainty scale [60]
| Whether outcomes feel uncertain without feeling arbitrary
| Vertical slice, alpha

| "Was this worth your time?", one tap
| The everyday measure, at scale
| Every session, live

| Behaviour: return rate, second session, share of players who play again unprompted
| What people do rather than say
| Always

The fun gate

PXI mastery, curiosity, autonomy, ease of control and goals and rules each at +1.5 or higher on the −3 to +3 scale, with no construct below 0.

CORGIS: cognitive and decision-making challenge above the midpoint; performative challenge below it.

At least 7 of 10 outside testers ask to play again without being asked.

"Worth your time" at 4.3 out of 5 or higher.

These are read by motivation profile as well as on average: a game that only works for one kind of player fails the gate.

19.6 What the evidence says

| Finding
| Evidence
| What Ruleborn does

| Needs for autonomy, competence and relatedness each predict enjoyment and future play.
| Four studies [1]
| Every system is checked against the three needs.

| Expected rewards for playing or finishing reduce later interest (effect sizes 0.40 and 0.36); positive feedback raises it (0.33). Tangible rewards hurt children more than adults.
| Meta-analysis of 128 experiments [3]
| No rewards for logging in; results rich in feedback (grades, replays, mastery); surprise finds instead of scheduled handouts.

| Intrinsic motivation predicts performance, and how visible incentives are changes that link.
| Meta-analysis, 183 samples, 212,468 people [4]
| Rewards stay modest and in the background; the heist itself is the point.

| Time played doesn't predict well-being; motivation and how well gaming fits a player's life do.
| Logged play of 38,935 players [5]; 703 players, 140,000 hours [6]
| The main quality measure is "Was this worth your time?", not minutes.

| Players whose needs are poorly met play more obsessively, play more, and feel more tension afterwards.
| Large survey [2]
| No obligations and no penalties for absence.

| Easier versions held players longer than moderately hard ones, though players learned more slowly.
| Experiments with 10,000 and 70,000 players [7]
| About 80% early success, falling to 55 to 65% after a month; hard targets optional (14.5).

| Flow comes from challenge matching skill, clear goals and a sense of control.
| Meta-analysis of 28 studies [8]
| Goals, clock and rules always visible; responsive controls (12.6, 12.7).

| Tutorials paid off only in the complex game (29% more play time); teaching in context beat manuals; forced steps didn't help; a help button cut play time by 15% in a simpler game.
| Experiment with over 45,000 players [9]
| Each law taught in context the first time it appears; no manuals or forced steps; help tested before release.

| Curiosity activates reward circuitry and improves memory.
| Brain-imaging study [10]
| Unknown laws shown as "?", Lab hints, scouting reveals.

| Large choice sets overwhelm when options are complex, decisions hard and preferences unclear.
| Meta-analysis, 7,202 people [11]
| Two or three recommended laws with a comparison; gradual unlocks.

| Competition has no overall effect on performance: the urge to win helps and the fear of losing hurts.
| Meta-analyses [12]
| Raids framed as winning; leagues with safe floors; results show gains first.

| Game elements work best with story and social interaction, especially competition combined with collaboration. Effects on motivation are less stable in rigorous studies.
| Meta-analysis [13]
| Constellation leagues plus co-op anomalies, with the story woven into both.

| Dopamine neurons respond to uncertainty, peaking at even odds.
| Animal neuroscience [14]
| Heist outcomes stay uncertain because of skill and information.

| Buying loot boxes correlates with problem gambling (0.27) and problem gaming (0.40).
| Systematic review [16]
| No paid randomness, ever.

| Losses weigh about twice as much as gains, though this has recently been disputed.
| Meta-analysis of 607 estimates, challenged in 2025 [15]
| Loss caps, the sealed share, and results that say "you kept 88%".

| Intact streaks raise engagement; breaks demotivate unless the streak can be repaired.
| Seven studies [17]
| A weekly streak with an automatic saver and repairs.

| People tend to resume interrupted tasks; the memory advantage of unfinished tasks doesn't replicate.
| Meta-analysis [18]
| Every session ends with something in progress.

| Habits form in about two months at the median, varying from 4 to 335 days; self-chosen habits are stronger.
| Systematic review, mostly high risk of bias [19]
| A designed 60-day arc; a reminder time the player chooses.

| Achievement predicts retention early on; social features predict it best at the top level.
| Logs of 51,104 players [20]
| Social systems deepen in the late game.

| About 3% of players meet criteria for gaming disorder. Reward and punishment, social and paid features are linked to symptoms; long grinds and social obligation are risk factors.
| Meta-analysis [21]; reviews [22] [23]
| The guardrails in 20.3.

19.7 Design rules

Reward with information, not payment: grades, replays and mastery build interest; scheduled handouts wear it down.

Make surprises unexpected and earned, and show their odds.

Be generous early: about four heists in five succeed in the first days.

Teach in context: every law the first time it appears, never in a manual.

Keep choices small: two or three options at a time, with a recommendation.

Frame raids as winning, with safe league floors.

Show what was kept, not what was lost.

Put competition inside cooperation.

Make the late game social.

Plan for 60 days, not 7.

Let players set their own rhythm: a chosen reminder time and a weekly streak.

End sessions with something in progress.

Take uncertainty from skill and information, never from purchase.

Measure value, not minutes.

No obligation and no grind.

19.8 Claims we don't rely on

That unfinished tasks are remembered better (the Zeigarnik effect): it doesn't replicate [18].

That moderate challenge maximises engagement: it failed with 70,000 players [7].

That habits form in 21 days: the median is about two months [19].

That player-needs surveys prove a design works: the latest scale later lacked predictive validity [24], so playtest surveys are a guide, not proof.

That losses always weigh exactly twice as much as gains [15].

That design should chase "dopamine hits": the robust finding is about uncertainty [14].

19.9 Evidence added for reach and sharing

| Finding
| Evidence
| What Ruleborn does

| Automatic activity notifications raised peer adoption by 246%; adding personal invitations added 98% more. Personal invitations worked better per message and went with longer use.
| Randomised trial, 1.4 million people [31]
| A friends' feed and highlight clips, plus personal challenge links (17.6)

| Difficulty tuned to maximise engagement raised it by up to 9% without changing revenue.
| A/B experiments in several Electronic Arts games [32]
| Rejected for hidden use: difficulty is labelled and chosen instead (12.12, 19.12)

| Players buy for unobstructed play, social reasons and value for money, and these go with how much they spend.
| Survey of 519 players on 19 purchase reasons [33]
| Gifting, showcases and fair bundles; nothing is sold to remove an obstacle

| Players in tight-knit groups stay the longest.
| Network data from a large online game [34]
| Friends first: heists with friends, constellations, trading

| The retention bar: median games keep about 22% of players after a day and under 4% after a week; the top 1% keep over 25% after a week.
| Benchmarks from thousands of games [35]
| Gates in 25.1

| Spending and success are extremely concentrated: 9 games passed $1 billion in 2025.
| Market data [36]
| The honest odds in 2.7

| Games with player-made content keep far more players after two and five years (correlation, not proof).
| The top 1,000 Steam games [39]
| The Island Workshop (18.6)

| Brand popularity, reviews and awards, and graphics, sound and game length predicted sales.
| Longitudinal study of 351 games [40]
| A quality bar for art and audio; an awards and press plan

| Moderate novelty tends to beat both the familiar and the very strange.
| Research on optimal differentiation across creative markets [41]
| Familiar anchors (heists, creatures, islands) around new rules

| In many mobile games a tiny share of players provides much of the revenue, and financial harm concentrates there.
| Analysis of spending at massive scale [42]
| Cosmetics only, spending caps for minors, no paid randomness

19.10 Rules for reach and sharing

Make every heist worth showing to a friend, and make showing it one tap.

Put friends first: a friend's island, a friendly heist or a co-op invite is always one tap from home.

Let players make the game: the Workshop is a core mode, not a side feature.

Collect by design: creatures come from the islands you build, never from paid chance.

Adapt to the player against the game, never secretly against other players.

19.11 Evidence on motivation, progress and learning

| Finding
| Evidence
| What changed

| Difficulty mattered less than expected; choice of difficulty, novelty and suspense each raised motivation.
| Three experiments, over 20,000 play sessions [43]
| Difficulty is chosen by the player; every run carries novelty and an uncertain ending (12.12)

| People accelerate as a goal nears, and a goal that starts partly filled is finished faster than an identical empty one.
| Field experiments and customer records [44]; a car-wash field experiment [45]
| Albums and tracks start partly filled and show what's left (14.8)

| People value what they built themselves far more, but only when they finish it.
| Four experiments [49]
| The Workshop requires you to clear your island before publishing; player creations are never deleted or overwritten (18.6)

| Spreading practice out and recalling rather than re-reading improve retention, though the recall advantage is smaller in some fields.
| Meta-analyses [46] [47] [48]
| Laws return on a spaced schedule; the game asks you to recall them in play (15.5)

| Matchmaking tuned for engagement rather than fairness keeps more players. Churn after repeated losses was nearly twice that of other states.
| 36.9 million matches from 1.68 million players [50]
| We refuse to use it against players, and say so (19.12)

| Research on games leans heavily on one motivation theory, often applied shallowly and rarely questioned.
| Review of 259 studies [51]
| Claims resting on that theory are now marked, and checked against behaviour (19.14)

| Twelve motivations group into six pairs; players differ widely in which ones they want.
| Survey of 466,000 players [52]
| A coverage check, and honesty about which motivations Ruleborn doesn't serve (19.13)

| Roughly a fifth to a third of players report a disability, and most people with an impairment play games.
| Industry surveys and platform figures [53]
| A full accessibility specification (20.8)

| Most churn happens in the first session; players who complete a meaningful action in it are far likelier to still be playing a month later.
| Industry benchmark analyses [54]
| Rules and a funnel for the first 60 seconds (25.2)

| Store-page images and icons change how many people install, and can be tested for free on real traffic.
| Platform tools and vendor reports [55]
| Store pages and languages are tested before launch (26.4)

| The median session in mobile games is 5 to 6 minutes; the top quarter reach 8 to 9, with about 4 sessions a day.
| Benchmarks across thousands of games [35]
| The 5 to 9 minute session target is confirmed as right for the top quartile, not lengthened

19.12 What we refuse to do

Some techniques are proven to work and are still wrong for this game. Writing them down stops them from creeping in later.

| Technique
| Why it works
| Why Ruleborn won't

| Matchmaking tuned for engagement
| Pairing players by predicted risk of quitting, rather than by skill, keeps more of them playing [50]
| It decides who wins without telling anyone. Ruleborn matches by Renown band and by the player's own choice of target, and shows why each target was offered.

| Hidden difficulty tuning
| Adjusting difficulty invisibly raises engagement by up to 9% [32]
| Choice beats the hidden version anyway [43], so difficulty is labelled and chosen.

| Soft-currency pricing
| Virtual currency raises willingness to pay through money illusion — in an incentivised trial of 753 people, significantly more was spent in virtual currency than in real money even at a 1:1 rate [156]
| Every cosmetic shows its real-money price. Starglass exists only as a gift and season-pass wrapper, always at a visible 1:1 rate (9.1, 22.1)

| Engineered near misses
| They raise arousal and the urge to continue [149]
| Never tuned for frequency; they occur only as a by-product of honest stealth

| Paid randomness
| It is the most profitable mechanic in mobile games
| It correlates with problem gambling and problem gaming [16].

| Personalised pricing or offers timed to weakness
| It raises revenue per player
| Everyone sees the same shop, at the same prices.

| Obligation
| Clan duties and daily streaks raise how often people return
| Missing anything costs nothing here (20.3).

| Endless grind for power
| It stretches out the content
| Time is spent on new ideas, not repetition.

19.13 Do we serve what players actually want?

A survey of 466,000 players groups what people want into twelve motivations in six pairs [52]. Ruleborn is checked against all twelve, including the ones it chooses not to serve.

| Pair
| Motivation
| Where Ruleborn serves it

| Action
| Destruction
| Deliberately not served. Nothing is destroyed permanently, and that is part of the game's identity.

| Excitement
| Partly: alarms, escapes and the last 30 seconds of a heist, without fast reflexes being required.

| Social
| Competition
| Leagues, Renown and defence challenges, with safe floors.

| Community
| Constellations, co-op anomalies, heists with friends, gifting and trading.

| Mastery
| Challenge
| Heists, harder difficulties by choice, Echo trials, top leagues.

| Strategy
| The heart of the game: laws, harmony, terrain, crews and defence.

| Achievement
| Completion
| 48 laws, 44 resonances, 12 chords, 144 forms, mastery tracks.

| Power
| Served through levels and tiers, but capped: a level advantage counts for at most 3 levels (12.8).

| Immersion
| Fantasy
| Being a Keeper of islands with their own rules.

| Story
| Light: the Book of Laws, the Sages, visitors and seasons. Not a narrative game.

| Creativity
| Design
| Islands, terrain, law combinations, crews, and the Workshop.

| Discovery
| Unknown laws, hidden resonances, chords and paradoxes.

Ten of the twelve are served well, one partly, and one not at all by choice. The two weakest, story and excitement, are where a player who bounces off Ruleborn is most likely to be leaving for a different game, which is a deliberate trade rather than an oversight.

19.14 How much weight the evidence carries

Marked as theory, not proof: the three-needs framework running through this document is the dominant theory in games research, but a review of 259 studies found it is often applied shallowly and rarely questioned [51]. Every claim resting on it is therefore treated as a hypothesis to check against behaviour, not as evidence.

Ranked by strength: randomised experiments and meta-analyses first, large play-data studies second, industry benchmarks third, single-game case studies last. Industry figures are labelled as such throughout.

Effects assumed to shrink: published effects are usually larger than what a new product reproduces, so every target in 25 is set on the assumption that these effects arrive smaller here.

Our own evidence outranks all of it once playtests and soft-launch data exist.

19.15 Evidence on company, failure, the senses and teasing

| Finding
| Evidence
| What changed

| Cooperation and interdependence each raised enjoyment, relatedness and trust between players on their own, and their effects add up. Sharing a goal also raised how much effort players put in.
| Four versions of an online game tested on paired strangers [67]
| Co-op heists give each player something the others can't do, and one shared goal (12.11)

| How players explain what happened to them — their own doing, luck, or the game — shapes their emotions and their motivation to continue.
| Attribution theory applied to player experience [68]
| Every result names the decisive moment, so failure is traceable to a decision (12.9)

| Autonomy in games comes from a steady stream of small decisions rather than a few large ones, and it is what players value most when they describe why a game is interesting.
| Experiment with 148 players plus interviews [69]
| The decision-density rule now applies at home as well as in heists (19.16)

| Creating an uncertainty and then resolving it produces a better experience than giving everything at once — but people don't choose it when asked, so it has to be designed in.
| Seven studies [70]
| The teasing rule: brief, honest withholding before every reveal (19.16)

| Sound and music raise enjoyment, flow, immersion and motivation; the effect on how positive players feel was small to moderate and consistent. Music players dislike lowers immersion.
| Systematic review and meta-analysis of 14 studies [71]; experiments on soundtrack music [72]
| Audio is treated as a fun variable with its own budget, and music is always separable and skippable (21.8)

19.16 Two rules this adds

| Rule
| What it means everywhere in the game

| Small decisions, constantly
| One meaningful choice every 8 to 12 seconds in a heist (12.6), and at least three at home each session: what to weave, who to move, what to shape. No screen exists only to be confirmed.

| Tease, then resolve
| Nothing important is revealed flat. A Vault shows its size before its contents; a new law arrives as a strange movement before it is named; a stray follows the crew for a few seconds before it is recognised; a Lab result hums before it resolves. The wait is seconds, never hours, and never purchasable [70].

19.17 The fun backlog

What the evidence cannot settle, listed as experiments to run in the vertical slice and alpha. Each is a real A/B test with the instruments in 19.5.

| Question
| Test
| Decides

| How much polish is right?
| Three levels of feedback intensity, success-dependent in all three
| The audiovisual budget for the whole game

| Is the heist clock a pleasure or a stressor?
| Clock versus a guard-pressure meter with no timer
| Whether heists stay timed

| Does the complexity budget hold?
| One, two or three unfamiliar laws per route for new players
| The limits in 7.8

| How long should a heist be?
| 90, 150 and 240 seconds
| Session shape

| Does teasing beat telling?
| Instant reveal versus a two-second tease on laws, Vaults and strays
| Whether 19.16's second rule is kept

| Is defence fun to design?
| Building an island with and without the rehearsal tool
| How much defence tooling to build

| Do friends change everything?
| Solo play versus play with one friend present, same content
| How hard to push friends-first onboarding

| Portrait or landscape?
| The same heist in both, with one tap to switch
| Which becomes the default, and whether landscape is worth its 15 to 20% interface cost

| Does the collection matter?
| Forms visible from day one versus introduced in week two
| When the collection is revealed

19.18 Thirty more studies, and what survived them

Version 0.8 added 30 sources and used them to challenge the design rather than to justify it. The full audit is in 27.5. In short: seven assumptions changed and three survived.

| Area
| Sources added
| Effect

| Cognitive limits
| [75] [76] [77] [78] [79] [80]
| A four-rule interaction cap, and guidance that fades with skill

| Failure and frustration
| [81] [82] [84] [98] [102]
| The three-strike rule; no long loss of control; the case against taking things from players

| Learning in play
| [86] [87] [99] [100] [101]
| Guidance across three sessions; teaching set up as breakthroughs the player earns

| Assistance and honesty
| [83] [93]
| All help is disclosed, which settles the catch-up question

| Creation communities
| [73] [74]
| Realistic Workshop targets and a remix path

| Curiosity and uncertainty
| [94] [95] [96] [97]
| Support for the tease-then-resolve rule, with a warning: curiosity can be made to work against the player, and we don't use it that way

| Company
| [91] [92]
| Interdependent co-op confirmed; games build trust better than conversation does

| Market shape
| [85] [88] [89] [90]
| Portrait-first; login rewards still refused, with reasons; the season pass confirmed

19.19 Evidence on faces, memory, ownership and first impressions

| Finding
| Evidence
| What Ruleborn does

| Infant-like proportions — a large head, round face, big low-set eyes — reliably raise perceived cuteness and the motivation to care for something, and they activate the brain's reward system.
| Experiments manipulating facial proportions [108]; brain imaging [109]; the effect transfers across species, so it works on animals and creatures too [110]
| The ruleborn specification is now explicit: 1.7 to 2.9 heads tall, clustered on two to two and a half, with eyes at or below the midline and at least a fifth of face width, round skulls and short limbs (21.6). The spread is deliberate: head count is what derives the size band a law can read (12.21), so a narrower range would leave the small and large bands empty. Caring for creatures is the emotional core of the collection, so this is a design requirement, not decoration

| People judge an experience by its most intense moment and its ending, not by its average or its length. The effect is large and robust across 174 effect sizes, and duration barely counts.
| Meta-analysis [111]; the original experiments [112]
| Every session is built to have one peak and a good ending (19.20)

| Players who customised their character identified with it more, and that raised autonomy, immersion, effort, enjoyment and time played.
| Experiment [113]; a controlled study of ten hours of play [114]; a systematic review of the wider effect [115]
| Naming and dressing your ruleborn moves from a shop feature to a first-week moment (19.20)

| What looks good is judged easier to use — but the link is largely explained by how easily the thing is understood, not by beauty itself. Controlling for that ease cut the correlation from .79 to .34.
| Foundational studies [116]; experiments on direction [117]; the fluency explanation [118]
| Polish serves legibility first. A screen is finished when it reads instantly, not when it is decorated (21.8)

| Things that are easy to say and read are judged more favourable and less risky.
| Experiments on name pronounceability [119]
| Every name a player must say out loud — the game, the schools, the creatures — is chosen to be easy to pronounce in our twelve languages (19.20)

19.20 Five more rules

| Rule
| What it changes

| Design the peak and the ending, not the average
| Every session gets one designed peak — the Vault opening, a law discovered, a friend's island cleared — and a deliberate ending: the haul counting up, the creature coming home, one line of what comes next. No session ends on a menu or a defeat screen with nothing after it [111]

| Make them yours in the first week
| Naming a ruleborn is offered the first time one hatches, and a free outfit comes with it. Customisation is introduced before it is ever sold [113]

| Build the faces to the cuteness rules
| Head-to-body proportion, eye size and eye height are specified, not left to the artist's taste, and every new creature is checked against them [108]

| Legibility is the polish budget
| When a screen can be made prettier or clearer, it is made clearer. Beauty pays off mainly through ease of understanding [118]

| Say every name out loud
| Names that are hard to pronounce make a thing feel riskier and less likeable, so the final naming pass tests each candidate aloud in all twelve launch languages [119]

19.21 Decisions re-checked against the grade of their evidence

Every decision in this document was traced back to the sources behind it and re-read against the grading in 28.1. Decisions resting only on B or C sources are listed here, with what would change them.

| Decision
| What it rests on
| Verdict

| Portrait by default
| One A source on how phones are held [103]; the rest is market pattern and platform documentation, all grade C [104] [105] [106]
| Held, but marked. No experiment says portrait sells better. This is the weakest major decision in the document, which is why landscape ships alongside it and question 9 in the fun backlog decides it

| The Island Workshop
| An A source on valuing what you build [49]; a C source on games with player-made content retaining better, which is a correlation [39]; participation rates from a heuristic and one observational study [73] [74]
| Held. The targets are set at the low end (1.5% publishing), so the feature does not depend on the optimistic reading

| Cosmetics and a season pass only
| An A source against paid randomness [16]; the revenue comparison itself is grade C [89] [90]
| Held on ethics, not on economics. The business case stays an assumption to be measured in soft launch (22.2)

| One-way theft
| A-grade work on need frustration and attribution [82] [68]; a disputed A source on loss aversion [15]. No experiment has tested one-way theft in a live game
| Held, and the most likely to be reversed. The Open Vault league exists precisely so both can be measured (27.5)

| Twelve languages at launch
| Market data only, grade C
| Reduced to a test. Store pages are translated first and the game follows the demand they measure (26.4)

| The mega-hit gates
| Benchmark data from thousands of games, grade C [35]
| Held as targets, not as science. They are thresholds for a decision, not claims about players

| Cuteness proportions
| A-grade experiments, but measured on infant faces and on judgements, not on players of a game [108] [109] [110]
| Held, with the gap stated. Directness is the weak link, so creature designs are A/B tested in the slice rather than assumed

| No login rewards
| A-grade work on expected rewards [3] and need frustration [82], against near-universal industry practice [88]
| Held. This is where the evidence and the industry disagree, and we are following the evidence knowingly

19.22 Rules for citing anything in future versions

No claim about players from a grade-C source. Industry data sets market shape, prices and benchmarks. It never establishes an effect.

Every effect gets discounted before it is used for planning: published effects replicate at roughly half their original size, and meta-analytic estimates can run about three times high [120] [121] [122].

Directness is checked out loud: who was measured, doing what, on which outcome, and how far that is from our players.

A citation must be able to lose. If no finding could change the decision, the citation is decoration and is removed.

Contradictions are printed, not hidden (19.8, 27.5).

Our own data outranks all of it once the slice and soft launch produce it.

19.23 Evidence behind the expanded content

| Finding
| Evidence
| What it changed

| Attachment to a game character correlates with enjoyment and time played, and can be measured on a 17-item scale.
| Scale development and validation [124]
| Character attachment becomes a measured outcome in playtests, not a hope

| Attachment is not one thing: players describe seven kinds, including admiring competence, enjoying a character's company, and worrying about its wellbeing.
| Qualitative survey of 213 players [125]
| Every ruleborn gets a habit you can watch, a personality line and a vulnerability, so more than one kind of attachment is available (11.11)

| Identification with a character rises with customisation and predicts autonomy, immersion, effort and enjoyment.
| Experiment [113]; controlled study [114]
| Naming and dressing in week one (19.20), and forms tied to the islands you build (11.10)

| Curiosity is the strongest predictor of both enjoyment and time played in moment-to-moment feedback.
| Experiment [62]
| Every law sheet now includes a weakness and a defender use, so laws stay worth investigating after they are unlocked (7.7)

| Depth is limited by what interacts at once, not by how much exists.
| Working-memory and cognitive-load work [75] [77] [78]
| The content can be this large precisely because the four-rule cap governs any one heist (7.8)

19.24 Evidence behind the endgame

| Finding
| Evidence
| What it decided

| People seek entertainment both for pleasure and for meaning, and the meaningful kind is a distinct experience involving reflection, being moved, and a sense that something mattered.
| Motivation research [126]; scoping review in games [127]
| The endgame is built around a place, a reputation and a body of work rather than a final unlock (14.9)

| Specific, difficult goals beat vague or easy ones across roughly a thousand studies, provided the person is committed and gets feedback.
| 35-year synthesis [128]
| Six named endgame goals with visible tracks, instead of an open grind (14.9)

| Achievement predicts retention early; social ties predict it at the top.
| 51,104 players [20]
| The economy stops being the point at month five; reputation and creation take over

| Perceived value of time predicts wellbeing; hours do not.
| [5] [6]
| "Was this worth your time?" remains the measure at every stage, including the endgame

19.25 Evidence review of the endgame, and what it changed

| Finding
| Evidence
| What changed

| Appreciation is measurably distinct from fun, but no study shows it predicts continued play, and its link to wellbeing is negligible in longitudinal data.
| Scale development [129]; scoping review [127]; two-wave panel of 2,561 people [130]
| The endgame thesis is labelled a hypothesis with a test attached (14.9, 14.10)

| Poorly met needs predict more hours with less enjoyment and more tension afterwards; harmonious and obsessive players differ in harm at the same play time, and obsessive players keep going while exhausted.
| [2]; passion research [132] [133]; 597 long-term players [134]
| Passion is measured, and playtime-up-enjoyment-down becomes a kill criterion (14.10)

| Practice explains a minority of skill: about 26% of variance in games, and roughly 5% when measured from logs rather than recall.
| Meta-analysis [141]
| Mastery goals are about skill expression and self-referenced progress, never accumulated hours (14.9)

| Mastery-approach goals predict intrinsic motivation and lower anxiety; appearance-based performance goals correlate negatively with performance and positively with anxiety.
| Meta-analyses of 243 studies and of induced goals [142]
| Reputation is built on self-referenced mastery and peer recognition; global ranking is secondary and optional (14.9, 17.7)

| Guilds average about 17 members with a median of 9 and dissolve often; commitment rather than membership predicts staying.
| Large-scale guild data [138]; investment model study [135]
| Crews of 4 to 8 inside constellations (17.7)

| People are motivated to expand the self through new activities and identities, and losing an expanded self is disorienting.
| Self-expansion research [136]; serious-leisure framework [143]
| Authored work can never be destroyed by another player: heists take resources and reputation, never creations (9.4, 14.9)

| High monetary incentives crowd out motivation specifically in the most motivated people, and creator earnings concentrate into a tiny elite.
| Experimental economics [144]; platform economic reporting [145]
| Confirms no creator revenue share, and shifts Workshop rewards toward recognition for the long tail (18.7)

| A battle pass had minimal effect on player uptake while spending rose; player themes were grind-versus-spend, elitism and pressure.
| Mixed-methods study of a live game [140]
| Seasons without pressure: nothing expires forever, catch-up built in, no countdowns (18.8)

| Veterans fade over weeks rather than stopping cleanly, and departure can be predicted well in advance.
| Operator analytics, method unpublished [137]
| The signal triggers a legacy card and a graceful off-ramp, never a win-back campaign (14.10)

19.26 The creator payment decision, against the evidence

This is the one place in the document where a decision runs against the direction of its own evidence, so it is recorded plainly rather than dressed up.

| What the evidence says
| What we decided
| How the risk is handled

| Money crowds out motivation in the most motivated people [144], and expected contingent rewards lower later interest [3]
| Pay creators anyway, from a fixed fund
| No promised rate, no live counter, paid in arrears — closer to the unexpected rewards that did not undermine interest in the same meta-analysis [3]

| Creator earnings concentrate into a tiny elite [145]
| Pay the tail, not the elite
| A per-creator cap of 2% of the fund and a minimum payment at 500 completions

| Paid creation can turn a hobby into work
| Accept the risk, measure it
| Output and meaning measured for the top 200 creators before and six months after; the fund is withdrawn if either falls (18.9)

| Payments to minors create legal exposure in a 13+ game
| Payment is 18+ only
| Younger creators keep the full ladder and can claim later; nothing is lost by waiting

Recorded in 27.5 as an assumption we are knowingly making against the grade of the evidence, with a stated failure condition. If it fails, the in-game ladder in 18.7 continues unchanged — which is why that ladder was built first.

19.27 Validating without players

Earlier versions of this section asked for thirty people in a room. That cohort does not exist for this project and waiting for it would stop the work, so the questions stay and the method changes: everything is answered by simulation, by instrumentation, and by the one person who will build it playing it.

What this costs, stated plainly
Simulation can prove a system is legible, balanced and decision-dense. It cannot prove a game is enjoyable — nothing except a person playing can, and that evidence now arrives at soft launch rather than before code. That is a real and accepted loss, and it is the reason the thresholds below are deliberately strict: they are the strongest proxies available, not a substitute for the thing itself. The panels this section used to call for were real and costed [180]; they are dropped for want of people, not for want of value.

| Question
| How it is answered without anyone
| Passes if

| Is the heist tense?
| A harness runs thousands of heists with scripted crews at three skill levels — careless, competent and optimal. Tension is inferred from structure rather than reported: how often a run comes within a second of capture, how many decisions the player faced, how close the clock ran
| A median run carries 15 to 25 decisions (19.2), at least one near-capture in half of runs, and the last ten seconds decide the outcome in a quarter of them

| Is there depth, or only noise?
| The same harness, comparing skill levels. A game with depth separates them; a game without does not
| The optimal crew succeeds at least 35 points more often than the careless one on the same islands. If skill does not separate outcomes, the systems are decoration

| Does delegated control feel like playing?
| Cannot be simulated, and is not asked of strangers. It is answered by the rule in 12.18: the crew never makes a decision the player did not make. The harness checks the rule holds — every state change in a replay traces to a command — rather than asking whether it felt right
| No unattributed decisions in ten thousand replays

| Is the defence fair?
| The ablation method in 13.4: hold rate across all thirty-two layer combinations
| Hold rate 30 to 45%, no layer above a quarter, design beating progression 60/40

| Does the economy hold?
| The day-by-day model re-run whenever a number changes (24)
| Pacing within 10% of the published curve, no runaway faucet at any level

| Does it run on a real phone?
| Unchanged: the spike on real devices (24.2). Needs no players, only two handsets
| Bit-identical checksums, and a build that survives store ingestion

The developer is the first player

The instruments that need a person — the eleven-item experience inventory [181], the appreciation and passion scales — move from before code to after launch, where about one player in seven answers a survey [182] and behaviour is the better witness anyway [183]., and counted as one person rather than a panel. The honest test is whether the person who built it opens it without being paid to. That is weak evidence and is recorded as weak evidence — but it is not nothing, and it arrives every day.

Everything else that once needed a room now waits for soft launch, where the instruments in 14.10 and 25.4 do the asking, on real players, at no cost, and with behaviour rather than opinion. Tension is asked about then, with the three-item scale built for it [221], once there is someone to ask.

20Wellbeing, safety and age

20.1 Audience and rating

Core audience: ages 16 to 34, with 25 to 34 at its heart. Players 35 and older are welcome. People aged 25 to 44 make up about half of all players worldwide, and interest in strategy stays stable with age while the appetite for competition falls, so planning and cooperation keep older players [25].

Why complexity is budgeted: across 1.57 million players surveyed since 2015, interest in long-term planning has declined [25]. The complexity budget in 7.8 answers this.

Rating: 13+ on the App Store, chosen on purpose even though the content alone would likely rate 9+; Apple lets developers set a higher rating to match their minimum age [25]. The matching rating is used on Google Play.

Age check: at first launch the game asks the platform for the player's age band (under 13, 13 to 15, 16 to 17, or 18 and over) instead of a birthdate. Players under 13 are told the game is for 13 and up, and no account is created.

Marketing: aimed at ages 18 to 34, with short clips of heists and law combinations. No marketing aimed at people under 18.

20.2 Protections by age band

| Setting
| 13 to 15
| 16 and 17
| 18 and over

| Chat
| Preset phrases and emotes only
| Preset by default; filtered free chat only after an explicit confirmation
| Filtered free chat

| Profile
| Private
| Private by default
| Player's choice

| Notifications at night
| Never, 21:00 to 08:00
| Never, 22:00 to 08:00
| Off 22:00 to 08:00 by default

| Play-time reminder
| Every 60 minutes
| Every 90 minutes by default
| Optional

| Purchases
| Family approval where set up; €25 a month cap
| €50 a month cap
| Monthly spending summary

| Tracking and advertising
| None
| None
| None; Ruleborn has no ads

20.3 Guardrails for everyone

No paid randomness of any kind, and odds shown for every random reward.

No power, speed or skipped waiting for money.

Nothing decays or weakens while a player is away.

No obligations: missing co-op events, donations or anomalies costs nothing.

No grind: no reward needs weeks of repetitive play, and repeatable rewards have daily and weekly caps.

Losses are capped and results show what was kept.

Every session can end with a short summary and a one-tap "Was this worth your time?" rating.

20.4 Moderation

A word filter in every supported language, for chat, player names and constellation names.

Report and block from any name or message; reports reviewed by a person within 24 hours.

Repeat offenders are muted, then banned. Constellation leaders can mute members.

A part-time community moderator from the beta onward.

20.5 Privacy

Collect only what's needed to run the game; no advertising identifiers; analytics are pseudonymous.

Age bands instead of birthdates. The age of digital consent varies across the EU from 13 to 16 (Portugal's is 13), so consent follows the player's country.

Account and data deletion from inside the game.

20.6 Accessibility and languages

School colours always paired with shapes (7.8), and palettes tested for the common forms of colour blindness.

Adjustable text size, left-handed controls, reduced motion, switchable haptics, and no information carried by sound alone.

Languages at launch: English and Portuguese (Portugal and Brazil). Spanish, French and German next.

20.7 Creation and sharing by age band

| Feature
