20.8 Accessibility in full

Roughly a fifth to a third of players report a disability, depending on the survey, and platform holders now count hundreds of millions of players with disabilities [53]. Accessibility is therefore both right and a serious part of the reach a game of this ambition needs.

| Area
| What Ruleborn does

| Seeing
| Every school has a shape as well as a colour, and every note a symbol; palettes tested for the three common forms of colour blindness; text size adjustable to 200%; a high-contrast mode; no information carried by colour alone; law names always written out, never only as icons.

| Hearing
| No information carried by sound alone; every alarm and cue has a visual and a haptic form; captions for the few spoken lines; separate volume for music, effects and alerts.

| Moving
| No action needs more than one finger; nothing needs a fast tap, a double tap or a drag that can't also be done as two taps; controls can be mirrored for left-handed play and moved; hold times adjustable; the heist can be paused at any time in ruins and rehearsals.

| Understanding
| Plain language throughout (1.4); one new idea per session (15); every rule visible on screen when it matters; a slower mode for ruins that runs the simulation at 75% speed; no reading speed requirements on banners, which stay until dismissed if the player chooses.

| Comfort
| Reduced-motion mode removes swoops and most particles; no flashing above three flashes a second; screen shake can be switched off; haptics adjustable or off.

| Screen readers
| Every menu, card and result works with VoiceOver and TalkBack; the universe map can be read realm by realm.

| Testing
| Players with disabilities are paid participants in playtests from the vertical slice onward, not only at the end.

| Honesty
| The store page lists exactly which accessibility features exist, so nobody buys into a barrier.

20.9 Why minors default to preset chat

No study directly compares preset-phrase chat with filtered free chat for minors — a real gap. What exists is prevalence: one in three boys aged 9 to 12 reported an online sexual interaction in 2024 [151], 40% of minors report being approached by someone they believed was trying to manipulate them [152], and recorded offences of sexual communication with a child rose 89% over six years in one country's police data [153]. The recurring pattern in every safety authority's guidance is that offenders move the conversation off-platform, which free text enables and preset phrases do not.

Everyone under 18 starts on preset chat. 16 and 17 year olds can move to filtered free chat after an explicit confirmation; 13 to 15 cannot.

Known material is caught by hash-matching, which is cheap and reliable; classifiers, which run at 70 to 97% accuracy depending on the task [154], add coverage for new material, with human review behind them.

Reporting is prominent, because even among harmed minors only about half use a reporting feature [152].

Moderation protects new players first. Analysing 2.5 million players and 18.25 million matches, toxic teammates significantly predicted newcomers quitting but had no significant effect on players past level 30 [155]. Moderation effort is spent where it changes outcomes.

20.10 The cheapest safe configuration

Three design decisions remove most of the moderation cost and nearly all of the legal exposure, and they are recorded here as decisions rather than as savings.

| Decision
| What it avoids

| No player-uploaded images, ever. Workshop islands are structured data — laws, terrain, guards, layout — not files
| Hosting images creates a legal duty to detect and report child sexual abuse material. The detection services that exist are priced for platforms, not for one person [184]. Designing images out removes the duty and the bill together

| Preset-phrase chat for everyone under 18 (20.2)
| Most grooming risk, most moderation volume and most consent machinery

| No advertising SDKs and no cross-app tracking
| The tracking prompt, most consent requirements, and the hardest parts of the privacy declarations

Free automated moderation runs in front of any player-written text; a paid service is only needed if free chat is ever opened, and is cheap per message even then [185].

Support is self-service first: an in-game help section and a ticket form. Deflection of roughly 40 to 70% is normal when self-service is done well [186].

Volunteers may triage reports inside their constellation; they never see personal data and never decide bans.

What cannot be avoided: truthful privacy declarations on both stores, a real privacy policy and terms, answering deletion requests, the age gate, and tax filing.

20.11 What the stores and the law require, all of which is free to build

| Obligation
| What it means here

| Apple's user-content rules
| A filter on published content, a way to report it, action on a report within 24 hours including removing the content and the account behind it, a way to block another player, published contact details, and account deletion inside the app [194]

| Google's user-content policy
| Ongoing moderation, in-app reporting and blocking, a terms-of-use gate, and a truthful data-safety declaration

| The European Digital Services Act
| A notice-and-action route, a reason given to anyone whose content is restricted, a single point of contact and clear terms. The heavier platform duties do not apply to a micro-enterprise [195]

| Children's privacy law
| A neutral age gate, no accounts below 13, and immediate deletion of anything collected from someone who turns out to be younger. Penalties are per-violation and large, so the gate is not decorative [196]

None of this costs money. All of it costs attention, and the 24-hour rule in particular is a commitment one person must be able to keep — which is another reason the Workshop carries no images and chat carries no free text.

20.12 Islands of players who leave

| Case
| What happens to their islands

| An account is deleted
| Personal details are erased or severed. The island itself — terrain, guards, laws — is not personal data once the name is gone, so it may remain as an unnamed derelict in the ruins, or be withdrawn entirely at the player's request. Erasure completes within thirty days, backups included, with a log kept

| An account is banned
| Their islands leave matchmaking and the Workshop immediately. The minimum identifiers needed to enforce the ban are kept, which the law allows

| Limits on publishing
| Between five and ten published islands per account, with a storage quota per account. Islands are small structured data (20.10), so the quota is generous — but it is enforced at the point of upload, because it is also the cost control

21Look and feel

A guide for Claude Design and for building the world in Godot: how the islands, buildings, characters, laws and interface look, move and sound. The principle behind all of it: every rule must be visible, and everything must read at thumb size.

21.1 Direction and camera

| Topic
| Rule

| Style
| Picture-book dioramas floating in a violet sky: chunky, soft-edged, toy-like shapes; warm light on the islands and cool shadows beneath; gentle physical humour in every animation.

| Readability
| Silhouette first. Every character, building and law signature must be recognisable as a dark shape at 32 points tall.

| Home camera
| An orthographic diorama view at a 35° pitch in a tall frame, rotatable in 90° steps, with three zoom levels: the whole universe, one realm, and a close-up. The universe is arranged as a vertical column of islands rather than a wide row, so it fits a portrait screen. Islands hang over the sky with roots and waterfalls beneath.

| Heist camera
| A closer follow camera at 50° pitch showing about 12 tiles across; a one-second swoop between realms; pinch to see a whole realm (12.6).

| Scale
| One tile is one metre. Ruleborn stand 0.5 to 1.4 metres, Sages about 1.5, buildings 2 to 6 metres. A realm is 10 to 14 tiles across, with a rim of cliffs and hanging roots.

| Screen
| Portrait-first, designed for 9:19.5, one-handed, with every frequently used control inside comfortable thumb reach in the lower two-thirds. Heists can be rotated to landscape for a wider view. Primary controls are at least 9 mm across — about 48 points — because real-world tap error only flattens near 9 to 10 mm and rises above 40% below 8 mm, measured across 120 million touch events [174]. Nothing interactive sits in a screen corner, where error rates are worst. Safe areas respected; nothing important within 44 points of the notch or home bar.

21.2 Colour and shape

| School
| Main colour
| Shadow tint
| Shape
| Where the shape appears

| Motion
| Sun gold #F2B43C
| #B8741E

Neutrals: island stone #D8CFC2, soil #8A6A4E, wood #B07A4F, ink #221C3A, paper #FCFBFF.

Sky: day #9FB9F0 to #F6D9C8; dusk #6A4FC9 to #F29C7B; night #1A1433 to #3A2B6B. The sky follows the player's local time, and Day and night overrides it inside its realm.

Notes use the colours of the wheel in 8.1, each with its symbol (wave, anchor, sun, crescent, sprout, star, ring, mask).

Colour-blind check: every pair of schools must stay distinguishable by shape alone, and palettes are tested for the three common forms of colour blindness (20.6).

21.3 Rendering and performance

Shading: a three-step toon ramp with coloured shadows tinted toward violet, a soft rim light, and 1.5-pixel ink outlines. Hand-painted gradient textures only; no photographic textures. The toon, outline, water, waterfall and sky shaders already exist from the mock-ups.

Effects: law signatures use particles, vertex animation and screen-space tints, never full-screen post-processing on the phone.

Budgets: characters 3,000 to 6,000 triangles, buildings 2,000 to 8,000, a whole realm 25,000 to 40,000, under 150 draw calls on screen.

Frame rate: 60 frames a second on iPhone 12 and newer, never below 30 on the oldest supported phone.

21.6 Characters

Shape, to the cuteness rules: 1.7 to 2.9 heads tall — ten of the eighteen sit in the two-to-two-and-a-half core, four below it and four above, which is what gives 12.21 its small, standard and large bands; the skull wider than the jaw; eyes at or below the vertical midline of the face, each at least a fifth of face width, set wide apart; small nose and mouth; short limbs; a rounded silhouette with no sharp points. These proportions reliably raise perceived cuteness and the urge to look after something, and the effect works on animals and invented creatures as much as on infants (19.19). Each ruleborn's school colour is its main colour, and its school shape appears as a marking.

Role accessories: Scouts wear goggles, Wardens carry a shield, Breakers a hammer or horn, Carriers a big pack, Menders a satchel of leaves. As guards, ruleborn add a sash and a small lantern.

Guards on screen: a soft fan of light shows each guard's vision cone, and a pip over its head fills white, then yellow, then red with awareness.

Animation set: two idles, walk, run, carry, climb, leap, slide, wade, ability, hit, knocked out (dizzy stars, then a puff of petals as it goes home), celebrate, nap, and one work loop per building.

Every creature passes the squint test: recognisable from its silhouette alone, in black, at 32 points. A form that only differs by colour has failed.

Forms: each of the eight forms (11.10) changes a ruleborn's pattern, glow and one accessory in its note's colour, using shared materials, so 144 forms need 18 models, not 144.

Laws as modifiers: law signatures are procedural changes to the shared animations (gravity, speed, lean, squash and stretch, time scale, trails), so 48 laws don't need 48 sets of animation. Each body exaggerates them differently: the lighter the body, the bigger the effect.

21.7 Laws on screen

Each law has a world signature (how its realm looks) and a movement signature (how characters move). Both are always on while the law is woven, so an observant player can read an island without opening a menu.

| Law
| How the realm looks
| How characters move

| Bounce
| Walls wobble like jelly when touched.
| Squash-and-stretch rebounds off walls with a springy boing.

| Half gravity
| Pebbles hover a hand above the ground; dust falls slowly.
| Floaty strides and long, slow hops; cloaks and loot sacks drift upward.

| Tilt
| Trees, lanterns and banners lean one way; water runs downhill.
| Arms windmill running downhill; bodies lean hard going uphill.

| Current
| Grass waves and leaves stream in one direction.
| Bodies lean into the wind; hair and cloaks stream; steps lengthen downwind.

| Heavy ground
| Grass lies flat; footprints sink deep.
| Stomping steps with bent knees; carriers hunch under loot; flyers walk.

| Leapfrog
| Springy turf covered in hop marks.
| A step-step-hop rhythm; each landing puffs dust.

| Frictionless
| Glossy ground with reflections.
| Skating glides with arms out for balance; spin-stops against walls.

| Inward pull
| Faint spiral streaks lead toward the Heart.
| A slight drift toward the Heart; feet drag when heading outward.

| Brittle stone
| Hairline cracks everywhere; crumbs fall from walls.
| Careful tiptoeing; wobbles on cracked tiles.

| Crystal growth
| Crystals sprout where walls broke and hum softly.
| Characters pause to tap humming crystal walls.

| Glass
| Walls turn clear with coloured edges.
| Characters crouch behind glass walls, in vain.

| Density
| Grass and props look slightly oversized.
| Big characters lumber in slow motion; small ones scurry double-time.

| Honeycomb
| A golden hexagon sheen covers the ground.
| Sticky steps pull golden strands; characters scale walls like geckos.

| Lodestones
| Iron filings fan out around each lodestone.
| Carriers are tugged sideways; sacks strain toward the stones.

| Shifting stones
| Walls glide along grooves with a grinding rumble.
| Characters wait at gaps, then dart through.

| Transmute
| Loot glints and shifts colour.
| Carriers juggle sacks that flicker between gold and green.

| Day and night
| The sky flips between day and night.
| In the dark phase, characters hold lanterns high and tread carefully.

| Slow rivers
| Water flows like syrup; drops hang in the air.
| Slow-motion wading with big, lazy splashes.

| Stillness
| The air is calm; not a leaf moves.
| Frozen statue poses with a shimmering outline.

| Afterimage
| Faint ghost frames linger where people passed.
| Translucent copies trail every character.

| Delay
| Ripples linger around anything triggered.
| Characters flinch a beat late; effects land after a pause.

| Haste
| Clouds race overhead; plants sway quickly.
| Sped-up strides with motion lines and little skips.

| Held breath
| Floating dust hangs still in the air.
| When the crew stops, everything freezes mid-motion; the crew breathes in deeply.

| Rewind
| Broken pieces whoosh back where they were.
| Characters glance back as debris flies back into place.

| Glowing dark
| A black realm lit by glowing mushrooms and runes.
| Glowing characters tiptoe; everyone else is a silhouette.

| Heat bloom
| Heat haze around braziers.
| Characters near fire puff up and swing faster, steam rising.

| Spark trails
| Sparkles fade slowly on the ground.
| Runners leave crackling sparks; walkers tiptoe to stay dark.

| Aurora
| Ribbons of light sweep across the ground.
| Characters wait at the edge of a light band, then dash.

| Chain lightning
| Small arcs between metal props.
| Hair stands on end; arcs jump between neighbours when hit.

| Static
| Crackling motes over the ground.
| Fur and hair frizz with every step; a zap pose on contact.

| Overcharge
| Pylons pulse in threes.
| Every third ability animation doubles with an afterflash.

| Sunburst
| A sun flare every 25 seconds; shade patches are marked.
| Characters duck into shade at the warning glint.

| Bloom
| Hedges sprout back in time-lapse.
| Characters push through hedges that close behind them.

| Kinship
| Vines braid between neighbours.
| Allies walk shoulder to shoulder, linked by a green thread.

| Wild paths
| Flowers bloom wherever people walk.
| Characters skip along flower paths.

| Flock
| Fluffy puffballs graze across the realm.
| Characters weave and shush their way through bleating puffballs.

| Spores
| Pollen clouds drift lazily.
| Drowsy yawns, slow blinks and stumbles.

| Thorns
| Thorny vines cover walls and hedges.
| Characters flinch away from walls and hop around hedges.

| Regrowth
| Moss creeps over everything.
| Guards' wounds glow green and close as they walk.

| Swarm birth
| Seed pods cluster around guard posts.
| Knocked-out guards pop into two tiny guards that toddle after the crew.

| Haunting
| Ghostly footprints trail anyone carrying loot.
| Carriers glance nervously at the glowing trail behind them.

| Soul lanterns
| Warm lantern pools in cold blue dusk.
| Characters hurry between pools of lantern light and flicker when outside them.

| Whispers
| Whispering wind lines drift between guards.
| Guards cup their ears; an alarm travels as a visible wave.

| Echoes
| Soft chimes; faint silhouettes.
| Knocked-out characters rise as translucent echoes that float a little.

| Sleepwalk
| Dreamy haze with drifting Z-shaped motes.
| Guards shuffle with arms outstretched and eyes closed; the crew tiptoes theatrically.

| Soul swap
| Wisps orbit every character.
| A magenta swirl as two characters trade places.

| Borrowed Face
| Festival lanterns and confetti.
| Everyone wears a mask; the crew copies the guards' marching step.

| Mirror images
| Mirror glints in the corners of the eye.
| Guards shimmer; illusions pop into sparkles when touched.

Law card: the school shape and colour at the top, the law's name, a looping four-second preview of its movement signature, a one-line rule, notes as symbols, rank and tier pips.

Law banner: slides in at the top left for two seconds when a crew enters a realm: shape, name and one line.

Harmony on screen: a thread in the note's colour links the two pylons; a dissonance is a jagged, flickering line; a chord forms a slowly turning triangle of light between three pylons.

21.8 Interface

| Topic
| Rule

| Type
| Body text is at least 16 pixels, and a line carries at least 13 characters, below which reading speed falls sharply [175]. Contrast meets 4.5:1, checked against a perceptual model as well, because the standard formula overstates contrast on dark backgrounds. A rounded, friendly sans (Baloo 2) for labels and numbers, with tabular numerals; Marcellus for titles and law names. Nothing smaller than 13 points.

| Colours
| Ink #221C3A on paper panels at 85% opacity over the world; gold for main actions, red #E2536B for alarms, green #3FBF7F for success.

| Home layout
| Resources with storage bars at the top left; Keeper level and Renown at the top right; the stability band in the top centre; build, weave and terrain tools at the bottom left; a large skiff-shaped Heist button at the bottom right; alerts down the left edge.

| Heist layout
| Clock and alarm meter at the top centre; loot and Vault progress at the top right; the law banner at the top left; crew portraits at the bottom left; Sage, cast and Get out at the bottom right.

| Icons
| Rounded shapes with a 2-point outline, flat colour and the school shape where relevant; drawn on a 24-point grid.

| Motion
| 150 to 250 milliseconds, easing out; buttons squash slightly when pressed; rewards fly into their counter. Screen shake never above 4 pixels. Reduced motion removes swoops and most particles.

| Haptics
| A light tap on buttons, a medium pulse on abilities and pylon breaks, a success pattern on escape, a warning pattern on alarm.

| Sound
| Treated as a fun variable, not decoration: sound and music raise enjoyment, flow and immersion, with a small to moderate effect on how positive players feel [71] [72]. Music, effects and alerts have separate volumes, and music can be turned off without losing information, since music a player dislikes lowers immersion [71]. Each school has an instrument: flute for Motion, glass marimba for Matter, celesta and ticking for Time, plucked electric strings for Energy, kalimba and birdsong for Life, choir and bells for Spirit. The music adds a layer for each school in the current realm, and percussion when an alarm sounds. Every school has a sound signature — six in all — so a player can hear which kind of rule is running with the screen half-watched. Per-law sounds were claimed in earlier versions and never authored: the asset budget below has six, and six is the design, and every reveal is teased with a two-second cue before it lands (19.16).

21.9 Asset list for launch

| Characters: ruleborn, Sages, visitors, paradox-born, anomalies
| 18, 4, 3, 3, 3

| 64

| 48 and 3

21.10 The two tests every realm and screen must pass

The grayscale thumbnail test. Shrink a screenshot to thumbnail size and remove the colour: the realm should still be identifiable and the important elements still readable. Value structure first, colour second.

Five-second recognisability. A one-second clip of any realm should be named correctly by most players. Each realm owns a distinct value range, hue territory and soundscape (21.4).

Hero features against ambient ones. Of the 39 terrain features, only the handful that change a route are drawn to be individually read; the rest are texture. Search time rises roughly 28 to 45 milliseconds per item when things must be told apart [176], so most of the board must not need telling apart.

21.11 Made, not licensed

Nothing in this game is paid for. The world is generated in code — meshes, shaders, palettes, the music layers — which is both the art direction (21.1) and the licensing position: there is no third party to credit, no attribution chain to maintain and nothing that can be withdrawn.

| Where something must come from outside
| Rule

| Fonts
| Open Font Licence only, which explicitly permits embedding in an application and does not affect the game's own licence [197]

| Sound, if any is not generated
| Public-domain dedication only. No attribution licences, no share-alike, no non-commercial clauses

| Code
| Permissive licences only — the engine itself is one, and charges no royalty

| Everything else
| Made for this game

The traps this avoids
"Royalty-free" means no fee per use, not free and not without obligations. Attribution licences create a chain that must be kept accurate forever. Share-alike can reach into what you build with it. Non-commercial licences cannot be used in a game that earns anything. Many typefaces are licensed for documents and forbid embedding in software. A licence ledger records the source, the licence and the link for every outside file, and it is the only defence if any of this is ever questioned. The ledger's first entries are the two typefaces — Baloo 2 for interface text and Marcellus for titles — both under the open font licence, which permits embedding in a commercial application without affecting the game's own licence, provided the licence text ships with it. It does, in the legal page of the settings.

21.12 Showing what a guard can see

The detection model in 12.17 is only fair if it is visible. The rules that make it readable are borrowed from the stealth games that solved this on small screens [222].

One shape at a time. The selected guard's vision is drawn; the others show only their facing. Five overlapping cones on a phone is noise.

Sharp edges, no gradients. A tile is inside or outside. Soft fades hide exactly the information the player needs.

The fill travels. When a guard begins to notice something, the fill moves visibly from its eyes toward the crew — so the player sees how much time is left rather than reading a number.

Every guard shares the same shapes, scaled by archetype, so the rule is learned once rather than five times.

Tall grass, shade and cover are drawn as ground, not as effects, and are legible in a still screenshot.

22Monetisation

Money buys looks and a season pass. It never buys power, speed or chance.

22.1 What is and isn't sold

Sold

Starglass, in packs from about €1 to €50

The season pass premium track (cosmetic), about €6 a season

Cosmetic sets: realm skins, sky themes, bridge styles, outfits, Sage robes, emotes, law visuals

Occasional supporter packs of cosmetics

Never sold

Resources, pages or laws

Characters, traits or Sages

Skipping rest, experiments or weaving

Shields or heist retries

Random boxes of any kind

Every price is shown in real money before purchase, with no randomness, which keeps Ruleborn clear of loot-box rules in Europe and of App Store disclosure requirements. Players under 18 have monthly spending caps (20.2).

22.2 The business case, as assumptions to test

Monthly revenue = monthly players × share who buy × average monthly spend per buyer, minus the store's commission (Apple takes 15% from developers earning under $1 million a year and 30% above that; EU terms differ)

Illustrative scenarios, not forecasts. Every input must be measured in soft launch.

| Scenario
| Monthly players
| Share who buy
| Spend per buyer
| Monthly revenue after commission

| Low
| 20,000
| 2%
| €8
| about €2,700

| Base
| 100,000
| 3%
| €10
| about €25,500

| High
| 500,000
| 4%
| €12
| about €168,000

| The high scenario is computed at 30% commission rather than 15%, because €204,000 a month crosses the small-business threshold in 22.5. The discontinuity is real: revenue between roughly €70,000 and €85,000 a month is partly a dead zone

Costs to estimate before the vertical slice: servers and backend, contract art (characters and interface), audio, community moderation, a one-off legal and privacy review, soft-launch marketing, and the Apple Developer fee.

Launch gates
Go from soft launch to global launch only if, after two rounds of tuning: at least 8% of players are still playing after 30 days, the average "worth your time" rating is at least 4 out of 5, and at least 2% of monthly players buy something.

Risk
Cosmetic-only games earn less per player than pay-to-win strategy games, so Ruleborn needs more players who stay longer. The model works when the game spreads by word of mouth, and when cosmetics are desirable because other players see them in every heist.

22.3 The scale a mega-hit would need

$2 billion a year ≈ $5.5 million a day = 18 million daily players × $0.30, or 37 million × $0.15 (illustrative; cosmetic-only games are likely nearer $0.15, to be measured)
With about a third of monthly players playing each day: 60 to 120 million monthly players

22.4 Gifting

Players can gift cosmetics to friends, with a monthly limit and parental approval rules for minors. Gifting makes spending social rather than competitive, and social reasons are among the strongest reasons players give for buying (19.9).

22.5 What the stores take, and what they handle

15%, not 30%. Both stores run small-business programmes that halve commission below a million dollars a year, and a new developer qualifies automatically [187]. Every revenue figure in 22.2 assumes 15%.

The stores are the seller of record. They calculate, collect and remit sales tax and EU VAT on store sales, so there is no need to register for VAT across Europe to sell the game.

United States withholding is handled by filing the standard treaty form; Portugal has a treaty, so the default 30% withholding does not apply once it is filed.

The entire unavoidable bill is $99 a year to Apple and $25 once to Google [196]. No domain is required, since both stores accept a free hosted page for the privacy policy and support link. There is no trade mark fee, no licence fee, no subscription and no middleware (24.5).

What this means for the model: infrastructure is a rounding error against store commission at every scale in 22.2. The business risk is reach, not running costs — this game cannot be killed by its own bills.

23Screens to design

The list for Claude Design, in order of importance.

23.1 The screens as they look now

Six screens rendered from the Godot project, portrait, at phone resolution. They are mock-ups, not the built game: the world is real 3D with the shading, islands, buildings and creatures described in 21, and the interface over it is the real layout at the real size. Everything here is drawn or modelled in code, so any screen can be re-rendered after a design change.

Islands. The universe as a portrait column. Resources and strain at the top, the closest goal and the two main actions in the thumb zone, five tabs at the foot.Realm. One island close up: the Spire, houses, paths, lanterns, law pylons with their zones. Law chips float over the pylons they belong to.Landing. Open edges. Green markers are silent landings, red ones are heard. The defender cannot choose where you arrive (5.4).Heist. Eight controls, all within thumb reach: four crew, Sage, cast and Get out. The law banner names the rule that is running.Result. The moment that decided it, with a three-second replay, then one suggestion, then the worth-your-time tap (12.13).Workshop. The Workshop with its tool rail, the creator ladder, and publishing (18.6, 18.7).

Known gaps in these renders: no ambient occlusion, depth of field or soft global illumination, because the renderer used here has no graphics hardware behind it; trees and creatures are still primitives rather than modelled assets; and nothing is animated. All three close on a machine with a GPU.

23.3 Orientation

Portrait is the default on phones; landscape is available everywhere and remembered per device.

| Reason
| Evidence

| Portrait is the only orientation a thumb can play
| About half of people hold a phone one-handed, and roughly three quarters of all interactions are thumb-driven [103]

| It matches a 5 to 9 minute session in a queue or on a train
| Our own session design (14.1) and the thumb-zone limits in 21.8

| The growing strategy games are portrait
| Last War, Whiteout Survival and Kingshot are portrait and won by widening the funnel — simpler interface, easier onboarding, less punishment — the same moves in this document [104] [105]

| Marketing is vertical
| A 9:16 video covers 75% of the screen on YouTube and all of it fullscreen, and adding vertical assets lifted conversions by more than 35% on Shorts [106]; playable ads convert far better than video [107], and a playable is a slice of the real game, so a portrait game needs no rework

Rules that make supporting both safe

Same information in both. Landscape never reveals more of an island than portrait, or rotating becomes an advantage. The board is designed to a portrait-safe area; landscape shows the same tiles, larger, with the interface moved to the sides.

Phones default to portrait; tablets and the later PC version default to landscape. The choice is remembered per device.

Never rotate mid-heist. Rotation is deliberate, suggested once after the third heist.

Cost: about 15 to 20% more interface work, concentrated in the heist HUD. The 3D world only needs a different camera frame.

Landscape is genuinely better for seeing a route at once and for two-thumb play, and it is native on tablets, which is why it ships as a real option rather than a compatibility mode. Which one players actually choose is question 9 in the fun backlog (19.17).

23.4 Making the concept legible in five seconds

Novelty is a commercial liability unless it is anchored. Across 51,411 songs, every kind of novelty was negatively associated with popularity, at roughly 5 to 6% per unit of uniqueness [159]. Ruleborn is a genuinely novel concept that fits no clean genre tag, which is a discoverability problem before it is a design one.

| Rule
| What it means

| Anchor first, differentiate second
| Signal the familiar — creatures you raise, islands you build, a heist you run — in the first five seconds. The laws are the novelty and they come after the hook

| Show, never explain
| The first screenshot is an island with a visible rule and a crew mid-heist, not a menu. No vocabulary in the first two sentences of the description

| One dominant framing
| The game is pitched as one thing, not four. The collection, the Workshop and the clans are discovered later, not pitched

| Tested, not assumed
| Five-second comprehension tests on the store page, and store-listing experiments on real traffic (26.4)

24Technical logic

| Area
| Approach

| Engine and languages
| Godot 4.5 for the client shell, interface and rendering, in GDScript. The heist simulation itself is a separate Rust library with no engine types in it, compiled into the app on the phone and to WebAssembly on the server, so both run the same code (24.7). Portrait-first, landscape optional in heists. One project for iPhone and Android.

| Heist simulation
| Fixed 30 steps a second, fixed-point numbers and a seeded random generator, so the same inputs always give the same result. The same code runs on the phone, on the server, in rehearsals and in balance testing.

| Heist validation
| The phone sends its taps with their timing; the server replays the heist against the defender's snapshot and settles loot, Renown and pages.

| Balance testing
| Automated crews with scripted tactics and a search-based planner run thousands of heists against generated universes before each release. Targets: no law in more than 25% of top defences, success rates inside the bands in 14.5, and page income within 10% of 14.4.

| Echo universes
| Snapshots of real universes kept for 14 days and offered when real targets are scarce; loot from echoes is created by the server.

| Server
| Authoritative for every resource change, heists, matchmaking, constellations and purchases. A game backend such as Nakama or a small custom service.

| Content as data
| Laws, resonances, ruleborn, traits, buildings, eras and anomalies live in data tables on the server, so content and balance ship without an app update.

| Age and safety
| Age bands from the platform (Apple's Declared Age Range and Google Play's age signals) set the protections in 20.2. Chat passes through a filter service with human review for reports.

| Analytics
| Events for sessions, heists (outcome, laws crossed, crew), weaving, births and purchases, plus the "worth your time" rating and the monthly survey in 25.

| Offline play
| Building, weaving and assigning work offline, confirmed on reconnection. Heists need a connection.

| No AI at runtime
| Guards and crews use hand-written rules, not AI services.

| Economy model
| A Python model of every cost, production rate and income source simulates engaged, regular and casual players day by day. It generated the tables in 10.2 and 14, and is re-run whenever a number changes.

| Harmony resolver
| Each law's notes are data. Harmonies, dissonances and chords are worked out per realm whenever laws are woven, and stored in that realm's rule sheet.

| Island Workshop
| Published islands are stored as data (terrain, laws, buildings, guards), validated by the same simulation, scanned automatically, and served through curated and rated lists.

| Clips
| The deterministic replay is rendered on the phone into a 15-second vertical video; no video is stored on the server unless shared.

| Instant play
| A small version of the game that can open a challenge link without a full install, where the platform's size limits allow.

| Cross-progression
| One account across iPhone, iPad, Android and later PC, with platform sign-in.

| Ruin difficulty
| Each ruin is built at the difficulty the player picked, from a pool weighted toward laws and terrain that player has not met recently (12.12, 15.5). No model changes a chosen difficulty.

| Co-op heists (1.1)
| The deterministic simulation lets two or three phones share inputs with the server as referee.

| Replay validation
| The server re-simulates submitted heists from their seed and input log and rejects any result that does not reproduce. Validation is sampled, not universal: every high-value haul, every league-affecting result, everything disputed, and a random share of the rest. Re-simulating all of them would exceed the per-request compute budget and the cost floor for no security gain, since a cheat that is only caught sometimes is still caught [200]. The same golden replays double as the regression tests that fail the build when the simulation changes [163].

| Bot detection
| Behavioural anomaly detection on crew choice and input timing, added when botting is observed rather than pre-emptively. Published behavioural detectors reach 96 to 98% accuracy on real game data [164].

| Input budget
| Under 25 ms from touch to picture for dragging, which is where performance starts to degrade; taps tolerate about 69 ms [165]. 60 frames a second is a floor, because 30 measurably harms performance while differences above 60 are not significant [166].

| Reference device
| A mid-range 4 GB Android at 60 Hz, not a flagship. Android is 81 to 95% of every target market, and the low-end price tier is the largest segment [167]. Install size is minimised because size gates installation in exactly those markets.

| Dependency map
| Notes are the highest-centrality objects in the system: each touches many laws, every resonance and every chord. Changing a note propagates everywhere; changing a law does not [168]. The note count is therefore frozen at eight, and laws are the expansion axis.

24.1 The backend, and what it costs

The asynchronous, deterministic design turns out to be the cheapest architecture a multiplayer game can have, and that was luck rather than judgement. A heist is a seed and an input log of a few kilobytes, the device renders the replay itself, and the server only re-simulates to check. There are no real-time servers, no held connections, no video and no uploaded files. The costs that dominate live games are absent rather than reduced.

| Layer
| Choice
| Why

| Static delivery
| Immutable, content-addressed island bundles on zero-egress object storage, cached at the edge for a year
| Most of what a player downloads never changes, so the network serves it and the origin is barely touched. Bandwidth is free on this storage, which is the single largest saving in the design [190]

| Dynamic tier
| Edge functions against a managed serverless SQL database. No virtual machine, no operating system, nothing to patch
| Publishing, validation, league tables and clan state are write-light. The paid plan is $5 a month and includes 25 billion row reads and 50 million row writes — several times what a million players generate [199]

| Object storage
| Replays and snapshots at about $0.015 per gigabyte a month, first 10 GB free [177] [178]
| A million islands at ten kilobytes each is ten gigabytes. Storage is not a meaningful cost here [190]

| Push
| Firebase Cloud Messaging
| Free and unlimited, with no per-message charge at any scale

| Analytics, flags, errors
| First-party events in our own database, a static configuration file on the edge, and a free-tier error reporting service
| No subscriptions. A remote-config service is a file we can host ourselves

| Moderation
| Deterministic word lists, rate limits, reports and mutes — no machine learning anywhere
| Preset-phrase chat means free text cannot be composed, so there is nothing for a classifier to classify (20.10)

Monthly running cost in euros, from published free-tier limits and prices. Engineering estimates, not quotes.

| Monthly players
| 1,000
| 10,000
| 100,000
| 1,000,000

| Egress
| 1 to 2 GB
| ~15 GB
| ~150 GB
| 0.75 to 1.5 TB

| Infrastructure
| €0
| €0
| about $5
| $5 to $15

| Unavoidable cash
| $99 a year to Apple, $25 once to Google. That is the whole recurring bill (24.5)

The dominant cost of this game is the Apple fee, not its servers. At a million monthly players the infrastructure is about $5 to $15 a month, because the paid allowances are larger than anything this design generates.

What actually costs money: database writes first, then origin compute. Telemetry is batched rather than written per event, and snapshots expire after 30 to 90 days.

Where the free tier breaks: the daily request and row-write allowances, in the low tens of thousands of players. The answer is the $5 paid plan, whose included volumes then carry the game to a million [191] — not a machine of our own.

Budget: about 100 to 200 kilobytes and five writes per active player per day. That is the design constraint, not an estimate made afterwards (24.4).

24.2 Determinism is load-bearing, and must be proved first

The first thing to build
Anti-cheat, replay validation, free analytics and the whole cost model all rest on the simulation producing bit-identical results everywhere. Floating-point arithmetic differs across processors, so the simulation uses fixed-point or integer maths throughout, and the first technical task — before art, before content — is proving a heist replays identically on an ARM phone and an x86 server. If it does not, every one of those advantages disappears at once.

24.3 Analytics we get for nothing

Heatmaps without logging positions. Because a replay can be re-simulated, route, detection and failure heatmaps are generated server-side from a sample of stored logs. The client never sends coordinates, which removes the largest telemetry cost in most games [179].

Replay analysis is already paid for. The replays kept for validation are the same ones used to watch what a churned or failed player actually did.

First-party only. No advertising identifiers, no third-party trackers, no cross-app tracking. This keeps the privacy declarations simple and honest, and avoids the tracking prompt entirely (20.5).

Short retention. Raw replays and logs are kept 30 to 90 days, long enough for validation and analysis, then aggregated and discarded.

24.4 Static first: why this costs nothing

Almost nothing in Ruleborn needs a server that thinks. An island is structured data, a heist is a seed and an input log, and an opponent is a snapshot rather than a person. So the game is delivered the way a website is delivered.

| Technique
| What it does

| Immutable, content-addressed bundles
| Many islands are packed into one file named by its hash and cached for a year. The edge serves it; the origin is almost never touched.

| Binary and dictionary compression
| Structured island data packed as binary and compressed with a trained dictionary, since every island shares the same shape. Five to ten times smaller than plain text [191].

| Seeds, never replays
| A heist is a few kilobytes whatever happens in it. Nothing is ever recorded as video.

| Generated, not transmitted
| The world's art and sound are made in code on the device (21.1), so those bytes never cross the network at all.

| The platform holds the save
| Progress syncs through iCloud and Google Play Games saved games, which are free to us and use the player's own storage.

| Egress-free storage
| Bundles are served from object storage that charges nothing for bandwidth, which is also what keeps the arrangement inside the provider's terms [192].

The byte budget. A session pulls about ten opponent islands at roughly ten kilobytes each, occasionally publishes its own, and sends a few heist logs: on the order of 150 kilobytes per active player per day. At a million monthly players, with a quarter of them active daily, that is about a terabyte a month — which on egress-free storage costs nothing. A million published islands is about ten gigabytes.

The dynamic tier, which is very small

Four things genuinely need code running somewhere: publishing an island, validating a submitted heist by re-simulation, the league tables, and constellation state. All are write-light. A managed serverless SQL database handles this at a million monthly players inside the $5 plan's included allowances, with automatic point-in-time recovery and nothing for us to run [193]. Validation is done in batches rather than instantly, because an asynchronous game does not need an answer in the same second.

24.5 The irreducible cost

| Cost
| Amount
| Recurs?

| Apple developer programme
| $99 a year
| Yes — the only unavoidable recurring cost of the whole project

| Google Play registration
| $25
| Once

| Privacy policy and terms
| Nothing
| Written by us, hosted free

| Domain name
| Optional
| Both stores accept a free hosted page for policy and support

| Infrastructure at a million monthly players
| About $5 to $15 a month
| Yes, and it is the smaller half of the bill

The honest statement of this project's economics: the servers are not the cost. The Apple fee is. What this configuration gives up is not reliability but control: we depend on one platform, and validation and league tables update on a cadence rather than instantly. The dependency is mitigated by keeping the data model in plain SQL and the game logic in the client, so the whole backend could be rehosted in a weekend. The cadence is a deliberate choice, not a limitation.

Three things we will not buy

Any machine-learning service in the runtime. No moderation classifier, no recommendation model, no generated content at run time. Everything is deterministic code, which is also what makes replays reproducible (24.2).

Any subscription tool where a static file on the edge will do: remote config is a JSON file, feature flags are fields in it, analytics are our own rows, and error reporting is self-hosted or the platform's own free reports.

Any paid licence. See 21.11.

24.6 Why serverless is also the safer choice

Running no machines is not only cheaper here; it removes most of the attack surface a one-person project cannot defend.

| Risk
| How it is removed

| Unpatched operating systems
| There is no operating system. Code runs as functions on a managed platform

| Open ports and stolen keys
| No remote access to anything, because there is nothing to log into

| Lost data
| Point-in-time restore to any minute in the last 30 days, automatically [199]. No backup script to forget

| Denial of service
| Absorbed at the edge as part of the platform

| Certificates and transport
| Issued and renewed by the platform; every connection encrypted by default

| A forged result
| Every heist is re-simulated from its seed and input log before it counts. A client can lie about what it did, but not about what happened (24.2)

| Someone else's data
| No advertising identifiers, no third-party trackers, no uploaded files and no free text — there is very little worth stealing (20.10, 24.3)

The one real exposure is vendor dependency. It is accepted deliberately, and bounded: plain SQL, no vendor-specific game logic, and an export of every table kept elsewhere. Nothing about the design is difficult to move.

24.7 The simulation core, and where it runs

One rule shapes the whole codebase: the heist simulation is written once, in a library with no engine dependencies, and both the phone and the server run that same compiled logic. Writing it twice — once in the client's language and once in the server's — would mean maintaining two implementations that must agree forever, and they would eventually not.

| Decision
| Detail

| Language
| Rust, compiled into the app as a native extension on iPhone and Android, and to WebAssembly for the server function [201]

| Numbers
| Fixed point throughout: 64-bit with 32 fractional bits for positions and speeds, 32-bit with 16 for angles and small values. No floating point appears anywhere in the simulation, because the same expression can produce different results on different processors [202]

| Trigonometry and roots
| Integer lookup tables with fixed-point interpolation, and integer square roots. Angles are stored as a fraction of a turn, which removes rounding around π entirely

| Randomness
| A 64-bit heist seed expanded through SplitMix64 into xoshiro256++, with a separate stream per subsystem. Both are pure integer operations, identical on every machine [203]

| Ordering
| Stable integer identifiers, sorted iteration, a fixed tick rate, and events resolved in a defined order. Hash-map iteration order is never relied on

| Floats
| Allowed only where the world is drawn, never where it is decided

Two things this settles

The store rules. The Rust library is linked into the signed application when it is built, so nothing is downloaded or executed at run time — which is what Apple's rules forbid [204]. A runtime WebAssembly loader on the device would break that; there is none.

The fallback. Rust extensions on mobile are officially still experimental [201]. If the first spike cannot get one building and loading on a real iPhone and a real Android inside about ten working sessions, the simulation moves to C# instead, which is a single-language path with its own mobile caveats. The fixed-point module and the golden replays are written to be portable, so that decision costs the spike and nothing else (24.2).

24.8 What the database holds

Small mutable rows in the database, large immutable things in storage, addressed by the hash of their contents.

| Table
| Holds

| accounts
| Identifier, platform identity, league, cosmetics owned, season pass expiry

| islands
| Owner, the hash of the current bundle, when it changed

| snapshots
| Bundle hash as the key, realm type, difficulty band, when published. Never edited

| heists
| Attacker, target bundle hash, seed, hash of the input log, resulting state hash, whether validated

| clans, clan_members, leagues, workshop_items
| Membership, standings, and published islands by author

Publishing writes an immutable bundle to storage under its own hash. Nothing is ever updated in place, which is what makes year-long caching safe.

Target selection is a file, not a query. Candidate lists per difficulty band are generated periodically as static objects, so choosing a target costs a cached download rather than a database search.

No passwords. Platform identity is exchanged for a short-lived signed token.

Submissions are single-use and idempotent: each carries a server-issued nonce bound to attacker and target, and is keyed by the hash of its input log so a retry cannot pay twice.

24.9 Tests the agent cannot satisfy by cheating

This codebase will be written largely by a coding agent, and the documented failure of agent-built code is not that it breaks the tests but that it writes tests it can pass. One study of 144 autonomous cycles found a suite that stayed entirely green while the code decayed, with most modules ending up imported by nothing [205]. The defence is tests that assert behaviour rather than existence, and that a human authors first.

| Test
| What it asserts

| Golden replays
| A committed seed and input log still produce the same final state hash, exactly

| Cross-architecture
| The phone build, the server build and the test build agree, checksum by checksum

| Property tests
| Rules that must always hold: resonance is symmetric, no chord divides by zero, no law produces an out-of-range value, no ordering depends on a hash

| Fuzzing
| Random islands, crews and inputs, checked for divergence and for saturation of the fixed-point range

| Content schema
| A malformed law fails the build rather than a player's device

The simulation's golden fixtures and the determinism checks are written by hand before the code is, and the agent is never both their author and their only judge.

24.10 The shape of one heist, in numbers

| Parameter
| Value
| Why

| Tick rate
| 30 a second, fixed
| Lockstep strategy games run as low as 10 and fighting games at 60. A stealth heist needs finer resolution than the first and no more than the second. A heist is 4,500 to 7,200 ticks; if a cheap phone cannot hold it, drop to 20 before sacrificing anything else

| Numbers
| 64-bit fixed point, 32 fractional bits
| Decimals are not identical across processors. The simulation refuses to compile if one appears

| What is recorded
| Commands, never state
| A move or an ability with its tick, crew member and target. State is always recoverable by re-simulating

| Size of a heist
| Under 2 kilobytes
| About 120 bytes of header, 300 to 700 bytes of commands, and a checksum a second. This is why storage and bandwidth cost almost nothing (24.1)

| Checksums
| Every tick in memory, one a second kept
| The first mismatched second narrows a divergence to thirty ticks, then a tick-by-tick comparison finds it exactly

| Order
| Entities by number, events by tick then source, phases always in the same sequence
| Inputs, then laws, then perception, then movement, then noise, then states, then the checksum. Iterating a map in whatever order it happens to be in is one of the three classic causes of divergence

24.11 When a law changes, history stays valid

Pin, do not patch. Every island and every replay records the exact set of laws it was made under, so a balance change never invalidates the past.

| Event
| What happens

| A law is re-tuned
| Old replays stay valid forever against the rules they were played under. Existing islands are re-costed at next login, and any that now exceed their stability budget enter a grace state: still defendable, not offered to new raiders, until the owner rebalances

| A law is removed
| Its name is never reused, and its definition stays archived so old replays still run. Islands using it move to grace state and prompt a replacement

| Any past heist
| Reproducible forever from four things: the seed, the input log, the island and the rules version

24.12 What happens when the network is not there

This game will be played underground, on trains, in lifts. Offline is the normal path, not an edge case, and the rules below are architecture rather than polish.

| Stage
| What happens

| Lease
| The server hands out a target snapshot, a seed, a single-use token and an expiry a day away. Nothing is created yet

| Play
| The heist runs on the device with no connection at all. The haul is shown immediately, marked as confirming

| Queue
| If offline, the result waits with its input log and a key made from the player, the token and the island

| Validate
| The server re-simulates, compares, and checks the token, the expiry and the caps

| Grant
| Only then is the haul minted, into an append-only ledger

| When it goes wrong
| What the player gets

| The connection drops mid-submission
| Nothing. The same key is retried and the server recognises it, so a haul can never be paid twice [229]

| The result does not reproduce
| No reward for that heist, nothing taken away from before, and a note for review. One mismatch is never a ban

| The lease expired
| No reward, with one grace submission allowed

| A cap was reached
| Whatever is left under the cap, and a clear message saying so

| A purchase completes but the confirmation does not
| The item shows as pending, is re-checked on next launch, and is never taken back once confirmed [230]

Rewards are shown at once but minted only after validation. Showing a number and then reducing it is the worst thing this game could do to trust, so nothing is ever confirmed before it is real.

The server clock is the only clock. Cooldowns, daily caps, season boundaries and lease expiry are all stamped at submission, so changing the time on a phone achieves nothing.

Two devices cannot double-count, because currency is a ledger of minting events rather than a number that gets overwritten. Only preferences use last-write-wins.

Editing an island during a raid is impossible to notice, since a raid runs against a frozen snapshot and an edit simply creates a new one (24.11).

24.13 Two edges worth naming

One lease per target per player at a time. A second device asking for the same target is handed the lease the first already holds, not a new one, so two heists against one snapshot cannot both be pending.

The entry fee is debited at validation, not at lease. A heist played offline and never submitted costs nothing and reserves nothing. The fee and the reward settle in the same transaction, which is also the only way the ledger can stay a single append-only record (24.12).

25Targets

Internal goals for the beta and soft launch, to be adjusted once real data exists.

| Measure
| Target

| Players who finish the first session
| 85%

| Players still playing after 1, 7, 30 and 60 days
| 40%, 18%, 8%, 5%

| Average "Was this worth your time?" rating
| 4 out of 5 or higher

| Players who say the game feels like a chore (monthly survey)
| 10% or fewer

| Session length
| 5 to 9 minutes

| Sessions a day for active players
| 3 to 6

| Heist success in the first 30 days, then after
| 70 to 80%, then 55 to 65%

| Decisions per heist
| 15 to 25

| Players who find a resonance by day 3
| 50%

| Players in a constellation by week 2
| 45%

| Monthly players who buy something
| 2% or more

25.1 Gates for a mega-hit forecast

The targets above describe a successful game. A forecast of one of the most successful games ever is only justified if Ruleborn clears the gates below, which sit around the top 1% of games in benchmark data [35]. Missing them doesn't mean failure; it means the mega-hit forecast isn't supported yet.

| Stage
| Gate

| Vertical slice
| At least 7 of 10 outside testers ask to play again; average "worth your time" of 4.3 out of 5 or higher

| Closed beta
| Players still playing after 1, 7 and 30 days: 50%, 25% and 10%; a third of monthly players play on a given day

| Social
| Within a week, 40% of players have played with or against a friend

| Creation
| Within 30 days, 1.5% of players have published a Workshop island and 10% have remixed one. Online communities typically see about 1% creating, 9% editing and 90% watching [73] [74], so the old 5% target was above what any platform achieves.

| Spread
| Every 10 players bring at least 3 new ones through clips, links and invites; at least half of installs are organic

| Soft launch
| The retention gates hold at scale; at least 3% of monthly players buy something

25.2 The first 60 seconds

Most churn happens on the first day, and players who complete a meaningful action in their first session are far more likely to still be playing a month later [54]. The first minute therefore has its own rules and its own funnel.

| Rule
| Detail

| Playing within 30 seconds
| No account creation, no permission prompts, no settings and no logos that can't be skipped before the first heist.

| One meaningful action
| Every first session ends with the player having robbed a vault and woven a law into their own island: something theirs, that they made.

| Permissions later
| Notifications are requested after the third session, and only with a reason on screen.

| Guidance across the first three sessions
| Not two steps and done. Tutorials paid off only in the complex game in a 45,000-player experiment, adding 29% more play time [9], and this is a complex game. The first minute is unguided play; sessions two and three each teach one idea in context, then guidance fades as the player shows competence [79].

| Funnel step
| Target

| Opened the app and reached the first heist
| 95%

| Finished the first heist
| 90%

| Wove their first law
| 85%

| Finished the first session
| 85%

| Came back for a second session the same day
| 60%

| Median time to the first heist
| Under 45 seconds

Every step is measured separately: "85% finished the tutorial" hides which step lost the other 15%.

25.3 Operational gates and early-warning metrics

| Gate
| Threshold
| Why

| Crash-free sessions
| 99.9% or better; ANR under 0.47%
| Below about 99.82% apps tend to fall under three stars, and half of one-star reviews mention crashes [157]. These are also the platform's own bad-behaviour thresholds [158]

| Defence participation
| 15 to 20% of new players publish an island in week one; 40% change their defence weekly
| The riskiest assumption in the design (13.1)

| Concept comprehension
| Half of testers can say what the game is after five seconds on the store page; 70% can define chord, resonance and ruleborn after the tutorial
| Novelty carries a measured commercial penalty [159]; vocabulary density is the sharpest comprehension risk (15.6)

| Churn early warning
| Recency of absence, plus the funnel in 25.2
| Absence time is the single strongest churn feature across published models, which reach 66 to 95% accuracy [160]

| Experiment power
| About 900 installs per cell to read day-1 retention to ±3 points; one readable cohort beats several unreadable ones
| [161]

| Usability rounds
| Three rounds of 5 to 8 testers, not one round of five
| Five-user sets have ranged from finding 99% of problems to 55%; ten raises the worst case to 80% [162]

25.4 How we will actually find out

No recruited panels, no paid playtesters, no thirty people in a room. Evidence comes from three places, in this order.

| Source
| What it answers
| Cost

| The simulation harness
| Decision density, skill separation, hold rates, pacing, economy health — everything structural (19.27, 13.4)
| Nothing, and it runs on every build

| The developer playing
| Whether it is worth opening. Weak evidence, recorded as weak, available daily
| Nothing

| Soft launch
| Everything a person must answer: enjoyment, meaning, whether anyone builds, whether it is worth their time
| The instruments in 14.10, answered by behaviour first and short surveys second

Behaviour beats opinion, and behaviour arrives free. Whether someone plays a second heist is worth more than what they say about the first, and the game logs it either way.

Surveys stay short and rare — the eleven-item experience inventory as the default, everything else rotated to small samples, never more than once a quarter per player.

The gap is named rather than hidden: nothing before soft launch tells us the game is fun. The simulation tells us it is not broken, which is a different and smaller claim.

Durations are estimates for a small team working with Claude Code. Each phase ends only when its exit test passes.

| Contents
| Exit test

| 1. Pre-production
