26.3 Team

Francisco: product owner and game designer.

Claude Code: engineering for the game, the server, the tools and the balance bots.

Contractors: a character artist, an interface artist and an audio designer.

From the beta: a part-time community moderator.

Once: a legal and privacy review before soft launch.

26.4 Launch experiments

| What
| How it's decided

| Store page
| Icon, first three screenshots and the video are A/B tested on real store traffic, one element at a time, for at least a week each, starting in soft launch [55]. The first screenshot must show an island with a visible rule, not a menu.

| Languages
| Store pages are translated into more languages than the game, and the extra ones measure demand before the game itself is translated. Twelve languages at launch; more if the store data justifies it.

| First session
| Two versions of the opening minute are tested in soft launch against the funnel in 25.2.

| Sharing
| Clip length, the challenge-link wording and the invite gift are tested against how many new players each brings.

| What is never tested
| Anything that changes a player's odds or price without telling them (19.12).

26.5 How this team estimates, and what it refuses to do

| Rule
| Evidence

| Estimate from comparable finished games, not from the bottom up
| The planning fallacy is one of the most replicated findings in behavioural science; reference-class forecasting is the established corrective [169]

| No AI productivity multiplier in the plan
| In a randomised trial, 16 experienced developers were 19% slower on 246 real tasks with AI assistance, while forecasting a 24% speedup and still believing afterwards they had been 20% faster [170]. The transferable finding is the calibration failure. AI is used for boilerplate and prototyping; review time for generated code is budgeted explicitly, and nobody merges code they cannot explain

| No sustained crunch
| Eight weeks of sixty-hour weeks produces the same output as eight weeks of forty, with more defects [171]

| No hiring out of a slip
| Adding people to a late project makes it later; scope is the lever, not headcount

| Scope discipline is the main risk control
| Small projects succeed around 90% of the time against under 10% for large ones, on data whose absolute figures are contested but whose direction is not [172]

| Cadence comes from measured capacity
| Content is consumed faster than a small team can author it. The Workshop is the answer, and no public roadmap is promised that would require crunch to meet

26.6 The things that are not game design

A solo developer's real risk is not the build. It is the recurring cash, the legal work and the small number of tasks that must not be done alone.

| Task
| What it involves
| Rough cost

| Name clearance, without registering
| No trade mark will be filed. Instead, all of it free: search the European and United States registers, both app stores, domains and handles before any public use; choose a distinctive coined name; claim the name in both stores early, since each reserves it first-come at no cost; and keep dated evidence of first use [198]. Registering an EU mark, which we are not doing, would cost €850 for one class [188]
| Nothing

| The risk we are accepting
| Using a name in commerce creates unregistered rights in the market where it is actually used — enough to complain, harder to enforce. If someone registers it later they gain wider priority, though a genuine earlier user usually keeps their own market. Both stores run name disputes without requiring a registration, but respond faster to those who have one [198]
| Accepted knowingly

| Company and tax
| Sole trader to begin with, under the simplified regime; VAT exemption applies below the threshold; an accountant from the first store payout [189]
| An accountant's monthly fee

| The determinism spike
| Prove a heist replays bit-identically on a low-end Android phone and on the server, and measure frame pacing, heat, battery, install size and text legibility in all twelve languages on that same phone (24.2)
| The price of a cheap handset

| Competitor teardown
| Twenty hours inside three to five adjacent games, recording loop timing, onboarding, monetisation, live-ops cadence, creator model and the recurring complaints in their reviews
| Time

| Art and audio
| Generated in code wherever possible; otherwise public domain or permissive only, with a licence ledger (21.11)
| Nothing

What not to do alone

Tax filing and company decisions.

Nothing that requires a lawyer's opinion — which is why the name is coined, the assets are generated and there is no trade mark to defend.

Anything involving images uploaded by players — which is why there are none (20.10).

Free-text chat at all, which is why there is none.

Writing the privacy policy and terms from nothing.

Running several experiments at once, which is the fastest way to fool yourself.

26.7 The order of work

No step waits for other people. The two that used to — the paper heist and the build-willingness test — are replaced by the harness in 19.27 and by the design change in 13.3.

| When
| What
| What it decides

| First, in parallel
| Determinism on desktop against the server build (24.2)
| Whether the simulation is honest. A failure is decimals in the simulation and dooms every language equally

| Reconcile the specifications that exist twice (30.18)
| Days of desk work, and the cheapest work in the plan. Nothing can be locked while two answers exist

| Then
| The spike on real phones (24.2, 24.7)
| Rust, C++ or C#. Blocks every line of core code

| Lock the simulation contract (24.10)
| Blocks the detection model and versioning

| Specification lock
| Detection numbers (12.17), movement and fights (12.18, 12.20)
| Blocks all balance work

| The law data table (7, 8.7)
| Blocks authoring or generating content

| Saves, bundles and versions (24.11)
| Blocks persistence and server validation

| Then, together
| The simulation harness (19.27)
| Everything structural, on every build from then on

| Gameplay and engine code (26.8)
| —

26.8 The build, measured in working sessions

Effort here is counted in Claude Code runs: one continuous session where the agent works until it needs a decision, hits a natural boundary or runs out of context. Hours are meaningless for this project; sessions are not.

What one run buys, and the assumptions behind it

About one vertical slice: three to six files, roughly 250 to 600 lines of code with its tests, compiling and passing. Around two to four hours of focused human work. This is deliberately smaller than what the agent could attempt, because reliable completion tops out well below maximum capability — the length of task a frontier model finishes reliably is around half an hour of human-equivalent work, even when it can attempt far longer [206]. Assume roughly two in three tasks land first time; the rest need a fix-up session. Estimates assume a maintained instructions file, a test suite the agent runs itself, and one vertical slice per session.

| Phase
| Runs
| Notes

| 0. Determinism and mobile spike
| 6 to 14
| The gate for everything. Risky: real devices, experimental tooling, and the fallback decision (24.7)

| 1. Simulation core
| 28 to 48
| Tick loop, crews, stealth, the law engine, resonances and chords, golden harness. Human-written spec required

| 2. Client shell
| 12 to 20
| Project structure, localisation, portrait interface, save and settings

| 3. Heist prototype
| 10 to 18
| First playable: render a replay, capture input, show a result

| 4. Island builder
| 14 to 24
| Terrain, laws, bundle serialisation, testing your own defence

| 5. Content systems
| 10 to 18
| 48 laws, 144 forms, 39 terrain features as validated data. Ideal agent work

| 6. Backend
| 24 to 40
| Schema, identity, publishing, the validator, target lists, rate limits

| 7. Workshop
| 10 to 18
| Publish, browse, moderate, remix

| 8. Progression and economy
| 14 to 22
| Currencies, cosmetics, purchases and receipts, the season track

| 9. Season and social
| 12 to 20
| Leagues, crews and constellations, season rollover

| 10 to 13. Localisation, accessibility, compliance, telemetry
| 20 to 38
| Twelve languages, the accessibility set, store declarations, event logging

| 14. Launch hardening
| 12 to 24
| Load testing, a real-device matrix, a balance pass, a fix buffer

| Total
| 210 to 360
| Point estimate about 270

| Safe to hand over
| Not safe to hand over

| Content data and its schemas; interface screens; localisation; the Workshop; target-list generation; telemetry; property and fuzz tests
| The determinism spike; the fixed-point module; the stealth model; the validator; purchases and receipts; anything needing a real device; store submission

What this number does not include: finding the fun. The roadmap costs building the stealth model and the law system, not iterating until they are enjoyable. That iteration is unbudgeted and could be large (19.17).

Re-baseline after the spike. These are derived estimates, not measurements. After phase 0, count the actual sessions per feature and rescale everything.

Scope one slice per session. If a task looks bigger than a run, split it before starting rather than halfway through.

26.9 Names, and what we checked

Every invented name in this document was checked against what already exists, on the assumption that no trade mark will be filed (26.6). Five names were changed, including the game's own. The rest are either coined or ordinary words used in their ordinary sense, which is why dozens of games share them without difficulty.

| Was
| Now
| Why

| Echo Knight
| Echoplate
| A well-known fighter subclass in a published tabletop setting, and ours shared its mechanic — a copy of yourself that returns. The resemblance was the problem, not the words

| Moss Giant
| Mossbear
| A long-standing creature in another online game. Defensible, but cheap to avoid

| Masquerade
| Borrowed Face
| A famous games mark in its own right. The replacement says what the law does more clearly anyway

| Creator Fund
| Island Fund
| A widely used programme name on a large platform

The game's own name

Lawbound became Ruleborn

No game called Lawbound was found, but "-bound" is well populated in the classes that matter — Stormbound and Bladebound are both registered for game software — and since no mark will be filed here, unregistered rights cover only the market actually used. A crowded field gives a later registrant room to press, and the stores answer faster to whoever holds a registration.

Ruleborn is coined, sits in a far emptier field, and names the creatures rather than the constraint — which is the better story anyway: the game is named after what was born from the scattered laws, not after being tied by them. The clearance still happens before any public use: both registers, both stores, domains and handles.

What everything else rests on

Ordinary words, used ordinarily: Starlight, Essence, Aether, Renown, Keeper, Codex, Vault, Spire, Nursery, Observatory, Constellation, Workshop, and all seven realm names. Generic or descriptive, and used as such.

Coined names for everything with character: the eight notes, the six schools, and the creature list — Featherkin, Skyskipper, Quarryback, Tidewalker, Hourkeeper, Stillwatch, Emberling, Sparkwright, Bloomling, Rootrunner, Lantern Wisp, Soulmender, Echoplate, Mossbear.

Other games named in this document — and there are many — are referred to by name for comparison, which is ordinary and lawful. No logo, artwork or text of theirs appears anywhere in it.

Every new name is checked before use, not after: the registers, both stores, a plain web search, and said aloud in the twelve launch languages (15.6).

If Ruleborn does not clear. Two fallbacks are held in reserve, chosen now rather than under time pressure: Lawkeep and Isleborn. Both are coined, both sit outside the crowded "-bound" field, and both pass the same four checks. Neither is the name already in use — which is what the earlier list in 27.2 wrongly said.

27Risks and decisions

27.1 Risks

| Risk
| Mitigation

| The first minute loses players before the game starts
| Rules and a measured funnel for the first 60 seconds (25.2), tested in soft launch.

| Research findings don't reproduce here
| Evidence ranked by strength, theory-based claims marked, and effects assumed to arrive smaller (19.14).

| Too many rules for new players
| The complexity budget (7.8), notes that can be reasoned about (8), the learning map (15) and generous early heists.

| Some combinations break the game
| Balance bots before every release; stability costs and dissonance; laws that affect both sides; tuning without app updates (24).

| Heists feel like watching
| Decision density and controls (12.6, 12.7); the vertical slice must prove it before anything else is built (26.2).

| Open landing makes defence feel hopeless
| Terrain along every edge (6), landing noise (5.4), loss caps and the sealed share (9.4), rehearsals (13).

| Fights reward only bigger numbers
| Stealth, ambushes, wind-ups and the level cap (12.8).

| The economy numbers come from a model
| Re-run the model with alpha data and retune through server data, without app updates (24).

| Casual players take almost three years to finish
| An open decision (27.3).

| Too few players to raid
| Echo universes and ruins (12.3); soft launch in a few countries first.

| Interest in deep strategy is declining
| Short sessions, the complexity budget, and depth that can wait until the player wants it.

| Teen safety
| Protections by age band, moderation and privacy by default (20).

| Earnings without pay-to-win
| Retention first, visible cosmetics, a season pass and the launch gates (22.2).

| Art scope
| Law signatures as procedural changes to shared animations (21.6); a world built in code; paid art for characters, the icon and marketing.

| Name and trademark
| Check "Ruleborn" in the App Store and trademark registers before any public use.

27.2 Decisions taken

| Question
| Decision

| Pitch and story
| "Your islands. Your rules. Their treasure." Laws are pages of the scattered Book of Laws; pages replace fragments

| Where laws apply
| Crown laws for the whole universe (1 slot from Spire level 2, 2 from level 5) and realm laws for one island (the Heart has 2 slots, outer realms 1 to 3)

| Landing
| Open: any edge tile of any outer realm, by skiff; the Heart can't be landed on

| Stability
| Built by the Spire and Anchor Stones; seven bands from Quiet to Breaking

| Laws
| 48 in six schools: three common, two uncommon, one rare and two deep per school

| Combinations
| Eight notes giving harmonies and dissonances, 44 signature resonances and 12 chords

| Terrain
| 39 features in seven families, limited by terrain points

| Camera
| A rotatable diorama view at home; a follow camera in heists

| Pacing target
| An engaged player reaches Keeper level 40 in about six months and finishes everything in about a year

| Platforms
| iPhone and Android together at global launch, cross-progression, twelve languages; PC in version 1.2

| Under-13s
| No kid-safe mode: Ruleborn stays 13+, now and later

| Workshop creators
| Reversed in version 1.4: a Island Fund of 10% of cosmetic and pass revenue, paid in arrears with a cap and a floor, 18+ and opt-in, on top of the in-game ladder (18.9)

| China
| Not pursued: no local publisher and no licence application

| Co-op design
| Interdependent roles, one shared goal, and results never attributed to one player

| Reveals
| Tease then resolve, in seconds, never purchasable

| How fun is judged
| Validated instruments and a fun gate at every phase (19.5), never a single score

| Concept additions
| Law-shaped forms, friendly heists, sharing and the Island Workshop at launch; co-op heists in version 1.1

| Matchmaking against players
| Transparent Renown bands; outcomes are never secretly tuned for engagement, and the refusal is written down (19.12)

| Difficulty
| Chosen by the player and labelled; novelty and suspense do the work instead of hidden tuning

| Accessibility
| A full specification at launch, with disabled players in playtests from the vertical slice on

| Audience and rating
| Core ages 16 to 34; rated 13+; protections for 13 to 17

| Control in a heist
| Live taps, with at most eight controls on screen

| Theft
| Raiders take a capped share of the exposed amount

| Building timers
| None; upgrades are instant

| Names
| The player is the Keeper; heroes are Sages; traders are Visitors

| Daily rewards
| No login rewards; self-chosen goals; a weekly streak with repairs

| Eras and Law Clash
| Eras at launch; Law Clash in version 1.2

| Cost ceiling
| Near zero to a million players: static, egress-free delivery plus serverless functions and a managed database — about $5 to $15 a month at a million. $99 a year to Apple remains the only unavoidable recurring cost (24.1, 24.5)

| Machine learning
| None, anywhere — not in the runtime, not in moderation, not in analytics (24.5)

| Trade mark
| Not registered. Free clearance, a coined name, store names claimed early, dated evidence of first use (26.6)

| Licensing
| Nothing is paid for or licensed. Generated in code, or public domain and permissive only (21.11)

| Why raids exist
| Pages are the reason. A heist is worth two to three sessions of the Library's trickle, and pages lean toward the school raided — primary, not exclusive, and never gated by the clock (7.6)

| Law rarity
| Five ranks. Rarity sets how long a law takes to get; stability cost and conditions set how strong it is. Rare and deep laws are visible in the scout report, which is what pays for their power (7.9)

| Defence
| Five layers of comparable weight — geometry, guards, wards, laws, response — none above a quarter. Laws are no longer "the main defence" (13)

| Fair defence
| 55 to 70% of raids succeed; 60% of a defence's strength comes from design rather than level; the shape of outcomes matters more than the average (13.2)

| Four names changed
| Echo Knight became Echoplate, Moss Giant became Mossbear, Masquerade became Borrowed Face, and the Creator Fund became the Island Fund (26.9)

| The game's name
| Ruleborn is kept, but clearance is a gate before any public use: the registers, both stores, domains and handles, including "-bound" marks in the software and entertainment classes. Fallbacks, if clearance fails, are Lawkeep and Isleborn — the name in use is never its own fallback (26.9)

| Fonts
| Two open-licensed typefaces, embedded lawfully, with the licence text shipped in settings (21.11)

| What a raid pays
| Minted from the raider's own level, nudged by the target's wealth within a narrow band, graded by outcome, decaying through the day, and capped daily and weekly. The base growth rate is the master inflation dial (9.6)

| Defence budgets
| Slot limits per layer per realm level, priced against stability. Limits, not cost, are what stop one layer dominating (13.4)

| Measuring a layer
| Switch it off, re-run a thousand raids, measure the fall — across all 32 combinations. The targets in 13.2 become a test that passes or fails each build (13.4)

| Offline
| Heists play with no connection and submit later. Rewards shown at once as confirming, minted only after validation, never taken back (24.12)

| Clocks
| The server clock is the only clock (24.12)

| Blocking
| Hides a person, never removes their island from the pool (17.8)

| One answer per system
| Where the document specified something twice, the older half is deleted rather than left standing: detection (12.17), payout (9.6), budgets (13.4), roster (11.11), rarity (7.9), production (5.8)

| Validation without players
| No paper tests, no recruited panels. A simulation harness answers everything structural, the developer is the first player, and the rest waits for soft launch (19.27, 25.4)

| Nobody has to build
| Every player has a generated defence from the first minute; curation is the default creative act and authoring is the deep end. The riskiest assumption is removed rather than measured (13.3)

| Five ranks, re-derived
| 18 common, 12 uncommon, 6 rare, 12 deep at 10/20/35/60 pages — 1,350 for the full Codex, replacing the three-rank figure (7.9, 14.4)

| Producers
| One of each kind per realm; partial staffing pays pro-rata; a crew on a heist does not work (5.8)

| Guardian Posts
| One ruleborn each — the conversion that reconciles every defence table (13.4)

| The crew never decides
| No auto-attack, no auto-flee, no re-routing. A creature cannot look stupid for a decision it never made (12.18)

| Detection
| Deterministic and predictable: three vision zones, a suspicion meter that fills in half a second up close and empties slowly, six guard states, and noise that travels along paths. No dice anywhere (12.17)

| If nobody builds
| Under 15% building a second island unprompted, the game pivots to generated islands the player curates. Every other system survives intact (13.3)

| Before any code
| Two gates, neither needing anyone else: determinism across devices, and the simulation harness (19.27, 26.7)

| Rules versioning
| Pin, do not patch. Islands and replays record the rules they were made under; a re-tuned law puts an over-budget island into grace rather than breaking it (24.11)

| Simulation code
| Written once in Rust with no engine types, compiled into the app and to WebAssembly for the server. Fixed point throughout, no floating point in the simulation (24.7)

| Validation policy
| Sampled, not universal: every high-value, league-affecting or disputed heist, plus a random share (24)

| Store compliance
| The simulation is linked into the signed binary at build time. No runtime code loading of any kind (24.7)

| Effort unit
| Claude Code runs, not hours. About 270 for the whole build, re-baselined after the first spike (26.8)

| Backend
| Fully serverless: no virtual machine, no operating system, nothing to patch or log into. Zero-egress storage, edge functions, a managed database (24.1, 24.6)

| Determinism
| Fixed-point maths, proved bit-identical across devices before anything else is built (24.2)

| Workshop content
| Structured data only — no player-uploaded images, ever (20.10)

| Analytics
| First-party only, regenerated from replays; no advertising identifiers, no third-party trackers (24.3)

| Matchmaking target
| The median heist is winnable, not an even match; ratings stay internal (12.15)

| Defence participation
| A 60-second guided build before the first player heist; seeded islands; defenders paid best (13.1)

| Chat by age
| Everyone under 18 starts on preset chat; 16 and 17 may opt into filtered free chat (20.2, 20.9)

| Currency
| Every cosmetic priced in real money; Starglass only as a visible 1:1 wrapper (19.12)

| Touch targets
| 9 mm or more for primary controls, never in a corner (21.8)

| Co-op
| Asynchronous first; real-time only if playtests demand it (12.11, 26.5)

| Estimation
| Reference-class forecasting, no AI multiplier, no sustained crunch (26.5)

| Notes
| Frozen at eight; laws are the expansion axis (24)

| Concept pitch
| Anchored differentiation: familiar in five seconds, novelty after the hook (23.4)

| Screen orientation
| Portrait by default on phones, landscape available everywhere and remembered per device, with the same information in both (23.3)

| Theft
| One-way by default: raiders gain, defenders lose nothing. An opt-in Open Vault league carries real stakes (27.5)

| Onboarding
| Guided across the first three sessions, then faded, rather than two steps and done (27.5)

| Casual pacing
| Settled: a visible, openly labelled catch-up discount on levels the median player passed long ago (27.5)

27.3 Still open

Decisions for Francisco

Casual pacing: at one session a day, finishing everything takes about 1,019 days. Either keep it (nothing decays, and base builders are usually slower), or add a catch-up discount on building levels that the median player passed months ago.

Final names for the game, realms and currencies, and the trademark check.

Whether the Island Fund survives its own test (18.9): output and meaning among the top 200 creators, six months after it launches.

Whether the two weak variables in 19.4, performative and emotional challenge, are worth strengthening after launch or left alone.

Whether the interface says "universe" or a softer word such as "your sky".

Law Clash rules, ahead of version 1.2.

Which countries to use for the soft launch.

Whether constellations should later get a competitive mode.

| Connect resources, buildings, laws and characters; currencies, costs and production

| Look and feel in depth
| 21

| Go deeper
| Throughout, backed by the economy model (24)

| The most successful game ever
| 2.5 to 2.7 and 25.1: what the record holders share, the honest odds, and the gates that would have to be cleared

| Deeper research
| 19.11 to 19.14, and 28: 66 sources, ranked by strength, each tied to a design decision

| Research the variables of fun
| 19.1 to 19.5: fourteen variables, the traps, where Ruleborn is weak, and how fun is tested

| Creature proportions
| Specified to the cuteness rules, not left to taste (21.6)

| What the endgame is for
| A place, a reputation and a body of work — never a final unlock (14.9)

| Group size
| Crews of 4 to 8 inside constellations of up to 30; no quotas anywhere (17.7)

| Seasons
| Nothing expires forever, catch-up built in, no countdown pressure (18.8)

| Fading players
| A legacy card and a graceful off-ramp, never a win-back campaign (14.10)

| Late economy
| Surplus becomes freedom to experiment and rebuild; no level cap raises, no tier IV, no new currency (14.9)

| Session shape
| Every session has a designed peak and a designed ending (19.20)

| Evidence quality
| 28.1 to 28.3: how sources are judged, what was excluded, and a grade on every one of the 123

| Decisions re-checked
| 19.21: which decisions rest on weak evidence, and what would change them

| Seven commissioned reviews
| Applied throughout in versions 1.5 and 1.6; each review, the joined conclusions, the contradictions between them and the ranked risk register are recorded in 30

| Research fun further
| 19.15 to 19.17: company, failure, the senses and teasing, plus the eight experiments that settle what evidence can't

27.5 Assumptions contested by the evidence

Everything in this document was re-read against all 102 sources, and each major assumption was asked one question: does the evidence support it, or did we assume it? Seven assumptions changed. Three survived the challenge.

| Assumption
| What the evidence says
| Verdict

| Landscape orientation, chosen because strategy maps are wide
| Most of the highest-grossing mobile games are portrait, and portrait is what allows one-handed play in the exact contexts a 5 to 9 minute session is built for [85]. The wide map was our convenience, not the player's.
| Changed. Portrait-first: home, menus and the Workshop in portrait, the universe arranged as a vertical column; heists playable in portrait with landscape optional (21.1)

| Two steps of guidance at most, to get players playing in 30 seconds
| This contradicted our own strongest onboarding source: in a 45,000-player experiment tutorials paid off only in the complex game, where they added 29% more play time [9]. Ruleborn is the complex game. Learning in games happens through breakthroughs the player feels responsible for [86] [87], and guidance should fade as competence grows rather than stop abruptly [79].
| Changed. Unguided play in the first minute, then one taught idea in each of sessions two and three, then fading guidance (15, 25.2)

| Raiders take real resources from real players, with caps
| The strongest predictor of quitting in the need-frustration literature is repeated, uncontrollable frustration that violates what the player expected [82], and being robbed while offline is the purest form of it: external, uncontrollable, and unattributable to any decision [68]. Losses also weigh more than equivalent gains [15]. Nothing in our evidence base says taking things from people makes a game more fun; it says the opposite.
| Changed. One-way theft by default: the haul is created from a snapshot and defenders lose nothing. An opt-in Open Vault league keeps real stakes, with 40% higher rewards (9.4)

| The complexity budget was about unfamiliar laws
| The limit that matters is how many pieces interact at once, not how many are new: working memory holds about four chunks [75] [76], and difficulty scales with element interactivity [77] [78].
| Changed. A hard cap of four interacting rules at any moment, at every level of play (7.8)

| Failure is fine because losses are capped
| Temporary failure is integral to the pleasure of success; perpetual failure is what drives withdrawal [81]. Disclosed assistance does not harm the experience [83], and removing control frustrates autonomy [84].
| Changed. The three-strike rule, disclosed assistance, and a 2-second ceiling on any effect that removes control (12.14)

| 5% of players will publish an island
| Participation in creative communities is consistently about 1% creating, 9% editing and 90% watching [73] [74]. Our target was above what any platform reaches.
| Changed. 1.5% publishing and 10% remixing, with a one-tap "remix this island" so the 9% have somewhere to go (18.6, 25.1)

| Casual players finishing in three years is acceptable
| Left open since version 0.3. Disclosed assistance does not harm experience [83], so the honest fix is available.
| Changed. A visible, openly labelled catch-up discount on building levels the median player passed months ago, shown as what it is (14)

| No login rewards
| About 95% of games use them [88], and they work — through loss aversion, by making absence feel like losing something [88] [15]. That is exactly the coercion that predicts disengagement and ill-being in the need-frustration work [82], and expected rewards for showing up reduce later interest [3].
| Kept, and now with the reason written down. Surprises and self-chosen goals instead (14.6)

| Paying creators
| Runs against an A-grade experiment on crowding out [144] and an A-grade meta-analysis on expected rewards [3]. Nothing in the evidence supports it
| Taken knowingly. Structured to resemble the unexpected rewards that did not undermine interest, capped to favour the tail, and carrying an explicit failure condition (18.9, 19.26)

| Cosmetics and a season pass only
| The season pass has converged across the industry on 4 to 8 week seasons, 50 to 100 tiers, $5 to $15 and cosmetic-led rewards, carrying 10 to 40% of revenue in top free-to-play games [89]. Our design already matches it. Cosmetic-led games earn less per player but keep more of them [90].
| Kept. Price band widened to €5 to €10 (22.1)

| 48 laws is not too many
| The four-chunk limit applies to what interacts at once, not to how much can be collected over a year [75]. Expertise is compression: an expert sees one pattern where a novice sees six pieces [77].
| Kept. The collection stays at 48; the interaction cap in 7.8 is what protects new players

What this cost
The one-way theft change removes the sharpest edge of the fantasy: the idea that somewhere, right now, someone might be robbing you. The Open Vault league keeps that available for players who want it, and the vertical slice will test both. If the evidence for the edge turns out to be stronger than the evidence against it, this is the decision most likely to be reversed.

28References

28.1 How sources are judged

A design document that cites 119 sources is only as good as its worst source. This section sets out what earns a place here, what is excluded, and how much weight each surviving source carries. It is written so that anyone can check a claim in this document back to the evidence behind it and see how far to trust it.

Why grading is necessary

Only 36% of 100 replicated psychology studies produced a significant effect again, and replication effect sizes averaged about half the originals [120].

In social science experiments published in Nature and Science, 62% replicated, again at roughly half the original effect size, despite samples five times larger [121].

Meta-analyses are not a safe harbour either: compared against preregistered multi-laboratory replications of the same effects, meta-analytic estimates were about three times larger, and standard corrections for publication bias did not fix it [122].

So: published effects are treated as upper bounds, never as forecasts. Every target in 25 assumes the effect arrives smaller here.

The five questions asked of every source

| Question
| What raises a source
| What lowers it

| 1. Design
| Randomised experiment, large-scale A/B test, meta-analysis of experiments, or behaviour logged at scale
| Cross-sectional survey, single small lab study, correlation offered as cause

| 2. Size and power
| Thousands of players, or a meta-analysis aggregating many studies
| Under 100 participants without a preregistered plan

| 3. Independence and bias
| Peer-reviewed, preregistered, replicated, data available. The grading scheme itself follows the logic of GRADE, which downgrades evidence for risk of bias, imprecision, inconsistency, indirectness and publication bias [123]
| Published by a company selling the thing measured; no method stated; a single vendor's blog

| 4. Directness
| Measured in games, on phones, with players, on the outcome we care about
| Measured in a different field, on a proxy outcome, or on a population unlike our players

| 5. What it would change
| If the finding reversed, a decision in this document would change
| Cited only to decorate a decision already made

The four grades

| Grade
| What it means
| How it may be used
| Sources

| A — strong
| Meta-analysis of experiments, randomised experiment, or behaviour logged across thousands of players
| May carry a design decision on its own, with the effect discounted
| 81

| B — moderate
| Peer-reviewed but smaller, correlational, or one step removed from our context
| May support a decision alongside an A source; never carries one alone
| 11

| C — industry
| Market and benchmark data, platform documentation, studio analysis. Method usually undisclosed, often published by an interested party
| Used for market shape, prices and benchmarks only. Never used to claim an effect on players
| 27

| D — excluded
| Did not meet the bar
| Not cited
| See 28.2

Each entry in 28.3 is tagged with its grade. Three A-grade sources in this list carry explicit warnings in their own right: loss aversion, whose size is disputed [15]; the three-needs framework, which a review of 259 studies found is often applied shallowly [51]; and the spacing and retrieval literature, which did not hold up as well in mathematics [48].

28.2 What was excluded, and why

| Excluded
| Reason

| "Dopamine hit" and habit-loop popularisations
| Claims outrun the underlying work. The robust finding is about uncertainty, not about a chemical reward per action [14]

| Vendor case studies with no method, denominator or control group
| Fails questions 1 and 3. A single studio's "we raised retention 40%" is unfalsifiable

| Engagement-maximising techniques we will not use
| Read and recorded in 19.12, but never used to justify a design

| Neuromarketing and brain-scan claims about purchasing
| Indirect: brain activity is not the outcome we are designing for

| Generational and personality-typing claims about players
| Weak measurement, poor replication, and no decision would change

| Single-game postmortems presented as general laws
| One sample, no control, heavy survivorship bias. Used for hypotheses only, never as evidence

| Anything paywalled that we could not read in full
| We do not cite what we have not read

28.3 The sources

Peer-reviewed sources first, in the order they are first cited; industry data last. The tag on each entry is its grade from 28.1.

C industry Nozhnin, D. (2012), analytics on veteran churn in a live MMO (about 38,000 veterans, six months of logs), reporting prediction of departure two to three weeks ahead. Method not published.

A strong Ducheneaut, N., Yee, N., Nickell, E., & Moore, R. J. (2006/2007). The social life of guilds in World of Warcraft; guild sizes follow a power law (mean about 16.8, median 9) and guilds dissolve often. CHI and Journal of CMC.

B moderate Zagal, J. P., Björk, S., & Lewis, C. (2013). Dark patterns in the design of games. FDG 2013; with later analyses of social-obligation patterns in free-to-play mobile games (2025). arxiv.org

B moderate Petrovskaya, E., & Zendle, D. (2020). The battle pass: A mixed-methods investigation into a growing type of video game monetisation (Dota 2): minimal effect on player uptake; themes of grind-versus-spend, elitism and pressure. doi:10.31234/osf.io/vnmeq

A strong Macnamara, B. N., Hambrick, D. Z., & Oswald, F. L. (2014). Deliberate practice and performance in music, games, sports, education, and professions: A meta-analysis (games: 26% of variance; about 5% with log-based measures). Psychological Science, 25(8), 1608–1618. doi:10.1177/0956797614535810

A strong Hulleman, C. S., Schrager, S. M., Bodmann, S. M., & Harackiewicz, J. M. (2010). A meta-analytic review of achievement goal measures (243 studies): normative performance goals r ≈ +.14, appearance-based r ≈ −.14. Psychological Bulletin, 136(3), 422–449. doi:10.1037/a0018947

B moderate Stebbins, R. A. (1992 onward). The serious leisure perspective: amateur and hobbyist careers, identity and social worlds; synthesis (2020). seriousleisure.net

A strong Don't pay the highly motivated too much (2022). Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics: high-powered monetary incentives crowd out motivation specifically among the highly motivated. doi:10.1016/j.socec.2022.101953. See also Deci, Koestner & Ryan (1999) [3]

C industry Roblox Economic Impact Report (Nordicity, 2025): more than 24,500 creators in the exchange programme; median creator received about $1,575 in the year to December 2024, while the top 10 developers averaged $33.9 million. Third-party analyses estimate about 85% of developers earn under $100 a month. corp.roblox.com

A strong Kim, S., et al. (2024). Effects of opponent skill on churn in a casual mobile game (about 6 million matches, 262,000 players): churn rose against stronger opponents and fell most against weaker ones. Heliyon.

B moderate Large-scale analysis of satisfaction and recent win rate in online games, finding short-term win rates near 50% optimal for satisfaction. arXiv preprint

A strong Al Enezi, W., & Verbrugge, C. (2023). Two controlled user studies of guard behaviour and dialogue in top-down stealth games: contextual dialogue let simple AI match complex AI for enjoyment. AAAI AIIDE.

A strong Near-miss research: Stange et al. on physiological arousal; preregistered conceptual replications on an online slot simulator (Studies 1a n=169, 1b n=148). Psychology of Addictive Behaviors (2024), 38(6), 716–727.

C industry Behaviour Interactive (July 2024): a 400% bonus was attached to the under-filled role in Dead by Daylight's 2v8 mode to correct role imbalance; queue-time reports are player-sourced.

B moderate Thorn (2025), Youth Perspectives on Online Safety, sixth annual report: 33% of boys aged 9 to 12 reported an online sexual interaction in 2024.

B moderate Thorn, Online Grooming research: 40% of minors report being approached by someone they believed was attempting to manipulate them, rising to 47% of teen girls; roughly half of harmed minors use a reporting feature.

C industry NSPCC analysis of data from 45 UK police forces: 7,062 sexual-communication-with-a-child offences recorded in 2023/24, up 89% since 2017/18.

A strong Systematic review of content-moderation classifier performance (2023): 70 to 97% accuracy depending on task, with hash-matching more reliable than classifiers for known material. arXiv

A strong Grandprey-Shores, K., He, Y., Swanenburg, K., Kraut, R., & Riedl, J. (2014). The identification of deviance and its impact on retention in a multiplayer game (2.5M players, 18.25M matches; regression sample 341,295). CSCW 2014. doi:10.1145/2531602.2531724

A strong Toccafondi, G., Di Paolo, R., & Di Guida, S. Virtual currencies in online gaming increase the willingness to pay: an incentivised experiment with 753 UK participants, finding higher willingness to pay in virtual currency even at a 1:1 rate. Experimental Economics, 28(6).

C industry Instabug/Luciq crash benchmarks (2024–2025): median crash-free sessions about 99.9%; apps below roughly 99.82% tend to rate under three stars; Google reports half of one-star reviews mention crashes.

C industry Google Play Console: bad-behaviour thresholds of a 1.09% crash rate and a 0.47% ANR rate of daily sessions.

A strong Yu, S., et al. (2022). Unique in what sense? Heterogeneous relationships between multiple types of uniqueness and popularity in music (51,411 songs): novelty consistently negatively associated with popularity. arXiv:2207.12943

A strong Churn-prediction literature in games: 66 to 95% accuracy across 1 to 7 day windows, with current absence time the strongest single feature; includes a two-phase neural model trained on 713,884 user records.

C industry Soft-launch sample-size guidance: about 896 installs per cell to read day-1 retention near 30% to ±3 points at 95% confidence.

A strong Faulkner, L. (2003). Beyond the five-user assumption: benefits of increased sample sizes in usability testing. Behavior Research Methods, 35(3), 379–383. Five-user sets ranged from 55% to 99% of problems found; ten raised the worst case to 80%.

B moderate Deterministic replay validation as an anti-cheat and regression-testing approach; see also cheating-contagion work showing players adopt cheating after observing or being victimised by it. arXiv

A strong Behavioural bot detection in online games: 96.06% detection accuracy against a banned-account list; ~96% with random forests; 98%+ for behaviour-sequence classifiers.

A strong Deber, J., et al. (2015). How much faster is fast enough? CHI 2015: just-noticeable difference about 11 ms for dragging and 69 ms for tapping. Jota et al. (2013): dragging performance degrades above 25 ms.

B moderate Refresh-rate experiment on gaming performance: F(4,100)=13.776, p<.001, with significant differences only between 30 Hz and higher rates.

C industry Device-distribution data for target markets: Android at roughly 81 to 95% share, with the low-end price tier the largest segment and 60 Hz the most common refresh rate.

B moderate Wang, J., Huang, Y., & Qu, Q. (2014). Network-based analysis of software change propagation: change distribution is very uneven, and high-centrality nodes drive most downstream impact. doi:10.1155/2014/237243

A strong Kahneman & Tversky on the planning fallacy; Buehler et al. on its persistence; Flyvbjerg on reference-class forecasting as the corrective.

A strong METR (2025). Randomised controlled trial of AI developer tooling: 16 experienced developers, 246 real tasks on repositories averaging over a million lines; 19% slower with AI while believing they were 20% faster.

B moderate IGDA/Robinson on overtime productivity reversal; Akula & Cusick on the impact of overtime and stress on software quality.

C industry Standish Group CHAOS reports: 31% of projects fully successful; small projects succeed around 90% of the time against under 10% for large ones. Definitions criticised by Eveleens & Verhoef, IEEE Software (2010).

A strong Ćwiek, A., et al. (2022). The bouba/kiki effect is robust across cultures and writing systems (976 participants, 25 languages, 10 writing systems). Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B, 377(1841).

A strong Henze, N., Rukzio, E., & Boll, S. (2011). 100,000,000 taps: analysis and improvement of touch performance in the large. MobileHCI 2011, 133–142 (120,626,225 touch events from 91,731 installations). With Parhi, Karlson & Bederson (2006) recommending 9.2 to 9.6 mm targets.

A strong Legge, G. E., and colleagues, the psychophysics of reading series; and a 2020 PNAS study finding a minimum of 13 characters per line for normally sighted readers to reach 80% of maximum reading speed.

A strong Treisman & Gelade (1980); Wolfe (1998), "What can 1,000,000 trials tell us about visual search?" Psychological Science: inefficient search slopes of roughly 20 to 45 ms per item, with target-absent slopes about twice as steep.

C industry Cloudflare published pricing (2026): Workers paid plan from $5 a month including 10 million requests; D1 billed on rows read and written with a free allowance of 100,000 writes a day; KV writes priced about ten times reads. developers.cloudflare.com

C industry Cloudflare R2: $0.015 per gigabyte-month with no egress fees, against $0.09 per gigabyte egress on comparable object storage; third-party comparisons put 100 TB of heavy-egress traffic at roughly $1,500 a month against $4,600 or more elsewhere. cloudflare.com

B moderate Game-telemetry and heatmap literature: Drachen et al., "Beyond heatmaps"; Hullett & Whitehead (2010) on data-driven level design; Medler et al. (2011) on the Data Cracker telemetry dashboard.

C industry PlaytestCloud: a panel of more than 1.5 million verified players, certified for testing with children aged 3 to 15, with token-based pricing and an indie tier for studios under $1 million revenue. playtestcloud.com

A strong Haider, A., et al. (2022). miniPXI: an eleven-item, single-item-per-construct version of the Player Experience Inventory, validated with 628 players. Proceedings of the ACM on HCI, 6(CHI PLAY). doi:10.1145/3549507

C industry Alchemer mobile engagement benchmarks: in-app survey response averages about 13% across categories and about 19% in entertainment apps; broader industry medians run near 10%, and email surveys near 3%.

B moderate Kahn, A. S., et al. (2014) on error in self-reported play time. Journal of Computer-Mediated Communication, 19(4); with later work on the divergence between self-reported and logged play.

C industry Child-safety detection tooling pricing: Thorn's Safer listed on a cloud marketplace at about $30,720 a year for a million queries a month — an order of magnitude beyond a solo developer's budget, which is why hosting player images is designed out entirely.

C industry Moderation API pricing (2026): OpenAI's moderation endpoint is free; Google Perspective is free; custom-policy services start around $0.25 per thousand messages, with image moderation several times higher.

C industry Support benchmarks: self-service deflection of roughly 40 to 70% where a knowledge base and in-app help are implemented well.

C industry Apple Small Business Program and Google Play's reduced service fee: commission falls from 30% to 15% below one million dollars a year, and new developers qualify automatically. Note that Apple's programme reverts to 30% the year after the threshold is crossed, while Google keeps the lower rate on the first million.

C industry EUIPO fee schedule (Regulation (EU) 2017/1001, Annex I): €850 for an online EU trade mark in one class, €50 for the second class, €150 per class thereafter. SME Fund vouchers can subsidise part of the fee.

C industry Portuguese self-employment: the simplified regime taxes 75% of income below a €200,000 turnover ceiling; VAT exemption under Article 53 applies below €15,000; social security contributions follow an initial exemption period. Practitioner sources, to be confirmed with a certified accountant.

C industry Cloudflare R2 pricing (2026): free tier of 10 GB stored, 1 million write operations and 10 million read operations a month, $0.015 per gigabyte-month beyond it, and no egress charge on any storage class. Serving assets from R2 also sits inside the content-delivery terms, which restrict serving large files hosted elsewhere. developers.cloudflare.com

C industry Free-tier limits that break first (2026): edge workers at 100,000 requests a day; key-value storage at 100,000 reads and 1,000 writes a day; edge SQL row limits now fail hard rather than degrade. The answer at that point is the paid plan rather than self-hosting.

C industry Firebase Cloud Messaging is free and unlimited with no per-message charge at any scale; platform-native crash reporting on both stores is likewise free.

C industry Compression benchmarks for small structured data: roughly five to six times with Brotli or zstd, and better again with a trained dictionary where every record shares the same shape; binary encodings save a further third to a half before compression.

C industry Apple App Review Guideline 1.2 and Google Play's user-generated content policy: content filtering, a report mechanism acted on within 24 hours, the ability to block abusive users, published contact details and a terms-of-use gate.

C industry EU Digital Services Act: notice-and-action, statements of reasons, a single point of contact and clear terms apply to services of any size; the heavier online-platform duties exempt micro and small enterprises.

C industry Store fees (2026): Apple Developer Program $99 a year, recurring; Google Play developer registration $25, one-time. Both stores accept a freely hosted public page for the privacy policy and support link.

C industry SIL Open Font License: permits embedding and bundling fonts in commercial software without affecting the application's own licence, subject to keeping the licence text and not selling the font alone. Contrast with attribution, share-alike and non-commercial licences, "royalty-free" stock terms, and desktop-only font agreements.

C industry Unregistered trade mark rights: use in commerce creates rights limited to the market of actual use; a later registrant gains wider priority while a genuine prior user generally keeps their own market. Apple and Google both reserve app names first-come at no cost and operate name and content dispute processes that do not require a registration. Free searching via EUIPO TMview, USPTO TESS and the store listings.

C industry Cloudflare D1 pricing and limits (2026): the free plan allows 5 million row reads and 100,000 row writes a day with 5 GB storage; the Workers Paid plan is $5 a month and includes 25 billion row reads and 50 million row writes a month, then $0.001 per million reads and $1.00 per million writes, with no egress charge. Time Travel gives point-in-time recovery to any minute in the last 30 days; a single database is capped at 10 GB. developers.cloudflare.com

C industry Cloudflare Workers limits (2026): CPU time per request of 30 seconds by default on the paid plan, configurable to 5 minutes, against 10 milliseconds on the free plan, with 128 MB of memory per isolate including WebAssembly allocations. developers.cloudflare.com

C industry godot-rust (gdext) documentation and issue tracker: mobile support is described as experimental, with iOS and Android tracking issues open and tooling still incomplete; on iOS a GDExtension is built as a static library linked into the application binary. github.com/godot-rust/gdext

B moderate Cross-platform determinism practice: IEEE 754 defines basic operator results but transcendental functions are library-dependent and may differ between platforms and compilers, which is why lockstep and replay systems use integer or fixed-point arithmetic in the simulation. See Fiedler, "Floating Point Determinism", and the Rust fixed crate, which deliberately omits sine, cosine and square root for this reason.

B moderate Blackman, D., & Vigna, S., on xoshiro/xoroshiro generators: SplitMix64 is recommended to initialise the state from a 64-bit seed, since initialisation should use a generator radically different in nature to avoid correlation between similar seeds. prng.di.unimi.it

C industry Apple App Review Guideline 2.5.2: applications must be self-contained in their bundles and may not download, install or execute code which introduces or changes features or functionality. Code compiled into the signed binary at build time is not affected.

B moderate Study of 144 autonomous self-modification cycles on a Python project: the test suite remained entirely green while the codebase decayed structurally, with 59 of 69 modules imported by nothing. Related benchmarks report agents breaking existing behaviour in a large share of maintenance iterations.

B moderate METR time-horizon research: the length of task a frontier model completes with 50% reliability has roughly doubled every seven months, accelerating to about four months since 2024; for the leading model of late 2025 the 50% horizon was measured at about 4 hours 49 minutes (95% interval 1 hour 49 minutes to 20 hours 25 minutes) while the 80% horizon was about 27 minutes. Confidence intervals are wide and the measure is of human-equivalent task length, not of session length. metr.org

D opinion Rosewater, M. (2018), "Quite the Rarity", Magic: The Gathering design column: rarity is assigned by complexity, size of effect and excitement — "being complex makes a card go up in rarity, but being a higher rarity does not necessarily mean a card must be complex". Authoritative primary source on rarity philosophy, but designer opinion rather than measurement.

C industry Counter-evidence that rarity tracks power in practice: community consensus and platform documentation for Magic and Hearthstone describe higher rarities as carrying the most powerful effects. Marvel Snap is the exception, where card rarity is explicitly cosmetic and affects collection level rather than strength.

B moderate Collection-set design synthesis: sets of roughly seven to twelve items are where completion motivation is strongest, with the endowed-progress effect taking hold once about half a set is held. Practitioner synthesis of the behavioural literature already cited in 19.19.

C industry Player reaction to calendar-gated directional farming: the long-running campaign against daily domain rotation in a major live game, where players argue the rotation removes the motivation to develop characters and is incompatible with modern design. Community sentiment, not measurement.

D opinion The admired version of directional farming: hunting a specific target for a specific part, always available, with legible drop rates. Direction comes from the player's goal rather than a calendar.

C industry Offence-and-defence unit allocation in base builders: defending troops that survive return at full strength while attacking troops do not, making the allocation a genuine trade rather than a loss; other games trade offensive or production capacity directly for defence.

B moderate Supercell developer communication (March 2026) on balancing the distribution of attack outcomes rather than the average, and treating a compressed distribution with little middle ground as an unhealthy polarised meta; with documented base-design practice where walls funnel, traps punish demonstrated routes, and reserve defenders are the hidden win condition.

C industry Community estimates of attacker success in a comparable base builder at roughly 98%, against a far lower defence-hold rate. Undisclosed method; used only to show how attacker-sided punishing games are, by contrast with this one.

D opinion Walsh, M. (2014), "Modeling Perception and Awareness in Tom Clancy's Splinter Cell: Blacklist", GDC and Game AI Pro 2, chapter 28: nested vision shapes rather than a single cone, line of sight sampled at several points on the body, detection time scaling with distance, and off-screen guards hearing at half strength for fairness. Authoritative developer account.

D opinion Miles, B., "How to Catch a Ninja" (Mark of the Ninja), Game AI Pro chapter 32: interest sources as data with small integer priorities, and sound propagated along a navigation mesh rather than through walls.

D opinion Magic: The Gathering Comprehensive Rules, section 613: continuous effects applied in fixed layers, then timestamp order, then dependency, with a defined rule for simultaneous ties. The most battle-tested deterministic modifier-resolution algorithm in games.

A strong Rhodes, R. E., & de Bruijn, G.-J. (2013). How big is the physical activity intention-behaviour gap? A meta-analysis using the action control framework (10 studies, N=3,899): an overall gap of 46%. British Journal of Health Psychology, 18(2), 296–309.

A strong Webb, T. L., & Sheeran, P. (2006). Does changing behavioral intentions engender behavior change? A meta-analysis of experimental evidence (47 tests): a medium-to-large change in intention (d = 0.66) produced only a small-to-medium change in behaviour (d = 0.36). Psychological Bulletin, 132(2), 249–268.

A strong Gallus, J. (2017). Fostering public good contributions with symbolic awards: a large-scale natural field experiment at Wikipedia (over 4,000 new editors): purely symbolic awards raised newcomer retention by 20%, with effects lasting more than a year. Management Science, 63(12). See also Restivo & van de Rijt (2012), PLOS ONE, where a single peer award raised median output 60% over ninety days.

B moderate IJsselsteijn, de Kort & Poels, the Game Experience Questionnaire: a three-item tension subscale scored 0 to 4. Its psychometrics are contested — reported reliability between 0.3 and 0.74 and a factor structure that has not fully replicated — so it is used alongside behaviour and observation, never alone.

D opinion Mimimi Games on Shadow Tactics and Desperados III: one vision shape shown at a time, sharp edges with no gradients, a fill that travels visibly from the guard's eyes toward the target, and identical shapes across guards so the rule is learned once.

C industry Clash of Clans loot mechanics, documented by the community and corroborated by Supercell patch notes: a percentage of the defender's storages by town-hall level (20% at level 6 falling to 10% at 11), a cap rising by 50,000 a level, half of what sits in collectors with no cap, and a penalty table paying 100% against equals down to 5% four levels below. A transfer economy, which is why its percentages do not transfer to a minting one.

C industry Boom Beach's vault: a fixed amount fully protected with only the excess exposed, loot locked in at first scout, and a guarantee that a raider can never come away with nothing. The closest shipped precedent to the sealed share in 9.4.

D opinion Practitioner guidance on faucets and sinks: an uncapped faucet collapses currency value, and the controls are soft caps, diminishing returns and daily or weekly limits, watched through the ratio of what is created to what is removed and the growth in what players hold. Unity's economy design guide and consultancy writeups; converging practice rather than settled science.

C industry Base builders pace progression through hard building-count caps unlocked per level rather than through cost alone — for example a fixed total of buildings permitted at each town-hall level.

A strong Shapley value as the standard method for attributing an outcome among contributing parts, satisfying efficiency, symmetry and additivity; applied to win-rate attribution in a live game through a win-probability model. arXiv:2506.04602, with the formal treatment in arXiv:2404.01078.

A strong Sample sizes for rate estimates: about 400 trials for a five-point interval and about 1,600 per arm to detect a five-point difference at conventional confidence and power; automated and simulated playtesting for balance attribution is established practice (Zook et al., FDG 2015; Foldit, AIIDE 2018).

B moderate Idempotency keys as the standard exactly-once mechanism in payment and mobile APIs: derive the key from the business fields rather than a random value, store it with an atomic upsert, and return the original result on a repeat.

C industry Purchase reconciliation: server-side receipt validation is the source of truth, unacknowledged purchases are automatically refunded by Google Play after three days, and a restore-purchases flow resolves the large majority of entitlement support tickets.

B moderate Wen, F., Nagy, C., Bavota, G., & Lanza, M. (2019). A large-scale empirical study on code-comment inconsistencies. ICPC 2019, 53–64. doi:10.1109/ICPC.2019.00019. 1,500 GitHub projects, 1.3 billion syntax-tree-level changes extracted to compare how comments and code move together, and 500 commits manually tagged as fixing a comment that had stopped being true.

A strong METR (2025). Recent frontier models are reward hacking. Reward hacking observed in 30.4% of runs (39 of 128) on RE-Bench and in 21 of 21 runs on one task; across four prompt variations at 20 runs each on that task, rates were 80% (original), 80% ("do not cheat"), 70% ("do not reward hack") and 95% ("solve it the way the designer intended") — instructing the model not to hack had a negligible effect, and the intent framing was the worst of the four. metr.org.

A strong Just, R., Jalali, D., Inozemtseva, L., Ernst, M. D., Holmes, R., & Fraser, G. (2014). Are mutants a valid substitute for real faults in software testing? FSE 2014, 654–665. doi:10.1145/2635868.2635929. 357 real reproducible faults across 5 Java projects (321 KLOC), 230,000 mutants and 35,141 test suites; mutant detection tracked real fault detection in 73% of cases against 50–60% for statement coverage, and 10% of real faults could not be represented by any mutant.
