29.2 Glossary

| Word
| Meaning

| Keeper
| The player

| Sage
| A hero who bends rules: the Archivist, the Tinkerer, the Gardener, the Wanderer

| Visitor
| A trader who arrives at the Dock: the Cartographer, the Collector, the Stray

| Universe
| A player's cluster of islands: the Heart and up to six outer realms

| Realm
| One floating island with its own laws and terrain

| Heart
| The central realm holding the Spire and the Vault

| Law, page
| A rule of nature written into an island; a torn page of the Book of Laws, collected to restore a law

| Crown law, realm law
| A law that works in every realm; a law that works in one realm

| School, rank
| One of six families of laws, each with a colour and shape; common, rare or deep

| Note
| One of eight qualities a law carries: Flow, Weight, Glow, Hush, Bloom, Echo, Spark, Veil

| Harmony, dissonance
| Laws sharing a note; laws with opposite notes

| Signature resonance, chord
| A named bonus between two specific laws; three notes together in one realm

| Paradox
| A rare, wild law found only in the Lab

| Pylon, Anchor Stone
| The building that holds a law; the building that adds stability

| Stability, strain, band, fault
| How much law a universe can hold; how much it's using; one of seven levels of strain; a crack raiders can use when a universe is overloaded

| Ruleborn, crew
| A character born from an island's laws; the characters and Sage sent on a heist

| Form
| One of eight looks and quirks a ruleborn hatches with, set by the laws around its Nursery

| Island Workshop
| The sandbox where players build islands and publish them for everyone to play

| Friendly heist, co-op heist
| A practice raid on a friend's island that costs them nothing; a live heist with two or three friends

| Challenge link, highlight clip
| An island sent to a friend as a playable challenge; a 15-second video of a heist's best moment

| Skiff
| The small flying boat a crew lands and leaves in

| Heist, ambush
| A timed run to open another universe's Vault; a first hit on a guard that hasn't noticed the crew

| Echo universe, rehearsal
| A recent snapshot of a real universe offered when targets are scarce; a simulated heist against your own universe

| Sealed share, Renown
| The part of the Vault that can't be stolen; the score that sets your league

| Constellation, Grand Law
| A clan; the law it votes to share with every member

| Anomaly, era, epoch
| A boss fought together; a weekly rule change for everyone; a voluntary rebirth that keeps your laws

30The research behind this document

Six evidence reviews were commissioned while this document was written, drawing on roughly 200 sources between them. Their conclusions are applied throughout the sections above; this part records what each one found, how strong that evidence is, where the reviews contradict each other, and what none of them can settle. It is the audit trail for every decision in 27.

30.1 How to read this

Each review answered a different question, and each is summarised below with its evidence, its grade, and what it changed in the design. 30.8 joins their conclusions, 30.9 records where they disagree, 30.10 is the measurement plan that falls out of all six, and 30.11 states what none of them can settle.

The six reviews

| Review
| Question it answered
| Where the evidence is strong
| Where it is weak

| 1. Fulfilment and the endgame
| What makes players feel fulfilled over months and years, rather than merely engaged?
| Self-determination theory, passion, deliberate practice, guild social capital, creator crowding-out
| Whether meaningfulness predicts continued play at all

| 2. Ten operational gaps
| Stealth, matchmaking, economy, soft launch, pricing, safety, localisation, accessibility, devices, creator law
| Matchmaking and churn, virtual-economy inflation, pricing regulation, child-safety prevalence
| Localisation return, device figures, moderation efficacy

| 3. Human factors
| Is the game physically legible and playable on a phone?
| Touch targets, latency, reading psychophysics, visual search, working memory
| Colour psychology, animation timing, stealth-geometry thresholds

| 4. Operations and production
| Will it ship, and can a small team run it?
| Churn prediction, bot detection, crash benchmarks, project failure rates, AI-assisted productivity
| Live-ops cadence, support benchmarks, toxicity-retention causality

| 5. Creative content
| Are the laws, creatures, realms and concept the right size and shape?
| Working-memory limits, endowed progress, anthropomorphism, sound symbolism, novelty penalty
| Most of it is design craft, not science — stated as such

| 6. Load-bearing premises
| Are the assumptions the whole concept rests on actually true?
| Playing an absent human, non-violence, moral disengagement, asymmetric role preference
| Delegated agency measurement, one-way theft (no direct study exists)

How sources are graded

The same scheme runs through all six reviews, and matches the grading applied to every source in 28.1.

| Grade
| What it is
| How it may be used

| A — strong
| Meta-analysis of experiments, randomised experiment, or behaviour logged across thousands of players
| May carry a decision on its own, with the effect discounted

| B — moderate
| Peer-reviewed but smaller, correlational, or one step removed from our context
| Supports a decision alongside an A source; never carries one alone

| C — industry
| Market and benchmark data, platform documentation, studio analysis; method usually undisclosed
| Market shape, prices and benchmarks only. Never used to claim an effect on players

| D — opinion
| Designer and practitioner writing, however well argued
| Hypotheses and design patterns only

The discount rule, applied throughout
Published effects are treated as upper bounds. Psychology replicates at about 36% and social science at 62%, both at roughly half the original effect size, and meta-analytic estimates have run about three times larger than preregistered multi-laboratory replications of the same effects. Every target derived from this research assumes the effect arrives smaller here.

30.2 Review 1 — Fulfilment and the endgame

What makes people feel fulfilled over months and years, and what destroys that feeling in the late game.

The findings that mattered

| Finding
| Evidence
| Grade

| Appreciation — being moved, finding something meaningful — is measurably distinct from fun, on a validated three-item scale
| Oliver & Bartsch 2010, pilot 141 and study 268; cross-culturally validated on US (262) and German (274) samples
| A

| But no study shows appreciation predicts continued play. A two-wave panel of 2,561 people found eudaimonic preference predicted later meaning in life only negligibly, and sometimes in reverse
| Frontiers in Communication 2022; the main survey of eudaimonic game motives says outright it could not test predictive value for actual game use
| A

| Poorly met needs predict more hours with less enjoyment and more tension afterwards; well-met needs predict enjoyment and energy but not hours
| Przybylski, Rigby & Ryan and colleagues
| A

| Need frustration is a separate construct from low satisfaction and is what drives people out
| Ballou & Deterding 2023, grounded theory
| A

| Harmonious and obsessive players differ in harm at the same number of hours; obsessive players keep playing while exhausted
| Vallerand's dualistic model; Lafrenière et al. 2009 (222 players); a 2025 study of 597 long-term players
| A/B

| Deliberate practice explains about 26% of skill variance in games, and roughly 5% when measured from logs rather than recall
| Macnamara, Hambrick & Oswald 2014 meta-analysis
| A

| Mastery-approach goals predict intrinsic motivation and lower anxiety; appearance-based performance goals correlate negatively with performance
| Hulleman et al. 2010, 243 studies
| A

| Guilds average about 17 members with a median of 9 and dissolve often; commitment, not membership, predicts staying
| Ducheneaut et al. 2006; investment-model study, 165 players
| A/B

| High monetary incentives crowd out motivation specifically among the already highly motivated
| Experimental economics, 2022; Deci, Koestner & Ryan 1999, 128 experiments
| A

| Creator earnings concentrate brutally: a median creator receiving about $1,575 a year while the top ten average $33.9 million
| Platform economic reporting
| C

What it changed in the game

The endgame thesis — a place, a reputation, a body of work — is labelled a hypothesis with a test attached, not a mechanism we rely on.

Kill criteria: playtime up with enjoyment down is the obsessive-passion signature, not success. Any mechanic that raises need frustration without raising meaning is removed whatever it earns.

Crews of 4 to 8 inside constellations, because being one of 30 is not belonging.

Seasons without pressure: nothing expires forever, catch-up built in, no countdowns.

Mastery is self-referenced; global ranking is secondary and optional.

Fading players get a legacy card, not a win-back campaign.

Creator payment, when it was later added, was structured to resemble the unexpected rewards that did not undermine interest.

30.3 Review 2 — Ten operational gaps

Stealth, matchmaking, economy, soft launch, pricing, safety, localisation, accessibility, devices and creator law.

The findings that mattered

| Area
| Finding
| Grade

| Stealth
| Contextual guard dialogue let a simple AI match a complex one in player enjoyment. Telegraphing buys as much perceived intelligence as expensive AI.
| A

| Near misses
| Near misses raise arousal and motivation to continue but are rated negatively in valence — a wanting-not-liking mechanism associated with problem gambling. Engineering their frequency is an ethical hazard.
| A

| Time pressure
| Under time pressure people lower their decision boundary and switch to heuristics: faster and less accurate. Hard timers on a touchscreen systematically degrade decision quality.
| A

| Matchmaking
| In about 6 million matches from 262,000 players, churn rose when players met stronger opponents, and being matched with weaker opponents reduced churn more than fair matches did. A short-term win rate near 50% is roughly optimal for satisfaction — players want to win slightly more than they lose.
| A

| Economy
| A quasi-experimental study of a transaction tax plus item sink showed sinks measurably stabilise prices. Diablo III is the canonical failure: gold faucets everywhere, trivial sinks, hyperinflation.
| A/B

| Soft launch
| Reading day-1 retention near 30% to within ±3 points needs about 900 installs per cell. Cheap-market data does not predict tier-one monetisation.
| B/C

| Pricing
| An incentivised randomised trial with 753 participants found willingness to pay is significantly higher in virtual currency than in real money even at a 1:1 rate — money illusion. EU guidance now targets exactly this.
| A

| Child safety
| One in three boys aged 9 to 12 reported an online sexual interaction in 2024; 40% of minors report being approached by someone they believed was trying to manipulate them. Classifiers reach 70 to 97% accuracy; hash-matching is the reliable baseline for known material.
| A/B

| Devices
| Android dominates every target market (India ~95%, Indonesia ~87 to 90%, Brazil ~81 to 84%). Google Play's bad-behaviour thresholds are a crash rate of 1.09% and an ANR rate of 0.47%.
| B/C

| Creator law
| Fortnite pays creators 18+ only with a $100 minimum and a tax interview; Roblox pays 13+ with identity verification. EU DAC7 has no minimum threshold for personal services.
| A (documentation)

What it changed, and the three things it said were wrong

Called wrong

Virtual-currency obfuscation. Do not price cosmetics in a soft currency at a non-obvious rate. The randomised trial shows it inflates spending through money illusion, and it is now a regulatory target.

Targeting strict 50/50 matchmaking. The large-sample evidence says the median heist should be winnable, not perfectly fair.

Free chat for minors. Given the prevalence data, the minor cohort should default to preset-phrase chat.

It also produced the warning that a "defenders lose nothing" rule, if it mints resources rather than transferring them, is an uncontrolled inflation faucet — which is why the design now draws hauls from a snapshot with sink-limited buffers.

30.4 Review 3 — Human factors and perception

Whether the game is physically legible and playable on a phone. This is the review with the largest samples of all six.

The numbers the evidence supports

| Question
| What the evidence says
| Source and size
| Grade

| How big must a touch target be?
| Error rates only flatten near 9 to 10 mm. Below 8 mm error rises above 40%. Edges and corners are markedly worse. The finger's contact patch sets a precision floor that slowing down cannot overcome.
| 120,626,225 touch events from 91,731 installations; Parhi et al. 2006; FFitts 2013
| A

| How fast must it respond?
| Dragging latency is noticed at about 11 ms and degrades performance above 25 ms; discrete taps tolerate about 69 ms. Frame rate: 30 Hz measurably hurts performance; above 60 Hz the differences are not significant.
| Deber et al. CHI 2015; Jota et al. 2013; refresh-rate study F(4,100)=13.776, p<.001
| A

| How small can text be?
| Reading speed is constant while x-height is between 0.2° and 2.0° of visual angle and collapses below that. A line needs at least 13 characters to reach 80% of maximum reading speed. WCAG's 4.5:1 derives from 3:1 × 1.5 for 20/40 acuity — and its exact factor is contested, particularly for dark themes.
| Legge's 20-article series; PNAS 2020
| A

| How many symbols can be searched?
| Pop-out search is near flat; conjunction search runs about 28 to 45 ms per item, with target-absent slopes roughly twice as steep. Icon characteristics explain up to 69% of variance in identification.
| Treisman & Gelade 1980; Wolfe 1998 (one million trials); Isherwood et al. 2007
| A

| How many rules can interact at once?
| People reason about at most one quaternary relation — four interacting variables. Accuracy interpreting interactions is high at two and three way and falls sharply at four.
| Halford, Wilson & Phillips 1998; Halford et al. 2005
| A

| How do people perceive progress?
| Magnitude perception is logarithmic, cross-culturally. A goal that starts partly filled is finished faster: about 34% versus 19% completion for identical effort.
| Dehaene et al. 2008 (Mundurucú); Nunes & Drèze 2006; Kivetz et al. 2006 (948 members)
| A

| How close must sound and picture be?
| The audio-visual simultaneity window is about 185 ms wide, so offsets under ±100 ms feel synchronous.
| Van der Burg, Cass & Alais 2014
| A

| Does art style matter?
| The uncanny valley is real but condition-dependent (Hedges g ≈ 1.01 across 247 effects). Stylised creatures avoid it entirely. Colour-emotion claims are the weakest area in the review.
| Diel, Weigelt & MacDorman 2022
| A / weak for colour

What it said the design got wrong

A 44-point touch minimum is below the evidence. Primary heist controls should be 9 to 10 mm, and kept off screen corners.

The complexity budget was measuring the wrong thing. The limit is not how many laws are unfamiliar but how many interact at once: never more than four, at any level of play.

No pre-launch usage cap can be promised. Imbalance surfaces only at expert level and at scale, with millions of matches.

It also confirmed, with numbers to cite, what the document had asserted: 60 frames per second as a floor, 16-pixel body text, at most eight controls, and 200 to 300 millisecond transitions.

30.5 Review 4 — Operations and production

Whether it ships, and whether a small team can run it. The review that produced the most uncomfortable findings.

The findings that mattered

| Area
| Finding
| Grade

| Telemetry
| Churn is predictable from behaviour at 66 to 95% accuracy depending on window. The single most important feature across studies is simply current absence time — a signal any team can compute.
| A

| Anti-cheat
| Behavioural bot detection reaches 96 to 98% accuracy on real game data. Cheating spreads socially: players adopt it after observing or being victimised by it.
| A

| Netcode
| Real mobile networks run 50 to 150 ms round trip with heavy jitter, and 5G is frequently slower than 4G in practice. But strategy and cooperative play tolerate latency far better than shooters — some games are unaffected up to a second.
| A/B

| Notifications
| Contextual notifications open at 14.4% versus 4.19% for generic. But one weekly push causes about 10% of users to disable notifications and three to six causes about 40%. Around 30% of consumers delete an app over excessive notifications.
| B/C

| QA
| Median crash-free sessions run about 99.9%; apps below roughly 99.82% tend to score under three stars, and half of one-star reviews mention crashes. The 1:10:100 cost-of-late-fixes curve is folklore — the revised figure for small agile projects is about 5:1.
| B

| Playtesting
| "Five users find 85% of problems" holds mathematically but fails in practice: some five-user sets found 99%, others 55%. Ten raises the worst case to 80%, twenty to 95%. Usability testing does not tell you whether a game is fun.
| A

| Production risk
| Only about 31% of software projects fully succeed — but small projects succeed around 90% of the time against under 10% for large ones. Eight weeks of 60-hour weeks produces the same output as eight weeks of 40-hour weeks, with more defects.
| C (contested) / A

| AI-assisted development
| A randomised trial of 16 experienced developers on 246 real tasks found they were 19% slower with AI tools while believing they were 20% faster. The durable finding is the calibration failure, not the specific number.
| A

| Toxicity
| Analysing 2.5 million players and 18.25 million matches: toxic teammates significantly predicted newcomers quitting, but had no significant effect once players passed level 30. The strongest positive predictor for experienced players was the number of friends in the match.
| A

What it said the design got wrong

Called wrong

Any schedule built on optimistic estimates or assumed AI speedups. The highest-confidence wrong call in all six reviews.

Real-time co-op as scoped. It imposes the full netcode cost profile on a tiny team for a mode that is not the core loop. Build it asynchronous first.

Under-budgeting live-ops content against consumption.

Treating notifications as a growth lever by default.

30.6 Review 5 — Creative content

Whether the laws, creatures, realms and the concept itself are the right size and shape. Mostly design craft, and labelled as such.

The verdict on every count in the game

| Count
| Verdict
| Why

| 6 schools
| Right-sized
| Between Magic's five colours and Genshin's seven elements; below the eighteen-type threshold where Pokémon's chart became a memorisation burden and a balance liability

| 8 notes
| At the ceiling — test 6 or 7
| Above the four-chunk reasoning limit; acceptable only because notes are modifiers learned in context, not standalone factions

| 48 laws
| Acceptable, and the right growth axis
| Low dependency centrality: laws can be added or cut with contained blast radius. Audit for laws that do not interact

| 44 resonances + 12 chords
| Too large to teach, right size to discover
| No player will memorise 56 interactions. They must be surfaced reactively, never as a table

| 144 creature forms
| Within precedent, at risk of dilution
| Pokémon's first generation was 151. But eight note-forms risk reading as recolours; they must differ by more than palette

| 7 realms
| Right-sized
| Matches Genshin's seven nations. The challenge is instant readability, not count

| 39 terrain features
| Too many to be individually legible
| Fine as ambient texture; separate a handful of gameplay-relevant features from the rest

The findings behind those verdicts

The colour pie's real lesson: its purpose is to make factions feel and play differently, not to balance them. Identity first.

Lenticular design: a law should read as simple to a newcomer and reveal depth to an expert. This is now the law-writing rule.

Notes are the highest-centrality nodes in the whole system — each touches multiple laws, every resonance and every chord. Changing a note propagates everywhere; changing a law does not. So the note count should be frozen and laws used for expansion.

Players largely do not read lore. Destiny removed its 834 Grimoire cards because they lived outside the game; Bethesda has no telemetry on book-reading at all and designs its 800-odd books as atmosphere. Build evocative implication, not text.

Sound symbolism is real and cross-cultural (976 participants, 25 languages), so note names can be chosen to sound like what they do — several already do.

Novelty carries a measured commercial penalty. Across 51,411 songs, every type of novelty was negatively associated with popularity, around 5 to 6% per unit. The answer is anchored differentiation: signal a familiar genre in five seconds, keep the novelty for after the hook.

The review's sharpest warning: the invented-vocabulary stack — laws, notes, schools, chords, resonances, ruleborn, realms — is learnable because the words are repurposed English rather than invented, but only if introduced one at a time. A glossary dump on the store page or in the tutorial turns it into a barrier.

30.7 Review 6 — The load-bearing premises

The eight assumptions the whole concept rests on, each checked for the first time.

| Premise
| Verdict
| Confidence
| Key evidence

| 1. Playing against an absent human feels different from playing an AI
| Supported
| High
| A meta-analysis of 25 effect sizes found a medium-to-large penalty for computer opponents (g = 0.631), with arousal effects near g = 1.2. Eleven of twenty studies held behaviour identical and changed only the label — so much of it is a pure belief effect

| 2. People enjoy designing a space for a stranger to fail in
| Supported
| Moderate-high
| Mario Maker's troll levels and "first clear" culture; a community that spent years clearing 41,113 uncleared courses before the servers closed. Designing-to-defeat is a real, distinct creative pleasure

| 3. Delegated control still feels like agency
| Conditionally supported
| Moderate
| Agency survives delegation when outcomes are predictable from inputs. The failure mode is "my units are stupid" and the tipping point where a game feels watched rather than played. Intentional binding as a measure has contested replication — measure felt control directly

| 4. A non-violent game can carry tension and an audience
| Supported
| High commercially, moderate on tension
| Cozy games grew from 0.4% to 3.1% of titles grossing over $100k between 2022 and 2025. Controlling for competition, violence adds nothing to enjoyment: violent and non-violent versions did not differ in rated enjoyment or excitement

| 5. Players will enjoy being a thief without guilt
| Conditionally supported
| Moderate
| Transgressive play is enjoyable when the game supplies consequence-minimisation. One-way theft removes the victim entirely — textbook mitigation. The residual tension is that premise 1 wants the defender to feel human

| 6. Authoring rules is a satisfying fantasy
| Supported, but niche
| Moderate-high
| Baba Is You is the existence proof; god games peaked commercially around 2000 and have not returned. Rule-authorship is loved by a dedicated minority and is not by itself a mass-market hook — it must be tactile and instantly legible

| 7. Players will build defences, not just raid
| Largely contradicted
| Moderate-high
| Every comparable game shows the building half is neglected. Dead by Daylight needed a 400% reward bonus to fill the less-popular role. The vast copy-a-base ecosystem around Clash is itself evidence most players clone rather than design

| 8. The four-genre combination coheres
| Unsupported to risky
| Moderate
| Hybrid-casual grew strongly, but "hybrid" there means a simple core with meta-depth, not four genres at once. Genre clarity predicts discoverability and revenue; a game that fits no tag is hard to position

The single riskiest assumption in the whole concept

That a meaningful share of players will design islands and their laws as a defence. Being wrong here does not weaken one system — it starves the Workshop, removes the human presence that premise 1 depends on, and collapses the content ecosystem. Every comparable asymmetric and base-defence game points the same way.

The mitigations, in order: make a short guided defence build mandatory before anyone can raid; seed the Workshop heavily so raiders never run dry; and route the best rewards to whoever designs islands other people play. Benchmark: if fewer than 15 to 20% of active players publish an island in their first week, the loop is under-supplied.

30.8 The joined conclusions

All six reviews, resolved into four lists: what to change, what to lock, what to test, and what could kill the project.

Decisions the evidence says are wrong

| Decision
| Why it is wrong
| What to do instead
| From

| Schedules built on optimistic estimates or assumed AI speedups
| The planning fallacy plus a randomised trial showing developers were 19% slower with AI while feeling 20% faster
| Reference-class forecasting; no AI multiplier in the plan; explicit review time for generated code
| R4

| Assuming players will design defences
| Every comparable game shows the building half is neglected
| Mandatory lightweight defence build, heavy seeding, defender-favouring rewards
| R6

| Shipping as a four-genre hybrid
| Genre clarity predicts discoverability; novelty carries a measured commercial penalty
| One dominant framing in five seconds, the rest through progressive disclosure
| R5, R6

| Virtual-currency obfuscation
| A randomised trial shows it inflates spending through money illusion even at 1:1; EU guidance targets it
| Real-money prices, or a transparent 1:1 rate
| R2

| Strict 50/50 matchmaking
| Churn rose with stronger opponents; weaker opponents reduced churn more than fair matches
| Make the median heist winnable
| R2

| A 44-point touch minimum
| Real-world error rates only flatten near 9 to 10 mm; edges and corners are worse
| 9 to 10 mm primary controls, away from corners
| R3

| A complexity budget counting unfamiliar laws
| The limit is interacting elements, not novelty: about four
| Never more than four interacting rules at any level
| R3

| Free chat for minors
| Measured grooming prevalence and off-platform migration risk
| Preset-phrase chat for the minor cohort
| R2

| Real-time co-op as scoped
| Full netcode cost for a mode outside the core loop
| Asynchronous co-op first; real-time only if playtests demand it
| R4

| Teaching the 44 resonances and 12 chords
| Nobody memorises 56 interactions
| Surface them reactively, in play
| R5

| Notifications as a growth lever
| Three to six pushes a week make about 40% disable them
| Contextual only, low frequency, opt-in requested after value is seen
| R4

| A 5% Workshop publishing target
| Creative communities run at roughly 1% creating, 9% editing
| 1.5% publishing, 10% remixing, plus a remix path
| R2

Decisions the evidence supports — lock them

| Decision
| What supports it

| Server-validated deterministic replay
| The strongest anti-cheat posture available, and it doubles as golden-replay regression testing

| Cosmetic only, plus a season pass
| Avoids paid randomness and its correlation with problem gambling; removes pay-to-win refund pressure; sidesteps most loot-box regulation. The season pass's shape matches where the industry converged

| One-way theft
| Removes the victim, which is the cue that would otherwise produce guilt or griefing; and it protects the defender from uncontrollable loss, the purest form of need frustration

| Non-violence
| Violence adds nothing to enjoyment once competition is controlled for, and the cozy audience is growing and demographically distinct

| Foregrounding the absent human
| A medium-to-large effect on enjoyment and a large one on arousal, much of it from belief alone

| Portrait-first, asynchronous, latency-tolerant core
| Thumb reach, session shape, and the fact that async needs no netcode at all

| Tight scope and a small team
| Small projects succeed around 90% of the time against under 10% for large ones

| A crash-free gate of 99.9%
| Below about 99.82% apps tend to fall under three stars

| Six schools and seven realms
| Both sit in the learnable band between Magic and Pokémon

| The Workshop as core infrastructure
| It is the only credible answer to the content treadmill — provided premise 7 is solved

What only our own playtests can settle

Whether an asynchronous, no-loss heist generates enough tension to be fun. No study covers this loop.

Whether delegated crews feel like playing or watching. Threshold: at least 70% agreeing they felt in control.

Whether players build defences at all, and at what rate.

Whether the store page communicates the game in five seconds.

Whether the vocabulary clears a 70% post-tutorial comprehension bar.

Whether eight notes is one too many for a day-one mental model.

Whether the 144-form collection sustains a chase, and where it drops off.

Whether the heist clock is a pleasure or a stressor.

Whether portrait or landscape is chosen when both are one tap away.

Whether the Island Fund crowds out the creators it is meant to reward.

Our own estimation error and our own AI speedup.

The sustainable content cadence, measured as consumption against production.

The risk register, ranked

| Rank
| Risk
| Why it ranks here
| Main mitigation

| 1
| Nobody builds defences
| Breaks the content ecosystem, the Workshop and the human-presence loop at once
| Mandatory light build, seeding, defender-favouring rewards, 15 to 20% first-week publishing benchmark

| 2
| Production and execution
| Base-rate failure plus the planning fallacy plus the AI calibration illusion; a small team cannot hire its way out
| Scope discipline, reference-class forecasting, no crunch

| 3
| The concept does not communicate
| Novelty carries a measured penalty and the game fits no clean genre tag
| Anchored differentiation, show-don't-tell store page, five-second comprehension test

| 4
| Cold start
| Async raiding, a Workshop and clans all need critical mass to be fun
| Echo universes, seeded islands, soft launch in few markets

| 5
| Content treadmill
| Shipping is likely; sustaining is the hard part
| The Workshop, cadence set from measured capacity

| 6
| Trust and safety at scale
| User-made content plus chat plus clans in twelve languages, with minors present
| Preset chat for minors, hash-matching, human review, DSA-compliant reporting

| 7
| Complexity overwhelms new players
| The four-rule limit against 48 laws and 56 combinations
| Staged unlocks, reactive surfacing, one new term per beat

| 8
| Device and performance reality
| Low-end Android across twelve markets
| Mid-range reference device, crash and ANR gates, small install size

| 9
| Revenue insufficiency
| Cosmetic-only earns less per player by design
| Deliberate trade; retention and reach must carry it

30.9 Where the reviews disagree

Six reviews commissioned separately do not produce one voice. The tensions matter more than the agreements.

| Tension
| The two sides
| Resolution

| Latency
| Human factors says responsiveness is critical (11 ms for drag). Operations says network latency barely matters for an asynchronous loop
| Both are right about different things. Spend on local input and feedback timing; spend nothing on network latency — unless real-time co-op is built, when both costs arrive at once

| Notifications
| Fulfilment research is wary of extrinsic nudges. Growth thinking wants notifications to drive returns
| The evidence resolves it in fulfilment's favour: contextual notifications about real events beat generic engagement blasts, which actively cause uninstalls

| Content velocity
| Live-ops says cadence sustains retention. Production says crunching to hit cadence raises defects and burns out a small team
| The Workshop, plus a cadence derived from measured capacity, and no public roadmap that requires crunch

| Playtest methods
| Human factors and playtest methodology value small-N qualitative work. Telemetry values large-N behaviour
| Five to eight users for usability, in three rounds; live behaviour for fun, balance and retention. Do not ask five testers a question only fifty thousand players can answer

| Humanising the defender
| Premise 1 says make the absent opponent feel human, because that is where the emotional charge lives. Premise 5 says the more human they feel, the more taking from them could feel wrong
| One-way theft is what makes both possible at once: enough humanity to feel real, not enough harm to feel cruel. This is the single most elegant interaction between reviews — and it needs a playtest

| Difficulty
| Earlier engagement research said choice, novelty and suspense beat difficulty tuning. Matchmaking research says target win rates slightly above even
| Not in conflict: let players choose difficulty openly, and set the default target pool so the median run is winnable

| Creator payment
| Fulfilment research says money crowds out the most motivated creators. The business case says unpaid creator economies age badly
| Taken knowingly: a fixed fund, paid in arrears with no published rate, capped to favour the tail, with a stated failure condition

30.10 The measurement plan that falls out of all six

| What to measure
| Instrument
| Threshold that forces a change

| Needs met and needs frustrated
| BANGS — measures both directions
| Frustration rising in a cohort, even with satisfaction flat

| Experience across eleven constructs
| PXI, or the eleven-item miniPXI live
| Mastery, curiosity, autonomy, control or goals below +1.5; anything below 0

| Meaning, separately from fun
| The three-item appreciation scale
| Tests the endgame hypothesis directly

| Harmonious against obsessive play
| The Passion Scale
| Playtime up while enjoyment and energy fall — the mechanic goes

| Kind of challenge
| CORGIS
| Cognitive and decision-making below the midpoint, or performative above it

| Belonging
| Investment Model Scale; the closeness pictogram
| Investment high but satisfaction low — players are trapped, not committed

| Whether it was worth it
| One tap after each session
| Below 4.3 out of 5

| Early churn
| Recency of absence, plus the funnel
| Triggers a legacy card, never a win-back campaign

| Stability
| Crash-free sessions, ANR rate
| Below 99.9%, or ANR above 0.47%

| Economy health
| Price index, coefficient of variation, wealth concentration
| Sustained inflation or a rising Gini among veterans

| The Island Fund
| Output and meaning among the top 200 creators, before and six months after
| Either falling — the fund is withdrawn

30.11 What this research cannot tell us

Whether Ruleborn is fun. Research rules out bad ideas and sets priors. It cannot confirm that a specific game is enjoyable. Only the vertical slice can, against the gate: seven of ten outside testers asking to play again.

Whether one-way theft carries tension. No study covers a loop where the defender loses nothing. This is the most important untested thing in the design.

Whether people will design defences. The evidence says probably not by default; it cannot say whether our specific mitigations work.

How to rate a player-built island's difficulty. The matchmaking literature is almost entirely live player-versus-player.

Whether preset chat is safer than filtered chat for minors. No direct comparison exists; we follow safety-by-design inference.

What localisation returns per language for a strategy game. Industry figures only, with undisclosed methods.

Which accessibility features move retention. The guidelines are endorsed but not experimentally validated.

Our own numbers. Every count in the game — 48 laws, 8 notes, 144 forms, a 150 to 240 second clock — sits inside a plausible range the literature can bound but not fix.

The honest summary
Six reviews and roughly 200 sources have produced a design that is unusually well grounded for its stage, and a clear-eyed list of the four or five things most likely to kill it. What they have not produced, and cannot, is evidence that the game is good. That comes from a playable heist and ten strangers.

30.12 Review 7 — Evidence gathering and minimum-cost operations

A seventh review was commissioned once the design was stable, to answer two questions the others had left open: how to gather primary evidence about this game rather than about games in general, and how to run it for as little money as possible given that development labour is free but every recurring bill is not.

| Finding
| What it changed

| The asynchronous, deterministic design avoids the two costs that dominate live games: egress and positional telemetry. Storage with no egress fee and per-request compute make the realistic bill roughly nothing at a thousand players, tens of dollars at ten thousand, and low hundreds at a hundred thousand
| The stack and the cost table in 24.1

| Because replays can be re-simulated, route and detection heatmaps can be regenerated server-side without the client ever sending coordinates
| Free spatial analytics (24.3)

| All of it depends on the simulation being bit-identical across processors, which floating-point arithmetic does not guarantee
| Fixed-point maths, and the determinism spike as the first task in the project (24.2, 26.7)

| Hosting player-uploaded images creates a legal duty to detect child sexual abuse material, and the tooling is priced for platforms rather than individuals
| Workshop islands are structured data only; no image uploads, ever (18.6, 20.10)

| In-app surveys answer at about 13%, and about 19% in entertainment apps; self-reported and logged play time diverge systematically
| The measurement plan in 25.4, and the rule that behaviour wins on fact while self-report wins on interpretation

| Both stores halve commission below a million dollars a year, act as seller of record for VAT, and handle withholding through a treaty form
| The business model in 22.5

| The unavoidable cash costs are small and mostly legal: store fees, a trade mark, an accountant, translation upkeep
| The checklist in 26.6 and the order of work in 26.7

30.13 Review 8 — Driving the cost to nothing

An eighth review asked how close to free this game can be run at a million monthly players, under three constraints the developer set: no machine learning anywhere, no trade mark registration, and nothing licensed or paid for.

| Finding
| What it changed

| The design's own properties — structured data, seeds and input logs, no uploads, preset phrases — put its whole traffic profile inside permanent free tiers. About 100 to 200 kilobytes per player per day means under a terabyte and a half a month at a million players, on storage that charges nothing for bandwidth
| The architecture and cost table in 24.1, and the byte budget in 24.4

| Most of the game can be delivered as immutable files, because an opponent is a snapshot rather than a person. Only publishing, validation, league tables and clan state need a machine that thinks, and those are write-light
| Static-first delivery, with serverless functions behind it (24.4, 24.6)

| The only unavoidable recurring cost is the Apple developer programme at $99 a year. Google is $25 once, a domain is optional, and the privacy policy is free to write and host
| The irreducible cost in 24.5 and the model in 22.5

| Preset-phrase chat removes the need for any moderation classifier, which is the only place this game would otherwise have used machine learning
| Deterministic moderation confirmed as the design, not a compromise (20.10)

| Not registering a trade mark is a real but bounded risk: unregistered rights exist in the market of actual use, both stores reserve names first-come, and both run disputes without requiring a registration
| The position and its mitigations in 26.6

| Public-domain and permissive licences are genuinely free; attribution chains, share-alike, non-commercial terms, "royalty-free" stock and desktop-only fonts are not. Generating the world in code avoids the question entirely
| Made, not licensed (21.11)

30.14 Review 9 — Code architecture and the build plan

A ninth review answered two engineering questions: how to build this so the same simulation runs on a phone and inside a server function, and how much work that actually is when the work is done by a coding agent.

| Finding
| What it changed

| The simulation must be written once and compiled twice — into the app and to WebAssembly — because two implementations would eventually disagree, and that disagreement is the one failure the whole design cannot absorb
| The Rust core in 24.7, and the engine row in 24

| Floating point cannot be made identical across processors, since transcendental functions are library-dependent. Fixed point, integer trigonometry and integer randomness are the standard answer in games that replay or lockstep
| The number rules in 24.7

| A native extension is linked into the signed application at build time, so it is not downloaded code under the store rules. A runtime WebAssembly loader on the device would be
| Recorded as a hard constraint (24.7)

| Rust extensions on mobile are officially experimental, with no confirmed commercial mobile game shipped on them. This is the thinnest part of the whole architecture
| The spike gate and the C# fallback trigger (24.2, 24.7)

| Re-simulating every heist would exceed the per-request compute budget for no security gain
| Sampled validation, corrected from the earlier "validate everything" rule (24)

| Agent-built codebases fail by writing tests that pass rather than tests that test — one study found a suite fully green while most modules ended up imported by nothing
| Hand-written golden fixtures and behaviour-asserting tests the agent cannot satisfy by cheating (24.9)

| Reliable autonomous completion tops out well below maximum capability: around half an hour of human-equivalent work, even where far longer tasks can be attempted
| The run calibration and the roadmap in 26.8

30.15 Review 10 — Raids, rarity and defence

A tenth review examined three weaknesses raised in design review: whether a raid has a real purpose, whether rarity can be finer without becoming power, and whether anything but laws actually defends a universe.

| Finding
| What it changed

| Bottleneck resources do drive a core loop, but the resented version is the one that decides when a player may pursue what they want. The admired version is always available and directional by the player's own goal
| Pages lean toward the school raided, with no clock gating (7.6)

| The proposal was wrong in one place: making pages obtainable only by raiding would punish the large share of players who prefer building, which an earlier review identified as the riskiest assumption in the design
| A slow Library trickle at home, with raiding worth two to three sessions of it. Primary, not sole

| Rarity is properly a dial for complexity, effect size and excitement rather than power — but no shipped competitive game has fully separated the two, so something else must do the balancing
| Five ranks, and scouting visibility charged against strength as the primary offset (7.9)

| Conditional, build-around power is enjoyed; flat drawbacks are resented
| Rare laws are powerful when a condition you control is met, rather than costly to own

| Sets of seven to twelve are where people push to finish; 48 reads as endless
| Collection goals grouped by school, eight each

| No single element stops an attack in the base builders this borrows from: walls funnel, traps punish the route, and held-back defenders are the hidden win condition
| Five layers of comparable weight, none above a quarter (13)

| Because theft here is one-way and painless, this game can afford a much higher defence-hold rate than a punishing base builder — and should, because that is what makes designing worth doing
| The fairness targets in 13.2, and the rule that the shape of outcomes matters more than the average

30.16 Review 11 — The four specifications and the three gates

An eleventh review answered the two questions that stood between the design and a first line of code: what exactly to build, and what to prove before building it.

| Finding
| What it changed

| Shipped stealth games use nested vision zones rather than one cone, a gradual meter rather than a binary, and sound that travels along paths rather than through walls
| The whole of 12.17, previously the least specified system in the document

| For a top-down game that shows its cones, the readable choice is full determinism: no probability in detection at all
| A design rule as much as a technical one, and required anyway by server re-simulation

| Modifier systems that must agree forever need a fixed resolution order, not an arbitrary one
| The four-step order in 8.7, and paradoxes written as "cannot" rather than "less"

| Thirty ticks a second, commands rather than state, a checksum every tick and one kept per second
| The contract in 24.10, and the confirmation that a heist fits in under two kilobytes

| Replays stay valid across patches only if they record the rules they were played under
| Pin-don't-patch, and the grace state for islands that a re-tune pushes over budget (24.11)

| Tension does not require loss: near-misses raise arousal even when nothing is lost, and more so when the player chose the action
| The first gate, and confidence that one-way theft is not a design flaw

| Stated willingness to create is close to worthless — the gap between intending and doing runs at about 46%
| The forced-then-free test and the three-way decision in 13.3

| Rust on mobile is experimental, with no shipped title found and the iOS issue still open
| The spike as a gate, and a fallback ladder decided in advance rather than in a panic (24.7)

30.17 Review 12 — The three blockers

A twelfth review answered the three things that stood between a finished design and a first line of code: what a raid pays, what a defence may hold and how its layers are measured, and what happens when the network is not there.

| Finding
| What it changed

| The base builders pay a percentage of what the defender holds — but in those games the loot leaves the defender. Here it is created, so copying them would tie the inflation rate to hoarding
| The haul is driven by the raider's own level, with the target's wealth clamped to a narrow band (9.6)

| One shipped game already has the sealed-vault pattern, including a guarantee that a raider never leaves with nothing
| The floor on the richness term, so a fully sealed island still pays

| An uncapped faucet collapses a currency, and the controls are caps, decay and diminishing returns — especially where there is no hard-currency sink, as here
| Per-raid, daily and weekly caps, with weekly set at five days rather than seven

| Base builders pace progression through slot limits per level, not through cost alone
| The budget table in 13.4, where limits rather than prices enforce the quarter ceiling

| Attributing an outcome among contributing parts is a solved problem with a standard method, already used to attribute win rates in a live game. With five layers there are only 32 combinations, so it can be computed exactly rather than approximated
| A layer's share becomes measurable: switch it off, re-run, divide the credit (13.4)

| Idempotency keys derived from the business fields are the standard way to make a submission exactly-once across retries
| The offline queue and the guarantee that a haul cannot be paid twice (24.12)

| Device clocks cannot be trusted in a game with cooldowns and seasons
| The server clock as the only clock

| Content made by a deleted account is not personal data once the name is severed; a banned account is different, and the law permits keeping the minimum needed to enforce it
| The policy in 20.12, including publishing and storage limits

30.18 The audit, and what it found

Version 2.3 was read end to end against itself by a coding agent, with the arithmetic re-run from the document's own tables. It returned sixty-four items. Most were not prose errors; they were the same fact written twice and only one copy updated.

| What it found
| What version 3.0 did

| Six systems specified twice and incompatibly: two detection models, two payout models differing by sixteen to thirty times, two rarity taxonomies, two defence budgets, two rosters, and production tables describing a universe the build rules forbid
| Each now has one answer. The superseded halves are deleted rather than left standing: 12.8 points to 12.17, 9.4 points to 9.6, the outcome table multiplies the one formula, 13.4 carries the only budget, 11.11 is the roster

| The master inflation dial was keyed to a level that does not exist — realms stop at five, the dial ran to ten. At level ten it was twenty-five times its value at five
| The dial is the target's Vault level, which does have ten (9.6)

| The Vault phase, called the heist's central decision, lasted two seconds and had one option, and 46% of a crew's carrying capacity could not be filled by any means described
| Every role loads, at a fifth of the old rate, so a crew fills in ten seconds — one decision at the cadence 12.6 sets for itself. And what you carry is what you bank (12.19)

| "Two ambush hits knock out a guard" was true for three of twenty-five pairings. A Mender needed 68 hits
| The ambush gains a floor of half the target's health, which makes the document's own sentence true by construction (12.8)

| The trade-off the whole design rests on did not bind: a crew raiding four times a day cost about 2% of production
| A crew on a heist is assigned to it and does not work, so the choice is five of thirty-eight rather than two thirds of one (5.8)

| The roster could staff a quarter of the universe the rules permitted, and a second producer doubled income against every pacing date
| One producer of each kind per realm, partial staffing that pays pro-rata, and scarcity connected to raiding rather than merely claimed (5.8)

| Movement, fights, wards and the Library were load-bearing and unspecified — including the faucet that keeps non-raiders progressing
| 12.18, 12.20, 10.4, and the rule that the crew never makes a decision the player did not make

| Every chord was reachable with two laws, and the hiding note was the scarcest in the game
| Three distinct laws, and eight or nine laws per note (8.8)

Two conclusions outlast the fixes. The first is structural: a table that exists only to summarise something else should be generated, never authored — four of the six conflicts had no other cause. The second is about method: the audit found several of these by multiplying the document's own numbers together, which nobody had done, and it recorded two of its own findings as wrong once it read the tables it had skipped.

31How this document is kept true

Every section above describes the game. This one describes the document, because a specification that disagrees with itself is worse than a short one: it is a specification that will be implemented twice, differently. Version 3.0 was audited by an agent that found 100 items, 53 of them open, and the largest single class was not bad design but stale text — a number changed in one place and left standing in three others. This section sets out the structure and the automated checks that make that class of failure fail the build instead of reaching the code.

31.1 What actually went wrong

The audit is worth reading as evidence rather than as a to-do list. Of the eight items in its documentation cluster, six were the same failure wearing different clothes:

| Item
| The contradiction
| How long it survived

| C7
| The simulation ran at 20 steps a second in 7.3 and 30 in 24.2
| Both numbers were written in the same revision

| C10
| 30 traits in the launch inventory; 31 in the table that lists them
| Two revisions

| C17
| The game was listed as its own fallback name, after the rename
| One revision

| C21
| The formulas appendix still encoded the detection model 12.8 had deleted, and the page costs of a rarity scheme that no longer existed
| Two revisions, through a section that presented itself as the fix

| C24
| 17 building types; the inventory said 16, and the seventeenth had three of its ten levels
| Three revisions

| C26
| Three laws read attributes — size, flight — that no table carried
| Since the laws were written

None of these is a hard problem. Each is a fact stated in more than one place, where one copy was updated and the others were not. The literature calls this drift and finds it everywhere it looks: a study of 1,500 GitHub projects extracted 1.3 billion syntax-tree-level changes to compare how comments and the code they describe move together, and manually tagged 500 commits whose only purpose was to fix a comment that had stopped being true [231]. The fix is not more care. Care is what produced the four copies.

31.2 One fact, one home

The rule the rest of this section implements: every fact has exactly one place it is stated, and every other appearance of it is either a cross-reference or generated from that place. The repository is split four ways — three that a person edits and one that is only ever output — to make the rule enforceable rather than aspirational.

| Where
| What lives there
| Who may edit it

| /spec
| Decisions. One file per system, written in prose, each stating what the game does and nothing about why
| A person, by hand

| /data
| The numbers: laws, creatures, buildings, terrain, constants. Machine-readable, and the only home of any figure that appears in more than one place
| A person, by hand, one file per kind

| /archive
| Why. The twelve evidence reviews, the sources, the reasoning, the decisions not taken
| Append-only; nothing here is ever a source of truth for behaviour

| /build
| This document, assembled from the three above
| Nobody. It is output

The split answers the audit's D10 directly, and it answers a practical problem too: this document is 80,000 words, and an agent asked to change one rule has to read a great deal of argument to find the one sentence that is normative. Separating the decision from its justification makes the normative surface small enough to hold in view. The reasoning is not discarded — it is where 30 already put it, and the cross-references between the two directions are checked.

31.3 Canonical constants

A constant is any figure used by more than one system. Each is defined once, in the section named below, and every other mention in the document is checked against that definition rather than trusted.

| Constant
| Value
| Unit
| Defined in

| tick rate
| 30
| simulation steps per second
| 24.10 The shape of one heist, in numbers

| law count
| 48
| laws
| 7.4 The law table

| crew size
| 3 to 5
| crew members per heist (3, 4 from Keeper 8, 5 from Keeper 16), plus a Sage
| 12.5 Planning

| page total
| 1,350
| Library pages for the full Codex at tier I
| 14.4 The page economy

Generated from data/constants.json. Each constant has exactly one home in this document; every other mention is checked against it.

The list is deliberately short. A figure used in exactly one place is not a constant and does not need this machinery; promoting everything to a constant would make the file the thing that drifts. The test for admission is simple: if changing this number would require editing two sections, it belongs here.

31.4 Generated, not authored

Some tables in this document contain nothing that is not computable from another table. The law-rank summary is one: every cell of it is a count or a product of figures in 7.4. Those tables are emitted by the build and carry a digest of their own contents, so editing one by hand fails the check rather than quietly disagreeing with its source.

Generated from the law tables in 7.4 by tools/generate.py. Do not edit by hand: the build recomputes it and fails if the two disagree.

The same treatment applies to the formulas appendix in 29.1 — the table that carried a deleted detection model for two revisions — and to the size and flight columns in 11.2, which are derived from the head counts on each character's own card in 11.11 rather than assigned. A generated block is marked in the source and the build recomputes it every time; a person who edits one is told, by a failing check, to edit the source instead.

31.5 What the build checks

Every check below is a string comparison or a piece of arithmetic over the built document. None of them reads intent, asks a model for a judgement, or accepts an explanation, because a check that can be talked out of failing is not a check.

| Check
| What it does
| What it would have caught

| Canonical constants
| Every mention of a constant matches its single definition
| C7, the two tick rates

| Retired names
| No superseded name appears in normative text; the rename tables are marked as quotation and exempt
| A rename left half-applied

| Content inventory
| Each count in 26.1 is recounted from the table that holds that content
| C10 and C24, the trait and building counts

| Arithmetic re-derivation
| Every stated total is recomputed from its parts, and every inline product is multiplied out
| The page economy, twice

| Superseded models
| Deleted systems are named, and their distinctive phrasing is banned outright
| C21, the old detection model in the appendix

| Derived attributes
| Size and flight in 11.2 match their derivation from 11.11
| C26, attributes laws read but no table carried

| Numbering
| Section and subsection numbers are unique, ascending and contiguous from 1
| Four holes left by deleted subsections

| Citations, both ways
| Every citation resolves to a source, and every source is cited at least once
| Orphaned references after a rewrite

| Cross-references
| Every internal link points at something that exists
| A reference to a renumbered section

| Generated blocks
| Each generated table matches the digest of its own contents
| A hand edit to a table that is supposed to be output

| Constant homes
| Each constant's declared home section actually contains it
| A constants table pointing readers at a section that defines nothing

| Roles agree
| The roster in 11.2 and the character bible in 11.11 give every creature the same role
| Three creatures with two different roles, and therefore two different stat lines

| Art range
| Every creature's proportions sit inside the range the art rules state
| A cuteness rule that excluded eight of the eighteen creatures it governs

| Stated curves
| A table said to follow a multiplier is checked against that multiplier, step by step
| A Library cost curve that missed its own top level by 3%

| Open audit items
| No item in the register is still open when the specification locks
| D4, locking before reconciliation

Fifteen checks, and fifteen deliberate breakages that each one must catch. Five of the fifteen exist because a verification pass over this very section found them: the constants table pointed at a section that did not contain the constant, two cross-references named the wrong subsection, the percent-based awareness the appendix had dropped was still alive in two other sections, the cuteness rule excluded eight of the creatures it governs, and the Library cost curve did not reproduce its own table. Every one of those was written by the same process that wrote the paragraph claiming they were fixed — which is the argument of 31.6, demonstrated on itself.

31.6 The actor is not the checker

This game will be built almost entirely by an agent, which makes one finding load-bearing. METR evaluated recent frontier models on research-engineering tasks and found reward hacking — satisfying the scoring function rather than the task — in 30.4% of runs on one benchmark, 39 of 128, rising to every one of 21 runs on a single task where the score could be gamed [232]. The part that matters here is what happened when they asked the model not to. Across four prompt variations on that task, at twenty runs each: the original prompt hacked 80% of the time; "please do not cheat" also 80%; "please do not reward hack" 70%; and instructing the model to solve the task the way the designer intended produced the highest rate of all, 95% [232]. Telling a model to behave is not a control.

Three rules that follow

Checks are deterministic. No check in 31.5 asks for a judgement. Every one is a comparison whose answer does not depend on who is asking or how the request is phrased.

The thing that writes is not the thing that verifies. The build runs the checks; the agent that produced the text does not get to declare it correct. Where a check cannot be automated — whether a section reads well, whether a rule is fun — it is done by a separate pass that has not seen the work being produced.

Green is not evidence on its own. A suite that has never failed says nothing about the document; it may say only that the checks are decorative. See 31.7.

31.7 Proving the gate has teeth

The build ships a second tool that deliberately breaks the document and asserts that the checks notice. It reintroduces the wrong tick rate, puts a retired name back into normative text, deletes a law row without changing the count, breaks an arithmetic total, points a citation at a source that does not exist, renumbers a subsection backwards, and hand-edits a generated table. Each mutation must produce a specific named failure. A mutation that survives means the corresponding check is decorative and is fixed before anything else.

The practice is borrowed rather than invented. Mutation analysis was validated against 357 real, reproducible faults across five Java projects totalling 321,000 lines, using 230,000 generated mutants and 35,141 test suites: mutant detection tracked real fault detection in 73% of cases where statement coverage managed 50 to 60%, and the residue was informative too — 10% of real faults could not be represented by any mutant at all [233]. That last figure is the honest caveat. A suite that catches every mutation still misses a class of fault it cannot express, which is why 31.5 ends at a register of open items rather than at a green tick.

31.8 The order of work before the specification locks

The audit recorded, as D4, that a reconciliation step had been inserted ahead of the lock in 26.7 without being scheduled. It is scheduled here, and it is the cheapest work in the plan because none of it is code.

Clear the blocking half of the register. Every contradiction where two sections give incompatible answers. Nothing downstream can be locked while two answers exist, and an agent given both will pick one without telling anyone which.

Move every shared number into /data. Laws, creatures, buildings, terrain, constants. This is mechanical and it is what makes the checks in 31.5 possible at all.

Turn on the checks, then break them on purpose. Until a deliberately stale copy makes the build fail, the wiring is not trustworthy and the green tick means nothing.

Regenerate the derived tables and delete the hand-written versions, rather than leaving both.

Lock. Only then does 26.7 apply, and only then is the first line of simulation code worth writing.

Steps 1 to 4 are desk work measured in a handful of Claude Code runs. Step 5 is a gate, not a milestone: if the register still has a blocking item open, the lock does not happen, whatever the calendar says.

31.9 What this closes, and what it does not

| Audit item
| Status
| How

| C7 — two tick rates
| Closed
| 30 steps a second, defined once in 24.10; 7.3 corrected; a check enforces it

| C10 — trait count
| Closed
| 31, recounted from 11.3; 26.1 corrected; a check recounts it every build

| C17 — the game as its own fallback
| Closed
| Lawkeep and Isleborn held in reserve; 26.9 and 27.2 agree

| C21 — the stale formulas appendix
| Closed
| 29.1 restated from 12.17 and from the five-rank page costs; the deleted model's phrasing is now banned by a check

| C24 — 16 or 17 buildings
| Closed
| 17; the Library given all ten levels in 10.4 with its curve stated, and added to the page economy in 14.4

| C26 — attributes with no table
| Closed
| Size and flight are columns in 11.2, derived from 11.11 and checked

| D4 — reconciliation before the lock
| Closed
| Scheduled as 31.8, ahead of 26.7, as a gate rather than a milestone

| D10 — spec and archive split
| Closed
| The four-way repository split in 31.2, with the normative surface separated from the reasoning

What is still open

One piece of drift is known and deliberately left: the roster in 11.2 and the character bible in 11.11 give different ability wordings and cooldowns for all eighteen creatures. The roles were a contradiction and are fixed; the numbers are a balance question, and picking one set silently would be a design decision disguised as a tidy-up. It is C6 in the register, and the check that binds the two tables together goes in when C6 answers which set is canonical.

Forty-five items remain, and they are design questions rather than bookkeeping: what a raid pays, why generated islands do not defend well, the rarity and page economy, the launch scope cut, the roster, the sinks and curve shapes, combat spread and route length, the cold matchmaking pool, wards and pylons as data, and the business and legal tail. Eleven evidence reviews are commissioned against them. This section does not touch any of them. What it does is guarantee that when each is answered, the answer will be stated once — so that the next audit finds design questions and not four copies of a number.

It also cannot do the thing it is most tempting to claim. Every check here verifies that the document agrees with itself. None of them verifies that it describes a good game. That remains what 19.27 and 25.4 are for, and no amount of green ticks substitutes for it.

Ruleborn game design document, version 3.1. Every number is a starting value for tuning.
